**Summary.** This theoretical paper distinguishes between decaying unstable particles and unphysical 'ghost' states by examining their propagators. By comparing infinite-time limits with finite-time formulations, the authors argue that ghosts cannot exist as freely propagating asymptotic states. The work provides a detailed framework for understanding how time duration affects the observable signatures of particle decay versus quantum field pathologies.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on QFT fundamentals, the methodology of analyzing time evolution in finite windows to resolve asymptotic ambiguities is highly relevant for open quantum systems and non-equilibrium dynamics where boundary conditions (like finite measurement times) are crucial.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper elucidates the physical distinction between 'ghost' particles (negative norm states) and standard unstable particles within Quantum Field Theory (QFT). It specifically contrasts their behavior when analyzed in infinite versus finite time.

**Main result.** The study concludes that freely propagating ghost particles do not exist in the asymptotic limit, as their one-particle state is masked by multi-particle interference. Finite-time analysis reveals distinct temporal regimes governing particle dynamics.

**Method.** The authors analyze the dressed propagator by resumming radiative corrections and comparing analytic structures (pole locations on Riemann sheets) across infinite and finite time intervals ($	au$).

**Model / system.** The model involves scalar fields $\chi$ and $\phi$ interacting via $L_{	ext{int}} = g \phi \chi^2$. The analysis is generalized to include models relevant for quantum gravity, such as quadratic gravity.

**Key observables.** Dressed propagator ($ar{G}$), spectral density ($ho$), resonance peak width and height, and the asymptotic pole structure in the complex momentum plane.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time interval $	au$, particle mass $m$, coupling constant $g$, and the distinction between ordinary ($a=1$) and ghost ($a=-1$) field parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies heavily on approximations, including the narrow-width approximation ($\Gamma/m \ll 1$) and assuming large time intervals ($	au \gg 1/\omega_{\vec{p}}$) for certain derivations.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the modulus squared of the dressed propagator for ordinary vs. ghost cases against the standard Breit-Wigner form, showing how finite-time effects amplify differences in resonance behavior.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the infinite-time asymptotic picture (comparing pole structures) and then developing a detailed analysis using finite-time propagators ($ar{G}_	au$), identifying distinct temporal regimes that govern whether free-particle, resonant, or multi-particle decay dominates.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We elucidate the physical nature of ghosts above the multi-particle threshold by contrasting them with unstable particles in quantum field theory. We first consider the asymptotic formulation, where ordinary positive-norm one-particle states can be unstable and decay, whereas ghosts survive asymptotically without decaying, yet admit no particle interpretation due to interference with the multi-particle component which masks the negative-norm one-particle state. This distinction originates from two different analytic structures of the dressed propagator, whose complex conjugate poles lie in the first or second Riemann sheet in the ghost or ordinary case, respectively. Ghost resonances are, in principle, phenomenologically distinguishable from ordinary ones, being narrower and exhibiting weaker interference between positive- and negative-energy peaks. We then formulate the quantum field theory in a finite interval of time and, working within a suitable approximation for the dressed propagator, find that finite-time effects amplify differences in the resonant behavior and give rise to new features, such as higher peaks in ghost resonances. Distinct temporal regimes are also identified: for times shorter than the inverse width, an approximate free-particle description is valid, whereas at later times interactions and interference effects dominate, leading to decay or multi-particle masking. Complex poles in the dressed propagator emerge only at late times and become complex-conjugate pairs asymptotically, determining the asymptotic dynamics. This study supports the absence of freely propagating ghost particles in the asymptotic limit.

</details>

**Summary.** The research characterizes the implementation of nested Walsh parity-check filters on an eight-mode photonic processor to encode and protect single-photon states. By measuring leakage suppression and syndrome routing fidelity across hundreds of thousands of shots, the authors validate the feasibility of using such hardware for quantum error correction primitives. This work advances the understanding of physical limitations in scaling fault-tolerant quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial, highly detailed experimental benchmark for implementing abstract quantum error-correcting principles using near-term linear optical hardware, directly addressing resource limitations in physical realization.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper characterizes the faithful implementation of nested Walsh parity-check filters on a photonic processor to encode and protect quantum states in a single-photon eight-mode register.

**Main result.** They successfully demonstrated high fidelity for subspace projection, achieving significant suppression factors ($\approx 21	imes$) for residual leakage compared to ideal baselines, while confirming the structure of syndrome routing channels.

**Method.** The methodology involves performing single-photon measurements on a cloud photonic processor, testing the physical realization of abstract quantum error-correcting codes (like the extended Hamming code) via path/mode encodings.

**Model / system.** The system uses a single-photon eight-mode spatial register indexed by the vertices of a 3-cube ($Q_3$) on Quandela's Belenos processor. The encoding realizes subspaces corresponding to classical codes, such as the zero-sum neutral subspace and the $[8,4,4]$ extended Hamming code.

**Key observables.** Residual DC-port leakage (mean $0.6\%$), selectivity of face-parity syndrome channels ($94$-$99\%$), and Hong-Ou-Mandel visibility degradation/recovery.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of detections ($>340,000$); suppression factors ($\approx 21	imes$); fixed-pattern separator bias ($\pm 0.02$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The measurements are conditional on postselected single-photon detections and do not demonstrate full error correction or protection against photon loss.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the $Q_3$ vertex layout, the Gray-code Hamiltonian cycle ordering modes for the core, and show leakage/dump probabilities as a function of applied unitary depth or error injection strength.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the theoretical encoding (nested filters realizing specific codes), then detailing the experimental setup on the photonic chip, followed by quantitative measurements of leakage suppression, syndrome routing fidelity, and diagnostic tests like HOM visibility degradation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We characterize two nested Walsh parity-check filters implemented on Quandela's Belenos cloud photonic processor in a single-photon eight-mode spatial register. The modes are indexed by the vertices of the cube $Q_3$. The filters realize the classical $[8,7,2]$ single-parity-check code, the zero-sum neutral subspace $\mathcal{N}$ and the $[8,4,4]$ extended Hamming code, the parity-checked subspace $\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{N}$ with one DC and three face-parity syndrome channels. These are first-quantized path/mode encodings of classical codes: the experiment verifies leakage suppression and syndrome routing, not error correction or protection against photon loss, and all probabilities are conditional on postselected single-photon detections. Across more than 340,000 detections, neutral inputs show residual DC-port leakage of $0.02\%$-$1.1\%$ (mean $0.6\%$), corresponding to $\approx21\times$ suppression relative to the ideal $0.125$ DC-capture baseline and $31.6\times$ relative to the measured non-neutral control. Injected DC contamination gives a monotonic soft error signal, and the three face-parity syndrome channels route to their predicted ports with $94$-$99\%$ selectivity. A sector-preserving unitary core keeps leakage far below non-neutral controls over one to three applications, with differences dominated by calibration and compilation systematics rather than gate-cycle physics. We quantify these limits, including fixed-pattern separator bias, $\pm 0.02$ calibration offsets, and compilation scatter near the $10^{-3}$ leakage level, and report a Hong-Ou-Mandel degradation episode in which suppression vanished and recovered after recalibration.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces a block-encoding compiler that treats Matrix Product Operators (MPOs) as natural intermediate representations for quantum circuits. It significantly accelerates Hamiltonian simulation by compiling the time evolution operator directly within the MPO structure, avoiding exponential resource scaling associated with explicit Pauli expansions.

**Why it may be interesting.** For AMO physicists studying open quantum systems or dynamics, the ability to efficiently simulate complex Hamiltonians like Heisenberg models using tensor network formalisms is crucial for accessing regimes where standard methods fail due to exponential overheads.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of efficiently compiling quantum circuits for simulating time evolution generated by Hamiltonians expressed as Matrix Product Operators (MPOs). It aims to improve upon standard methods that suffer from exponential resource scaling.

**Main result.** A novel MPO block-encoding compiler achieves polynomial speedups by treating MPOs as structured intermediate representations, thereby avoiding the prohibitive $\mathcal{O}(NK)$ Pauli-string growth seen in explicit expansions.

**Method.** The core method involves developing a block-encoding compiler that maps MPOs to conditional PREP and SELECT stages within a Linear Circuit Unit (LCU) framework. This allows for compiling polynomial transformations directly at the MPO level.

**Model / system.** The focus is on real-time quantum Hamiltonian simulation, exemplified by the Heisenberg model and its perturbed variants. The underlying mathematical structure is the representation of time evolution unitaries ($e^{-itH}$) using tensor networks (MPOs).

**Key observables.** Computational cost scaling, MPO bond dimension ($\chi$), Pauli-string growth rate, and the normalization overhead factor ($\alpha$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bond dimension ($\chi$), truncation error, system size ($N$), and Taylor expansion order ($K$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that classical pre-processing using tensor network data structures can accelerate quantum algorithms. It compares different compilation routes (Pauli vs. MPO-transition).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the scaling of MPO bond dimension ($\chi$) independent of system size for Hamiltonian powers, and compare relative Frobenius errors when synthesizing unitaries via MPO Taylor expansions.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a block-encoding compiler by first mapping MPOs to LCU structures. It then compares this MPO-transition route against explicit Pauli expansion routes, demonstrating polynomial speedups in resource scaling for simulating time evolution.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a block-encoding compiler that speeds up linear combination of unitaries Hamiltonian simulation programs by treating matrix product operators as compressed, virtual-path LCU programs. In showing how these new conditional PREP and SELECT stages are compiled in terms of a parent matrix product operator, we go beyond typical operator splitting product formulas and illustrate how tensor networks are a natural language and valid intermediate representation for quantum circuits. Our results are numerically verified for two important cases, namely, Heisenberg and perturbed Heisenberg-adjacent chain real-time evolution, and highlight polynomial speedups. Specifically, we highlight a polynomial speedup that avoids the $\mathcal{O}(N^K)$ Pauli-string growth when the compressed MPO bond dimension and path normalization remain mild. We quantify how MPO truncation error and bond-dimension budgets affect the compiled polynomial representation. Our algorithms show how classical pre-processing in terms of tensor network data structures opens new avenues to accelerate quantum algorithms.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes that quantum mechanics can be understood by viewing classical reality as specific, localized submanifolds embedded within the full projective quantum state space. Measurement and environmental interaction are modeled unitarily via random-matrix dynamics (GUE), which forces stochastic diffusion leading to Born probabilities and stabilizing macroscopic systems into predictable Newtonian trajectories.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly abstract yet potentially unifying geometric mechanism for solving foundational problems in QM, linking stochastic processes (Brownian motion/GUE) directly to the emergence of classical determinism within a strictly unitary framework.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a unified, geometrically consistent framework that resolves foundational quantum mechanics problems like measurement, the origin of the Born rule, and the quantum-to-classical transition.

**Main result.** The framework successfully embeds classical dynamics (Newtonian motion) as distinguished submanifolds within the projective quantum state space, while environmental interactions are modeled by random-matrix dynamics yielding isotropic diffusion.

**Method.** It employs a geometric approach, identifying classical spaces with low-resolution equivalence classes on the projective state manifold. Measurement and environment coupling are modeled using Random Matrix Theory (GUE) to enforce unitary stochastic evolution.

**Model / system.** The system is quantum mechanics evolving in the projective Hilbert space ($	ext{CPL}^2$). Classical dynamics emerge when the state is restricted to localized submanifolds ($M_\sigma^3$) whose geometry is Euclidean, yielding Newtonian equations via the tangent component of the Schrödinger flow.

**Key observables.** Born-rule transition probabilities (microscopic); Stroboscopic Newtonian trajectories (macroscopic); Equivalence classes defining measurement outcomes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Localization parameter $\sigma$ (related to measurement resolution); Planck's constant ($\hbar$); Random Matrix Ensemble (GUE).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The central assumption is the Random-Matrix Conjecture, positing that environmental interactions are unitarily equivalent to random matrices drawn from the GUE. It also assumes classicality arises from finite-resolution coarse-graining.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in notes; figures likely illustrate the geometric embedding of classical manifolds within the projective state space and the stochastic nature of measurement paths.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first establishing the geometric embedding of classical phase/configuration spaces as submanifolds. It then introduces the Random-Matrix Conjecture to model environmental coupling, showing this leads to isotropic diffusion (Born rule). Finally, it synthesizes these elements to provide a unitary account of measurement and the quantum-to-classical transition.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a state-space geometric framework for measurement, classicality, and quantum paradoxes, based on one dynamical conjecture. Classical configuration space and classical phase space for a mechanical system arise as distinguished submanifolds of projective quantum state space. On these submanifolds, the Fubini--Study geometry induces Euclidean classical geometry, and the tangent component of Schrödinger evolution reproduces Newtonian dynamics. Within this framework, interactions with measuring devices and environments are described by random-matrix dynamics on projective state space, generated by matrices drawn from the Gaussian Unitary Ensemble. We show that this random-matrix dynamics yields isotropic diffusion, giving Born-rule transition probabilities in microscopic measurements and stabilizing classical behavior in macroscopic systems. We further argue that the random-matrix conjecture is not an independent ad hoc assumption: under natural translation-invariance assumptions on the distribution of state-space steps originating on the classical submanifold, the unitary lift of homogeneous and isotropic Brownian motion on that submanifold is uniquely given by the Gaussian Unitary Ensemble, up to scale and an irrelevant scalar part. The resulting framework provides a unitary account of measurement and the quantum-to-classical transition and, if accepted, offers a dynamical resolution of standard quantum paradoxes.

</details>

**Summary.** The researchers successfully achieved coherent microwave manipulation of donor electron spins in ZnO, confirming its potential as a qubit platform. They characterized Rabi oscillations and measured coherence times using advanced pulse sequences. The key finding is that while microwave control works, the coherence time is limited by spectral diffusion from the local electronic spin bath, presenting an open challenge for future quantum device development.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete experimental realization of solid-state spin qubits in a wide-bandgap semiconductor ($	ext{ZnO}$), directly addressing the feasibility of microwave control necessary for quantum gate operations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal was to demonstrate coherent microwave control of donor electron spins in ZnO, overcoming limitations inherent in purely optical manipulation techniques.

**Main result.** Coherent Rabi oscillations were observed with a $\pi$-pulse time of $\sim 14$ ns, establishing microwave control. However, the measured spin coherence ($T_2$) was found to be significantly shorter than previously reported values.

**Method.** The experiment utilized pulsed optically-detected magnetic resonance (PODMR) combined with microwave excitation within a microstrip resonator geometry embedded in ZnO.

**Model / system.** The system consists of optically addressable shallow donors ($	ext{In}$ ions) implanted in a $	ext{ZnO}$ host lattice. The physics involves the coupling between the electron spin, the ${}^{115}	ext{In}$ nuclear spin ($I=9/2$), and external microwave fields.

**Key observables.** Rabi frequency ($\Omega/2\pi \approx 36$ MHz), $\pi$-pulse time ($\sim 14$ ns), inhomogeneous dephasing time ($T_*^2 = 17$ ns), and Hahn echo coherence time ($T_2 \approx 200$ ns).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperature (1.7 K), high magnetic field regimes for characterization, and the hyperfine coupling constant associated with ${}^{115}	ext{In}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The short coherence time is attributed to spectral diffusion from the local electronic spin environment, ruling out microwave heating or instantaneous diffusion as dominant causes.

**Figures summary.** Figures detail PL/PLE confirming donor incorporation and linewidths; schematics show the resonator geometry; Rabi oscillations confirm coherent control; Ramsey/Hahn echo sequences quantify coherence decay times.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical platform (ZnO donors), demonstrates initialization and hyperfine-resolved microwave control via PODMR, characterizes fundamental dynamics using Rabi oscillations, and finally measures coherence time ($T_2$) using advanced pulse sequences to diagnose decoherence mechanisms.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Optically addressable shallow donors in ZnO combine efficient spin-selective optical transitions with the potential for long spin coherence in an isotopically purifiable host lattice, making them an attractive platform for spin-photon quantum technologies. A key missing capability, however, has been coherent control beyond the small-angle rotations accessible with ultrafast optical pulses. Here we demonstrate coherent microwave control of implanted $^{115}\mathrm{In}$ donors in ZnO. Resonant optical pumping initializes and reads out the donor electron spin. Pulsed optically-detected magnetic resonance resolves the ten hyperfine transitions associated with the coupled $^{115}\mathrm{In}$ nuclear spin (I = 9/2) and reveals optical-pumping-induced nuclear spin polarization. We observe coherent Rabi oscillations with a maximum Rabi frequency of $Ω/2π= 36.2 \pm 0.7$\;MHz, corresponding to a $π$-pulse time of 13.8$\pm$0.3\;ns, and characterize the spin coherence using Ramsey, Hahn echo and dynamical-decoupling measurements. Unexpectedly, the measured coherence is substantially shorter than reported in previous optical studies of donor spins in ZnO at high magnetic field. Control experiments rule out several simple explanations including microwave heating and instantaneous diffusion from the driven donor ensemble, leaving an open question regarding the origin of decoherence at low magnetic field in microwave-controlled ZnO donors. These results establish microwave control of ZnO donor qubits with resonant optical access to specific donor species. More broadly, they demonstrate that coherent microwave control can be achieved in optically addressable spin systems with nanosecond-scale inhomogeneous dephasing, enabling field-, temperature-, and materials-dependent studies of coherence-limiting mechanisms and the development of optically interfaced electron-nuclear spin registers.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper reports on the fabrication and characterization of a novel hybrid photonic chip integrating SnV color centers in diamond nanobeams with Al2O3 waveguides. By achieving clear, room-temperature guided emission, the authors demonstrate a scalable, lithography-based method for coupling solid-state quantum emitters into integrated optical circuits.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the hardware challenge of coupling solid-state qubits to photonics, which is central to building scalable quantum networks and quantum computing architectures. The demonstrated scalability via lithography is highly relevant for AMO/Quantum Optics research.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is developing a scalable fabrication method to integrate solid-state quantum emitters, specifically Tin-Vacancy (SnV) centers in diamond, with integrated photonic waveguides for quantum chip applications.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated efficient optical coupling by observing clear guided SnV emission at room temperature using a wafer-compatible bilayer fabrication approach. This confirms the feasibility of integrating these solid-state qubits into scalable photonic circuits.

**Method.** The work involves complex, multi-step nanofabrication combining wafer bonding, precise thinning (using RIE), and lithographically defined etching to create a diamond nanobeam coupled to an Al2O3 waveguide.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a vertically integrated 'diamond-on-Al2O3' dual-taper waveguide structure. The quantum element is the SnV color center acting as a solid-state qubit, whose emission is coupled into the engineered photonic waveguide.

**Key observables.** Clear guided SnV- emission peak at approximately 620 nm (Zero Phonon Line), and an overall device yield of 82.9% across tested devices.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The nanobeam thickness ($\sim 200 	ext{ nm}$), the waveguide material ($	ext{Al}_2	ext{O}_3$), and the operating temperature (room temperature) are critical parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The process assumes that the fabrication steps, particularly etching and bonding, maintain the structural integrity required for efficient optical coupling between the diamond nanobeam and the waveguide.

**Figures summary.** Figures show schematic fabrication flows, SEM images confirming the physical structure of the integrated bilayer device, and PL spectra comparing emission from coupled vs. reference waveguides to validate coupling efficiency.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a progression from conceptual design (schematics) through detailed structural characterization (SEM), culminating in optical measurements (PL spectroscopy) that confirm the functional integration of the quantum emitter into the photonic platform.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Tin vacancy (SnV) centers in diamond are promising solid state qubits for integrated quantum photonics. Here, we fabricate and characterize a diamond on Al2O3 dual taper waveguide structure containing SnV centers, demonstrating optical coupling between the diamond nanobeam and the underlying Al2O3 waveguide. The devices are realized using a bilayer fabrication approach compatible with wafer scale lithography. Clear guided SnV- emission is observed in all optically active devices, indicating effective optical coupling in the integrated structure. These results demonstrate a scalable fabrication approach toward integrating diamond color centers with photonic waveguides.

</details>

**Summary.** The research demonstrates the experimental observation of anyonic thermodynamics in a 1D strongly interacting quantum gas. By measuring thermodynamic signatures like the equation of state and pressure, the authors confirm that the system obeys Generalized Exclusion Statistics (GES). This work establishes a versatile platform for engineering and studying fractional statistics in quantum matter.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a highly controlled platform for observing fundamental quantum statistics beyond standard bosons and fermions, which is crucial for understanding exotic matter states in ultracold atomic systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to experimentally realize and detect anyonic thermodynamics governed by generalized exclusion statistics (GES) in a quantum gas system.

**Main result.** The authors successfully measured the equation of state, showing a continuous crossover from fermionic ($\alpha 	o 1$) to bosonic ($\alpha 	o 0$) statistics, providing direct evidence for the generalized Pauli principle.

**Method.** Measurements involve analyzing the equation of state and other thermodynamic quantities (like pressure and Tan's contact) by tuning temperature and interaction strength in a 1D quantum gas.

**Model / system.** The system is a one-dimensional strongly interacting quantum gas, modeled using the Lieb-Liniger Hamiltonian for ${}^6	ext{Li}_2$ molecules confined in optical lattices. The theory relies on mapping dynamics to statistical exclusion via GES.

**Key observables.** Equation of state ($\epsilon_{	ext{total}}$ vs $N_{1	ext{D}}$), pressure ($P$), Tan's contact ($C$), and the effective statistical parameter $\alpha$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Statistical parameter $\alpha$ (interpolating between 0 and 1), temperature ($T$), interaction strength ($\gamma$), and particle number ($N_{1	ext{D}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often relies on the local-density approximation (LDA) when treating trapped systems, and a bijective mapping is used to relate dynamical interactions to statistical exclusion in 1D.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the crossover of $\epsilon_{	ext{total}}$ vs $N_{1	ext{D}}$ across different $\alpha$ values; other figures show pressure ($P$) and Tan's contact ($C$) measurements confirming the FD-BE crossover trend.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework connecting anyons to GES, details the experimental setup in 1D quantum gases, presents measurements of thermodynamic quantities (equation of state, pressure), and concludes by quantifying the continuous statistical crossover via $\alpha$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Anyons are quasiparticles with quantum statistics interpolating between those of bosons and fermions. Two distinct manifestations of anyonic behaviour have been theoretically established: fractional exchange statistics where particle exchange can produce any phase, and generalized exclusion statistics which extends the Pauli exclusion principle. While anyons exhibiting fractional exchange statistics have been observed in diverse platforms, experimental realizations of generalized exclusion statistics and direct measurements of its thermodynamic signatures have remained elusive. Here, we realize an anyonic thermodynamic ensemble obeying generalized exclusion statistics and detect its anyonic thermodynamics in a one-dimensional strongly interacting quantum gas. To achieve this, we exploit the bijective mapping between dynamical and statistical interactions in one dimension. By tuning interaction strength and temperature over a wide range, we measure the equation of state and identify clear departures from Bose-Einstein and Fermi-Dirac statistics. These deviations are quantitatively captured by generalized exclusion statistics, providing direct evidence for the generalized Pauli principle. Independent probes of other thermodynamic quantities including pressure and the Tan contact further validate this framework. Our results establish a versatile platform for engineering anyonic exclusion statistics and open the door to thermodynamic applications of anyons in quantum technologies.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the entanglement response of interacting two-qubit systems to temperature changes by deriving exact expressions for thermal concurrence and TQFI. It establishes that both how fast entanglement changes with temperature and how much it degrades due to temperature uncertainty are fundamentally limited by the system's TQFI. This provides a new, fluctuation-based constraint on quantum information in thermal equilibrium.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous link between quantum information measures (entanglement) and fundamental thermodynamic/metrological quantities (QFI) in an equilibrium setting, which is crucial for understanding sensing near critical points or thermalization processes.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how entanglement, quantified by thermal concurrence, changes when subjected to variations in temperature within interacting two-qubit systems.

**Main result.** The rate of change and the loss due to temperature uncertainty in thermal entanglement are fundamentally bounded by the Thermal Quantum Fisher Information (TQFI).

**Method.** The authors derived exact analytical expressions for various thermal observables, including concurrence and TQFI derivatives, using the properties of the Gibbs state.

**Model / system.** The system is an interacting two-qubit model analyzed in thermal equilibrium, described by a general interaction Hamiltonian $H$ leading to a Gibbs state $ho(eta)$.

**Key observables.** Thermal Concurrence ($C_{	ext{th}}(eta)$), Thermal Quantum Fisher Information ($	ext{F}_{th}^Q(eta)$), and their first/second derivatives with respect to inverse temperature ($eta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inverse temperature ($eta$), the entanglement threshold defined by $p_{11}(eta) = 1/2$, and temperature uncertainty ($\sigma_eta^2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to two interacting qubits, assuming the Hamiltonian can be analyzed in a canonical anisotropic form.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the framework by defining thermal entanglement and TQFI for the system. It then derives key bounds showing that both the rate of change and the robustness against temperature uncertainty are constrained by $	ext{F}_{th}^Q(eta)$. Finally, it discusses the implications of these bounds compared to coherent dynamics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the response of entanglement to temperature variations in interacting two-qubit thermal states. For a general two-qubit interaction Hamiltonian, we derive exact expressions for the thermal concurrence, its first and second derivatives with respect to inverse temperature, and the thermal quantum Fisher information. We show that the rate of change of thermal entanglement is bounded by the thermal quantum Fisher information. We further derive a bound relating entanglement curvature and thermal quantum Fisher information, and show that temperature uncertainty induces a loss of entanglement bounded by the same quantity that determines thermometric sensitivity. These results establish thermal quantum Fisher information as a fundamental constraint on the response and robustness of entanglement in interacting two-qubit thermal states.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper thoroughly characterizes thin-film Barium Titanate (BTO) resonators, demonstrating its strong piezoelectric response from room temperature down to cryogenic temperatures. By measuring SAW resonances and analyzing ferroelectric switching dynamics, the authors establish that BTO remains a viable, highly coupled platform for both classical RF electronics and emerging superconducting quantum circuits.

**Why it may be interesting.** The demonstration that a classical ferroelectric material like BTO maintains significant coupling strength ($d_{33,	ext{eff}}$) down to the millikelvin regime is highly relevant for integrating solid-state transducers into superconducting quantum circuits and quantum memories.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study characterizes the piezoelectric properties of thin-film Barium Titanate (BTO) across a wide temperature range, specifically investigating its potential for coupling in superconducting quantum circuits.

**Main result.** Thin-film BTO exhibits strong piezoelectric coupling ($d_{33,	ext{eff}} \approx 19 	ext{ pC/N}$ at millikelvin), which persists down to cryogenic temperatures, making it a promising platform for both classical RF and quantum information technologies.

**Method.** The research combines microwave characterization of Surface Acoustic Wave (SAW) resonators with finite-element modeling and scaling relations to extract effective piezoelectric coefficients across temperature regimes.

**Model / system.** The physical system is an Interdigital Transducer (IDT) fabricated on thin-film BTO deposited on a Si substrate. The analysis models the material's ferroelectric, piezoelectric, and elastic responses under varying DC bias and cryogenic temperatures.

**Key observables.** Electromechanical coupling coefficient ($k_{2	ext{eff}}$), effective piezoelectric coefficients ($d_{31}, d_{33}, d_{15}$), resonance frequency shifts, and the temperature dependence of these parameters.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature range (Room Temperature to millikelvin); high electromechanical coupling ($\sim 14\%$) and fast switching dynamics ($\sim 100 	ext{ ns}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on extracting coefficients via scaling relations at cryogenic temperatures due to measurement limitations, and assumes the persistence of piezoelectric coupling mechanisms.

**Figures summary.** Figures show SAW resonance spectra ($S_{11}$) at various temperatures, hysteresis loops ($\gamma_e$) demonstrating ferroelectric switching, and plots detailing the temperature dependence of coupling rates and coefficients.

**Paper structure.** The work progresses from characterizing room-temperature performance (high $k_{2	ext{eff}}$) to studying voltage-controlled reconfigurability, culminating in detailed measurements and analysis of piezoelectric response at millikelvin temperatures for quantum applications.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Ferroelectric materials, with their strong nonlinearities, underpin key technologies across radio-frequency (RF) signal processing, optical communications, and emerging quantum systems. Barium titanate (BTO) is a notable example, combining strong piezoelectric and electro-optic responses. While bulk BTO has been studied for decades, the piezoelectric properties of its recently available thin films, and their behavior at the millikelvin temperatures relevant to quantum hardware, remain largely unexplored. Here, we fabricate and characterize surface acoustic wave (SAW) resonators on thin-film BTO. The measured devices exhibit high electromechanical coupling (k2eff 0.14 at 5.2 GHz) and operate up to 7.8 GHz. From these measurements, combined with finite-element modeling of the multi-domain microstructure, we extract an effective piezoelectric coefficient d33eff of 53 pC/N, comparable to bulk BTO. Exploiting the intrinsic ferroelectricity, we further demonstrate low-voltage switching with a fast (100 ns) response, attractive for reconfigurable RF front-ends and parametric amplifiers. Extending these measurements to millikelvin temperatures, we find that the piezoelectric response persists, with d33eff 19 pC/N, pointing to the potential of BTO for piezoelectric coupling in superconducting quantum circuits. These results position thin-film BTO as a promising piezoelectric platform for both classical and quantum information technologies.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper explores the generalized SYK model by varying the mean-to-standard deviation ratio of its random couplings. It demonstrates a continuous crossover from highly chaotic behavior (maximal scrambling) towards less chaotic regimes as disorder is tuned. This work highlights that different measures of quantum chaos can decouple, providing deeper insights into the relationship between entropy and maximal chaos in strongly correlated systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical framework for understanding how tuning disorder in strongly interacting fermionic systems modifies fundamental concepts like maximal chaos and scrambling, which are central to holographic theories and condensed matter physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how tuning the mean-to-standard-deviation ratio ($g$) of random couplings affects the chaotic properties of the Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev (SYK) model, exploring a crossover between chaotic and non-chaotic regimes.

**Main result.** Increasing $g$ causes a continuous chaotic-to-non-chaotic crossover in scrambling dynamics; crucially, different measures of chaos decouple, showing that finite residual entropy does not mandate maximal chaos.

**Method.** The analysis employs multiple quantum chaos diagnostics—including Lyapunov exponents (from OTOCs), spectral form factors (SFF), and nearest-neighbor level spacing statistics—across varying disorder parameters.

**Model / system.** The system is the generalized Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev (SYK) model, featuring fermions with all-to-all interactions whose complex couplings are drawn from a Gaussian distribution parameterized by $g = J_0/\deltaJ$.

**Key observables.** Lyapunov exponent ($\lambda_L$), Residual Entropy ($S_0/N$), Out-of-Time Correlators (OTOCs), Spectral Form Factor (SFF), and nearest-neighbor energy level spacing statistics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The tuning parameter $g = J_0/\deltaJ$, which controls the degree of randomness in the couplings, ranging from $g=0$ (standard SYK) to $g 	o \infty$ (clean limit).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Numerical simulations rely on Exact Diagonalization (ED) for finite system sizes ($N$), and the analysis assumes self-averaging properties hold in the thermodynamic limit.

**Figures summary.** Figures summarize the evolution of chaos diagnostics across varying $g$, showing $\lambda_L$ decreasing from $2\pi T$ to zero, while level repulsion persists and residual entropy remains finite.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes spectral density ($ho(E)$), entanglement entropy ($S_A$), and various time-domain chaos measures (OTOCs, SFF) as functions of the disorder parameter $g$, demonstrating the decoupling between these different indicators of quantum chaos.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The complex Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev (SYK) model, featuring fermions with all-to-all interactions, serves as a dual paradigm for understanding non-Fermi liquid behavior and the holographic nature of charged black holes. Two defining characteristics of the standard SYK model are its maximal chaos (Lyapunov exponent $λ_{\mathrm{L}}=2πT$ at temperature $T$), and its finite zero-temperature residual entropy. While previous studies have largely focused on couplings drawn from a zero-mean Gaussian distribution, we investigate a generalized model with a finite mean-to-standard-deviation ratio, $g\equiv J_{0}/δJ$ of the coupling distribution in order to get deeper insight into the evolution of chaos. We find that increasing $g$ yields the following effects: (i) The system remains a fast scrambler with $λ_{\mathrm{L}}=A~T$, but with a suppressed coefficient $A<2π$. (ii) In the limit $g\to \infty$, out-of-time-ordered correlators (OTOCs) no longer exhibit exponential growth with $λ_{\mathrm{L}}\simeq 0$. (iii) The spectral correlations indicative of late-time chaos maintain Wigner-Dyson level spacing statistics for all values of $g$. (iv) The system preserves a finite residual entropy, albeit with reduced magnitude, for all $g$ values. We conclude that in this generalized SYK model, there is a chaotic to non-chaotic crossover. Moreover different measures of chaos decouple, demonstrating that the presence of finite residual entropy does not strictly imply maximal chaos.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops multi-entropy entanglement measures to probe the universal properties of topological order by calculating the topological partition function $Z(M)$. It establishes that this extraction is possible only if the manifold $M$ possesses a property called 'local achirality.' This framework successfully distinguishes between different types of topological phases and relates these quantum constraints to fundamental geometric invariants like the Pontryagin number in higher dimensions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful theoretical link between quantum entanglement measures derived from ground states and deep geometric invariants of spacetime manifolds, which is crucial for classifying exotic topological phases beyond standard symmetry protection.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is determining if multi-entropy entanglement measures can extract all universal information about a topological order from the ground state wavefunction.

**Main result.** Topological partition functions $Z(M)$ can be extracted universally if the underlying manifold $M$ satisfies a condition called 'local achirality.'

**Method.** The authors use multi-entropy measures, which are multipartite entanglement generalizations, and relate their phase to the topological partition function $Z(M)$. They establish geometric constraints (like vanishing Pontryagin numbers) that characterize these locally-achiral manifolds.

**Model / system.** The study concerns topologically ordered quantum phases of matter described by gapped ground states on lattices approximating various manifolds ($S^3$, 4-manifolds). Specific models include Quantum Double Theories $D(G_{p,q}, u)$ and Time-Reversal Symmetry Protected Topological Orders (T-SPT).

**Key observables.** Multi-entropy measures $M(\psi)$, the topological partition function $Z(M)$, and geometric invariants like the Pontryagin number $\int_M p_1$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension of the manifold ($d$), specific parameters defining anyon theories $(p, q, u)$, and the local achirality condition.

**Assumptions / limitations.** It is assumed that multi-entropy measures are valid probes for universal information extraction, and in higher dimensions, local achirality implies vanishing Pontryagin numbers.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate manifold constructions (e.g., $S^3$ decomposition), entanglement structures using 'gems,' and the relationship between topological invariants and geometric properties like the Pontryagin number.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining multi-entropy probes, establishing the condition of local achirality required to extract $Z(M)$, demonstrating this capability for 2+1D theories beyond modular data, and finally extending the analysis to higher dimensions (4D) by relating local achirality to vanishing Pontryagin numbers.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We consider the problem of identifying a topological order based on bulk entanglement of the ground-state wavefunction. Previous work showed that some universal information can be extracted from multi-entropy measures, a class of multipartite entanglement measures obtained by applying permutation operators exchanging the degrees of freedom between different replicas of the wavefunction. It remains an open question to what extent such entanglement measures can be used to extract any universal information from the ground state. Here we show that the topological partition function $Z(M)$ of a manifold $M$ can be extracted provided that $M$ satisfies a topological condition which we term ``local achirality". We show that locally-achiral manifolds can be used to extract universal properties of 2+1d topological phases that go beyond the $S$ and $T$ matrices. As a first step towards classifying locally-achiral manifolds, we show that, in four dimensions, such manifolds have vanishing Pontryagin number. We relate this property to the existence of beyond-cohomology time-reversal symmetry protected topological order (T-SPT) in four dimensions. Finally, we present an entanglement measure that detects this nontrivial T-SPT.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a robust method to extract multiple eigenenergies of Ising-type models using noisy IBM quantum computers. It combines Trotterization with the Hadamard test and DFT analysis, achieving significant results by optimizing circuits toward constant depth. This advances the field's capability beyond simple ground-state finding towards full spectral characterization on NISQ devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it pushes the boundary of quantum simulation techniques toward extracting detailed spectral information (multiple eigenvalues) rather than just the ground state, which is a major goal for simulating complex Hamiltonians in condensed matter physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is extracting multiple ground- and excited-state energies of quantum many-body systems using noisy, near-term quantum hardware.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated the reliable extraction of several low-lying eigenenergies for both TFIM and TLFIM on IBM quantum computers, comparing results across different hardware generations.

**Method.** They employ the Hadamard test combined with Trotterized time-evolution operators, followed by a Discrete Fourier Transform (DFT) to map the time dynamics back to spectral energies.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on simulating the Transverse Field Ising Model (TFIM) and its variant, the Transverse Longitudinal Field Ising Model (TLFIM). These models are implemented on IBM quantum processors using Trotterization circuits optimized for constant depth where possible.

**Key observables.** Eigenenergies ($E_k$), spectral weights ($|c_k|^2$), and the time-dependent signal $g(t) = \langle\psi|e^{-i \hat{H}t}|\psiangle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis involves balancing Trotter error ($\Delta t$) against circuit depth, with specific parameters like $\Delta t/t_0 = 0.1$ (TFIM) and $\Delta t/t_0 = 0.241$ (TLFIM) being crucial.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that the signal can be accurately reconstructed via DFT despite noise, and it relies on optimizing circuits to achieve constant depth where possible.

**Figures summary.** Figures show Fourier spectra confirming resolved eigenenergies above a calculated noise threshold ($\eta_i$), and comparisons of measured population dynamics $|\langle 0^{\otimes N}|\psi(t)angle|^2$ against high-accuracy classical Runge-Kutta simulations for both TFIM and TLFIM.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the H-test/Trotterization framework, details optimizations like Constant-Depth Circuits (CDCs) for specific Hamiltonians (TFIM), applies this to both TFIM and TLFIM, and concludes by comparing results across different IBM hardware generations against classical benchmarks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We implement the Hadamard test with Trotterized time-evolution operators on IBM quantum computers to simultaneously extract ground- and excited-state energies of the transverse field Ising model (TFIM) and transverse longitudinal field Ising model (TLFIM). The Trotterization circuits for the TFIM admit constant-depth circuits (CDCs) for arbitrary time, allowing us to locate a large number of eigen-energies above the background noise for up to six spins. Via circuit synthesis we show that the three-spin TLFIM has constant-depth structure although it does not meet the known CDC criteria. The CDCs enable the extraction of the ground and first-excited state energies of the three-site TLFIM via its dynamics. We also address complications from the noisy background and discrete Fourier transform to enhance the reliability of the extraction process and compare the results from different generations of IBM hardware to highlight the improvement.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces a novel 'quantum-advantage resource' measure for multimode Gaussian light, quantifying its inherent quantum complexity using advanced convex optimization techniques. By analyzing pulsed OPAs, the authors demonstrate that this resource can be maximized and extracted, providing a critical theoretical tool for realizing scalable photonic quantum computing beyond simple entanglement measures.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous mathematical framework ($	ext{\#P}$-hard complexity) to quantify the non-classical resources in continuous variable systems, offering a superior metric over standard entanglement measures for assessing true computational advantage.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To introduce and provide a universal quantitative measure for the 'quantum-advantage resource' contained within mixed multimode Gaussian states, distinguishing it from classical correlations.

**Main result.** The proposed quantum complexity resource dimension ($N_{QA}$) is shown to be a more realistic metric than previous measures like Bloch–Messiah supermodes, and this resource can be engineered in pulsed OPAs for photonic quantum computing.

**Method.** The analysis uses convex optimization techniques applied to the quadrature covariance matrix, relating the resource measure to the $\text{\#P}$-hard complexity of joint photon number probability distributions via the Hafnian Master Theorem.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using multimode Gaussian states generated by pulsed Optical Parametric Amplifiers (OPAs). The state is characterized entirely by its quadrature covariance matrix, $V$.

**Key observables.** Quantum-advantage resource dimension ($N_{QA}$), the quantum part of the covariance matrix ($V_q$), and the mean photon number in the quantum part ($\langle n_S angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The squeezing parameters ($\tilde{r}_j$) derived from $V_q$, and the number of modes ($M$) which can reach thousands.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is primarily restricted to Gaussian states, although it aims to guide applications toward non-Gaussian regimes. It assumes that the resource measure depends only on the exponential factor of complexity.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the exact quantum advantage resource ($N_{QA}$) against the Wigner quasi-probability distribution bound ($N_W$), showing excellent agreement, and illustrate how mode pruning severely depletes this resource.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework by defining the resource via convex optimization (Oh decomposition), comparing it to older metrics (BM supermodes), deriving bounds using Wigner functions, analyzing resource depletion under loss/pruning, and finally applying these concepts to pulsed OPA sources for quantum computation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce the notion and reveal remarkable properties of quantum complexity resource contained in a mixed multimode Gaussian state and providing universal quantitative characterization of its quantum advantage. The notion is based on convex optimization, multimode photon number statistics, Hafnian Master Theorem, and #P-hard complexity. We consider pulsed OPAs targeting maximal quantum complexity resource and thousands of multipartite-entangled squeezed modes of output light via nonlinear, spatio-temporally nonadiabatic generation inside OPA and optimized extraction out of OPA. We show that such figure of merit is more realistic than Bloch--Messiah supermodes and guides to multimode OPAs opening new paths to important applications in quantum information science such as generation of 3D cluster states for one-way photonic quantum computing and demonstration of quantum advantage.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a method to precisely control the spectral correlations of entangled photon pairs generated via SPDC using structured light pumps. By exploiting the Gouy phase mismatch, the authors show that structuring the pump in both space and spectrum allows for 'field-programmable' generation of complex quantum states. This offers a powerful alternative to traditional crystal engineering for advanced quantum source development.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to quantum optics, specifically in the area of photon source engineering. The concept of 'field-programmable' sources that bypass complex crystal fabrication techniques by manipulating the pump beam is a major theoretical advance for generating high-dimensional entangled states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to achieve all-optical control over the spectral correlations of photon pairs generated via Spontaneous Parametric Down-Conversion (SPDC). This is accomplished by structuring the pump beam in both spatial and spectral domains.

**Main result.** By utilizing Gouy phase matching with spatiotemporally structured light, the authors demonstrate full control over biphoton spectral correlations, enabling the generation of complex quantum states inaccessible through traditional crystal engineering methods.

**Method.** The method involves modeling SPDC using mode expansions (e.g., Hermite-Gaussian basis) and deriving the joint spectral amplitude (JSA) dependence on the pump field's spatiotemporal structure via the Gouy phase mismatch.

**Model / system.** The physical system is SPDC in a nonlinear medium, modeled by an interaction Hamiltonian. The control mechanism relies on structuring the pump beam using its spatial and temporal wavepacket properties to dictate the effective phase-matching function.

**Key observables.** Mode-dependent joint spectral amplitude (mJSA), fidelity of the effective nonlinear susceptibility ($\chi_{	ext{eff}}$) matching target Hermite-Gaussian structures, and spectral purity ($P$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Gouy phase mismatch term $\Delta \phi_G(z)$ is crucial; control is achieved by structuring the pump field in space (spatial modes) and spectrum (temporal wavepackets).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Approximations include using the standard SPDC regime (low pair generation probability) and assuming linear dispersion.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate conceptual setups with structured pumps, optimization results showing high fidelity of $\chi_{	ext{eff}}$ matching HG targets, and examples of generated JSAs using Laguerre-Gaussian modes in a rotated spectral basis.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework by relating the mJSA to the pump field structure via Gouy phase matching. It then demonstrates this control capability by generating specific quantum states (separable vs. entangled) and compares it favorably against previous methods like those using Aperiodically Poled Crystals.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Here we investigate the all-optical control of spectral correlations in spontaneous parametric down-conversion. We show that when photon pairs are projected onto high-order spatial modes, the spatial structure of the pump field defines the phase-matching function of the nonlinear interaction. Thus, by structuring the pump field in both space and spectrum, the biphoton spectral correlations are fully controlled.Considering a standard periodically-poled crystal as the nonlinear medium, we show that the Gouy phase matching method proposed here can generate both spectrally uncorrelated and high-dimensional spectrally entangled photon pairs, similarly to what is achieved with aperiodically-poled crystals. Furthermore, we show that our method can generate a wider class of quantum states if the pump field is a spatiotemporal wavepacket, that is, if its spatial and spectral structures are correlated.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper theoretically investigates how chiral exceptional points in an optical microcavity can be exploited to achieve universal photon blockade. By analyzing the system's nonreciprocal dynamics, the authors demonstrate that one direction of light propagation exhibits strong anti-bunching (PB), while the other shows different statistics. This provides a pathway for designing highly directional quantum sources.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to AMO physics and quantum optics as it explores non-Hermitian effects (CEPs) to engineer fundamental quantum phenomena like single-photon emission with unprecedented directional control, moving beyond standard cavity QED setups.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates achieving universal photon blockade (PB) using two-photon light-matter interactions within a microcavity system exhibiting chiral exceptional points (CEPs). The goal is to build multiplexing quantum sources with nonreciprocal photon statistics.

**Main result.** The presence of CEPs causes a stark contrast in photon statistics between the two counter-propagating modes: one mode exhibits strong PB ($g^{(2)} 	o 0$), while the other shows Poissonian or super-Poissonian distribution, enabling directional control.

**Method.** Theoretical analysis using the extended cascade quantum master equation and deriving equations of motion for probability amplitudes. Photon statistics are diagnosed via equal-time second-order correlation functions, $g^{(2)}(0)$.

**Model / system.** The system is an optical microcavity coupled to a quantum emitter (QE), modeled by a two-photon two-mode JC Hamiltonian ($H_{JC}$). The dynamics are governed by the master equation incorporating cavity and atomic decay rates.

**Key observables.** Second-order correlation functions ($g^{(2)}_L(0)$ and $g^{(2)}_R(0)$), mean photon numbers ($n_L, n_R$), and probability amplitudes of Fock states.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling strength ($g/\kappa$), detuning ($\Delta_c/\kappa$), phase ($\phi$), and cavity/mirror reflectivity ($r$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the weak-driving regime approximation in some sections, and the system dynamics are modeled using a master equation approach.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate $g^{(2)}(0)$ dependence on parameters like coupling strength, phase, and detuning. Key findings show PB ($g^{(2)} 	o 0$) appearing selectively in one mode (e.g., the right cavity) under specific parameter settings ($\phi=0$ or $\Delta_c=0$).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops by first establishing the theoretical framework using a two-photon JC model and master equation dynamics. It then analyzes the system's steady-state behavior, focusing on how CEPs induce directional differences in photon statistics (PB vs. bunching) under varying driving conditions (resonant vs. detuned).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The photon blockade (PB) effect is a hallmark non-classical phenomenon in quantum optics and finds important applications for building quantum sources, while the control of PB by the non-Hermitian exceptional points remains largely unexplored. In this work, we theoretically investigate universal photon blockade in a microcavity harboring chiral exceptional points (CEPs) for building multiplexing quantum sources with nonreciprocal photon statistics. The results reveal that the presence of the CEPs leads to a stark contrast in the photon statistics of two whispering-gallery modes with opposite propagating directions. That is, one mode exhibits a strong PB effect while the other displays either sub-Poissonian or super-Poissonian distribution. Our findings thus may pave the way for advanced applications of photon blockade, and provide a theoretical foundation for the selective generation of single-photon and two-photon emission

</details>

<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> FIG. 1: Schematic classical-to-quantum symmetry selection along a parameterized Hamiltonian family H(θ). We assume H(θ0) saturates the classical bound while H(θ1) reaches the maximal quantum violation. As θ is tuned from θ0 to θ1, the ground-state energy density e(θ) decreases and contextuality appears. At θ0, the classical-saturating Hamiltonian has a ground space supporting multiple distinct commensurate periods. For θ &gt; θ0, this period degeneracy is lifted and the ground state becomes strictly pQ-periodic with pQ &gt; 1, signaling spontaneous breaking of one-site translation symmetry.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Summary.** This work establishes that contextuality serves as a quantitative diagnostic for detecting spontaneous translation-symmetry breaking in 1D quantum systems. By analyzing how the maximal violation of contextuality witnesses forces the ground state into a unique periodic structure, it connects fundamental concepts from quantum information theory to condensed matter physics principles like commensurate order.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful, measurable quantum correlation diagnostic—contextuality—that directly maps onto fundamental concepts in condensed matter physics like spontaneous symmetry breaking and emergent periodicity.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates contextuality as a diagnostic tool for detecting spontaneous breaking of translation symmetry in quantum systems described by translation-invariant Hamiltonians.

**Main result.** Maximal quantum violation of contextuality witnesses coincides with the spontaneous breaking of one-site translation symmetry, forcing the ground state into a unique, strictly periodic structure ($p>1$).

**Method.** The analysis uses advanced mathematical tools including Semidefinite Programming (SDP) hierarchies (NPA), Matrix Product States (MPS), and variational minimization to derive lower bounds for quantum correlations.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on infinite one-dimensional translation-invariant chains governed by local Hamiltonians, analyzed using contextuality witnesses derived from energy density measurements.

**Key observables.** Contextuality violation ($\langle E angle_{	ext{TI}} < L$), the emergent periodicity ($p_Q > 1$) of the ground state, and the associated energy density $e(	heta)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The interpolation parameter $	heta$ controls the Hamiltonian family between classical-bound and quantum-optimal regimes; system size reduction to finite rings is also key.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Approximations include using LTI states in SDP relaxations, and assuming that the contextuality witness can be mapped onto a local energy density observable.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the transition from multiple degenerate periods at the classical bound to a single quantum-selected period upon increasing contextuality violation. Finite-size scaling plots show convergence of energy densities.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops the connection between contextuality, symmetry breaking, and periodicity by first establishing bounds using SDPs (NPA hierarchy), then demonstrating this via MPS/variational methods, and finally showing the physical implication through period selection upon tuning Hamiltonian parameters.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Bell- and contextuality-type inequalities have become practical probes of many-body quantum correlations, often involving only few-body correlators and quantities with a direct Hamiltonian interpretation such as an energy density. Here we show that, in infinite one-dimensional translation-invariant chains, contextuality can acquire a genuinely thermodynamic meaning: within the witness families studied, the maximal quantum violation coincides with spontaneous breaking of one-site translation symmetry, producing strictly $p$-periodic ground states with $p>1$. Along natural continuous interpolations between classical-bound and quantum-optimal Hamiltonians, the classical bound marks a symmetry-breaking point where competing classical periodicities are lifted in favor of a unique quantum-selected period. At the quantum optimum, the studied families admit exact finite-size reductions: a translation-invariant contextuality witness induces a $p$-site periodic-boundary-condition inequality with identical classical and quantum bounds (hence no loss under reduction), and in several cases the resulting finite inequalities are tight. This reduction turns an infinite-chain contextuality certification into a compact, hardware-testable benchmark on a small ring, requiring only local energy measurements. We establish the mechanism analytically in representative two- and three-body witness models and corroborate it more broadly using a translation-invariant adaptation of semidefinite-program hierarchies together with variational matrix-product-state algorithms.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper analyzes how doubly pumped Kerr microresonators generate complex, non-Gaussian Schrödinger-cat-like states by treating pump depletion quantum mechanically. By comparing the full dynamics to simplified models, the authors demonstrate that genuine quantum back-action is crucial for maintaining the fragile interference patterns (Wigner negativity) associated with these highly nonclassical resources.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses generating nonclassical states—specifically cat-like structures—using cavity nonlinearities, which is a central theme in continuous-variable quantum information processing and quantum optics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates quantum pump depletion and the generation of non-Gaussian, cat-like states in Kerr microresonators.

**Main result.** Quantum-depleted intraresonance dynamics are shown to generate multicomponent Schrödinger-cat-like states characterized by Wigner negativity, which is suppressed when uncompensated nonlinear phase terms (SPM/XPM) dominate.

**Method.** The authors employ a full quantum treatment of the pump modes and compare it against an effective reduced model that isolates resonant photon conversion dynamics.

**Model / system.** The system is a doubly pumped Kerr microresonator operating in the intraresonance regime. The Hamiltonian includes terms for Self-Phase Modulation (SPM), Cross-Phase Modulation (XPM), and Four-Wave Mixing (FWM).

**Key observables.** Wigner function negativity, non-Poissonian photon number statistics, quadrature squeezing, and single-mode Schmidt numbers quantifying entanglement.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The $n=2$ intraresonance branch exhibits a residual $\mathbb{Z}_3$ symmetry; the dynamics are tracked up to a characteristic interaction length $\tau_{\text{max}}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary comparison is between the full quantum Kerr Hamiltonian and an effective reduced model that isolates coherent photon conversion, with the undepleted-pump limit serving as a baseline approximation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate Wigner functions for both reduced (quantum-depleted) and full models, showing negative regions indicating nonclassicality. Photon number distributions ($P(n)$) are shown for pump and generated modes at peak conversion ($\tau_{\text{max}}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the dynamics by first defining the full Kerr Hamiltonian, then deriving a reduced model focusing on resonant photon conversion, analyzing key diagnostics (Wigner function, squeezing), and finally comparing these results against the simpler undepleted-pump approximation to highlight the necessity of quantum back-action.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate quantum pump depletion and non-Gaussian state generation in doubly pumped Kerr microresonators operating in the intraresonance regime. The pump modes are treated quantum mechanically rather than as undepleted classical amplitudes, allowing pump depletion, back-action, entanglement generation, quadrature fluctuations, and Wigner-function negativity to emerge from the same multimode dynamics. Starting from the Kerr four-wave-mixing selection rule, we distinguish an effective resonant photon-conversion model from the full Kerr Hamiltonian containing self-phase modulation (SPM), cross-phase modulation (XPM), and four-wave mixing (FWM). The reduced model isolates the photon-conversion network responsible for the discrete $\mathbb{Z}_{n+1}$ phase structure, whereas the full model retains operator-valued nonlinear Kerr phases. For the \(n=2\) intraresonance branch, the four-mode reduced initial-value problem with fixed coherent pump phases has a residual \(\mathbb{Z}_3\) symmetry and generates cat-like Wigner structures near the interaction length at which the generated-mode population \(\langle n_1\rangle\) is maximal and the pump population \(\langle n_0\rangle\) is strongly depleted. The resulting states are not the canonical even or odd coherent states of Dodonov, Malkin, and Man'ko, but multicomponent Schrödinger-cat-like states characterized by Wigner negativity, non-Poissonian statistics, pump-mode quadrature squeezing, and large single-mode Schmidt numbers. Comparison of the reduced and full Kerr dynamics shows that uncompensated SPM/XPM-induced phase shearing suppresses the interference fringes and Wigner negativity responsible for the clearest cat-like signatures. These results identify quantum-depleted intraresonance Kerr dynamics as a route to symmetry-organized non-Gaussian states in Kerr resonators.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper models unstructured database search using a classical system of interacting spherical spins governed by dissipative dynamics. By analyzing the relaxation time to equilibrium at low temperatures, the authors show that the search time scales faster than Grover's quantum limit. This suggests that classical, dissipative analog computers can surpass unitary quantum computation for certain search problems.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete physical analogy for computation using classical dissipative dynamics, linking concepts from spin glasses (disordered systems) to computational complexity theory, which is relevant for understanding non-unitary information processing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper proposes a physical realization of unstructured database search using classical, dissipative dynamics on a spin system, aiming to achieve a search time faster than Grover's quantum algorithm.

**Main result.** The search time scales as O(M^a) with a factor 'a' less than 1/2, demonstrating that a classical dissipative analog computer can outperform the standard quantum unitary approach for this task.

**Method.** The analysis uses statistical mechanics tools, specifically solving over-damped Langevin equations and analyzing the low-temperature equilibrium state of the spin system.

**Model / system.** The model is a spherical spin system where the database is encoded by inter-spin couplings. The Hamiltonian includes coupling terms ($J_{IJ}$) and an external field term ($\epsilon h_I$), constrained by $\sum S_I^2 = N$.

**Key observables.** Mean magnetization of selected spins ($\langle S_1 angle, \langle S_2 angle$) and the relaxation time to equilibrium ($1/\sigma$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The search performance depends critically on the ratio between coupling strengths ($R$ vs $J$), temperature ($T$), and external field strength ($\epsilon$). The regime $T \ll R$ is crucial.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include neglecting inertia in the dynamics, assuming random Gaussian external fields $\{h_I\}$, and analyzing the system in the low-temperature limit ($T 	o 0$) or specific scaling regimes for $\epsilon$.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates a phase transition occurring at $T=R$, showing how selected spins begin to grow below this critical temperature, which is central to the search mechanism.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the model using Hamiltonian and Langevin dynamics, analyzes the low-temperature limit to determine relaxation times, and compares the resulting scaling of the search time against established quantum bounds like Grover's algorithm.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a physical realization of the unstructured database search that works via classical, dissipative model of spherical spins. The database is implemented via spin-spin couplings, where the selected coupling refers to a larger ferromagnetic interaction between two selected spins. The low-temperature equilibrium of this model leads to magnetization strongly concentrated on the selected spins, which means that the search is complete. The search time refers to the relaxation time to equilibrium from a homogeneous initial state, and is described via Langevin equations. This time scales as ${\cal O}(M^a)$ with $a<1/2$, where $M$ is the database volume. This is faster than Grover's search, showing how a dissipative, classical analog computer can overcome the quantum unitary computer.

</details>
