**Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes a Parity Anomalous Semimetal exhibiting a half-quantized Hall effect. By calculating the contributions from standard disorder mechanisms like side-jump and skew-scattering using advanced many-body techniques, the authors prove that these extrinsic effects vanish. This confirms the intrinsic robustness of the half-quantized topological state against realistic material disorder.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on topological materials, the rigorous mathematical treatment of disorder effects (using SCBA) and the demonstration of robustness against scattering are highly relevant to understanding localization phenomena in complex quantum systems, which shares methodological overlap with disordered lattice models used in condensed matter theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how extrinsic disorder contributions, specifically side-jump and skew-scattering mechanisms, affect the half-quantized anomalous Hall conductivity in a Parity Anomalous Semimetal (PAS).

**Main result.** The analysis rigorously demonstrates that both the side-jump and skew-scattering mechanisms make no net contribution to the half-quantized Hall conductance. This proves that the PAS phase is robust against such extrinsic disorder effects.

**Method.** The investigation employs analytical techniques, including the self-consistent Born approximation (SCBA) within the Kubo formalism, applied to a tight-binding Hamiltonian model.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a disordered Parity Anomalous Semimetal (PAS), derived from semi-magnetic topological insulator thin films exhibiting gapless Dirac cone surface states. The analysis uses lattice Hamiltonians and incorporates on-site random potentials for disorder scattering.

**Key observables.** Anomalous Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{xy}$), specifically its half-quantized value ($rac{1}{2} e^2/h$), and the contributions from side-jump ($\sigma_{sj}$) and skew-scattering ($\sigma_{sk}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Fermi level position relative to the Dirac cone, disorder strength ($W$), and scattering times ($	au$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include treating different scattering mechanisms as statistically independent and utilizing approximations like the Ioffe-Regel condition in certain regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate Feynman diagrams detailing various scattering contributions to $\sigma_{xy}$ and show schematic representations of how disorder affects gapless versus gapped Dirac cone states.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a theoretical derivation, first establishing the model Hamiltonian and then systematically calculating the total anomalous Hall conductivity by evaluating the individual contributions from side-jump and skew-scattering terms using advanced Green's function techniques.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present an analytical investigation of the extrinsic contributions to the anomalous Hall conductivity in the context of the half-quantized Hall effect observed in disordered parity anomalous semimetal emerged from semi-magnetic topological insulator thin films. The gapless Dirac cone surface state, which embodies the quintessence of the half-quantized Hall effect, exhibits remarkable robustness against disorder scattering. Two primary extrinsic mechanisms, the side-jump and skew-scattering, are deemed irrelevant and make no contributions. These results establish the parity anomalous semimetal as a disorder-resilient quantum phase, thereby providing insights into Dirac fermion physics.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper applies quantum mechanics concepts, specifically using non self-adjoint Hamiltonians, to create a 'quantum-like' model of political consensus. It tracks how supporters' opinions change based on the perceived competence of a governing coalition. The key novelty is demonstrating that this mathematical structure allows for observables to lose and regain compatibility over time, mirroring real-world shifts in public trust.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a novel mathematical framework—using non-Hermitian quantum dynamics—to model complex, open, and inherently non-equilibrium social phenomena like political trust decay, which is highly relevant to theoretical physics applications in decision theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper models how political consensus or trust changes over time based on a governing coalition's perceived performance.

**Main result.** The use of non self-adjoint Hamiltonians and associated $\gamma$-dynamics allows the model to capture the loss and recovery of compatibility between observables (like efficiency and supporter satisfaction) over time, which standard Hermitian dynamics cannot predict.

**Method.** The methodology employs a quantum-like formalism using non self-adjoint Hamiltonians ($H 
eq H^\dagger$) to govern time evolution via $\gamma$-dynamics ($\gamma_t(X) = e^{iH^\dagger t} X e^{-iHt}$).

**Model / system.** The system models a political coalition ($\C$) and its supporters ($\Sc$), represented by operators $\hat{C}$ (efficiency) and $\hat{F}$ (satisfaction). The dynamics are governed by various non-self-adjoint Hamiltonians ($H_1, H_2, H_3$).

**Key observables.** Time-evolved expectation values of observables ($\hat{c}(t), \hat{f}(t)$) and the commutator $[\gamma_t(\hat{F}), \gamma_t(\hat{C})]$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio $
u = \lambda_1 / \lambda_2$ (the index of success for G); coupling constants ($\lambda_i$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that standard self-adjoint dynamics are insufficient because they fail to predict the time evolution of observable compatibility in complex socio-political systems.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the sinusoidal time dependencies of $\hat{f}(t)$ and $\hat{c}(t)$ for various ratios of coupling constants ($\lambda_1, \lambda_2$), showing periodic behavior.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem using non-self-adjoint Hamiltonians, proposes three specific models ($H_1, H_2, H_3$) corresponding to different physical interpretations, and analyzes their dynamical behavior by calculating time evolution of observables and commutators.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We discuss here how non self-adjoint Hamiltonians, and their related Heisenberg-like dynamics, can be used to model a political system consisting in a coalition $\C$ of different parties (forming a government) and by their (original) supporters $\Sc$. Our aim is to model how the opinion of these supporters changes depending on the efficiency, competence and coherence of the coalition $\C$, as these are perceived by $\Sc$ during their action while governing. After a rather general introduction we propose three specific models, and we describe and comment the dynamical behaviour of the { full} system, $\Sc\cup\C$. The role of the so-called {\em balanced Hamiltonians}, recently introduced by the authors in connection with integrals of motion, is discussed in details.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops and compares two advanced methods—analytical high-frequency expansion and numerical flow equations—to find the effective Hamiltonian for a driven polar quantum system. It successfully handles complex interactions by treating longitudinal coupling nonperturbatively, providing accurate predictions across various coupling regimes relevant to modern quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a robust theoretical toolkit to analyze driven quantum systems where standard approximations fail due to strong coupling terms ($g_z \sim \omega$), making it highly relevant for trapped ion or superconducting circuit platforms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses developing a Floquet treatment for a driven polar two-level quantum system subjected to both longitudinal and transverse coupling from a periodic field.

**Main result.** A dual framework combining analytical high-frequency expansions with numerical flow equations successfully yields the time-independent effective Hamiltonian, even when longitudinal couplings are strong.

**Method.** The authors employ Floquet theory, utilizing an analytical expansion (up to first order in $\omega^{-1}$) and a numerical flow-equation approach based on continuous block diagonalization.

**Model / system.** The system is a driven two-level quantum system characterized by a Hamiltonian involving longitudinal ($g_z$) and transverse ($g_x$) coupling strengths modulated periodically by the driving frequency ($\omega$).

**Key observables.** Effective transverse coupling strength, effective detuning, time evolution operator ($\hat{U}_{	ext{eff}}$), and reconstructed micromotion components.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Driving frequency ($\omega$), longitudinal coupling ($g_z$), transverse coupling ($g_x$), and the ratio $g_x/\omega$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analytical method relies on a high-frequency expansion in $\omega^{-1}$, while the numerical flow approach requires truncation of the harmonic series.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare convergence of harmonic weights, validate the zeroth-plus-first-order expansion against numerical results, and show agreement between direct integration, flow-equation, and analytical effective Hamiltonians across various coupling regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the physical model and Hamiltonian. It then details the analytical derivation using dressing transformations and high-frequency expansions. Subsequently, it presents the complementary numerical flow-equation framework for calculating the time-independent effective Hamiltonian, culminating in comparisons between both methods.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present an analytical and numerical Floquet treatment of a driven polar two-level quantum system characterized by both longitudinal and transverse coupling to a periodic field. Analytically, we derive a dressed-frame effective Hamiltonian up to first order in the inverse driving frequency, incorporating the longitudinal coupling nonperturbatively. This yields closed expressions for the effective transverse coupling strength and the effective detuning, both of which are modified by the presence of the longitudinal interaction. In the nonpolar limit, these expressions recover the usual near-resonant Rabi coupling and the Bloch-Siegert shift. As a second main result, we develop a numerical flow-equation framework that yields a time-independent effective Hamiltonian across a broad range of transverse and longitudinal coupling strengths. This dual framework is relevant for a variety of platforms, including driven polar quantum systems, optical lattices, superconducting circuits, and solids subject to surface acoustic waves.

</details>

**Summary.** This work derives the hydrodynamic action for anomalous fermionic fluids by calculating the low-energy effective action from the Dirac fermion's path integral. It successfully incorporates chiral anomalies using transgression forms derived from higher-dimensional bulk terms. The resulting framework provides a microscopic, quantum field theory justification for the necessary ingredients in anomalous hydrodynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter theory (anomalous hydrodynamics), the rigorous use of path integrals, effective field theories, and symmetry constraints mirrors advanced techniques used in describing quantum many-body systems far from equilibrium.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To provide a microscopic explanation for incorporating chiral anomalies into the hydrodynamic action formulation derived from fermionic path integrals.

**Main result.** The effective low-energy action is shown to correspond to previously proposed anomalous hydrodynamic actions, yielding specific forms for single-fluid and two-fluid systems involving transgression forms.

**Method.** The analysis involves integrating out Dirac fermions via the path integral method, utilizing anomaly inflow concepts (5D Chern-Simons terms), and deriving the low-energy effective action through restricted variational principles.

**Model / system.** The system models a perfect fluid described by an anomalous Dirac fermion coupled to background vector and axial gauge fields. The theory is analyzed in both single-fluid and two-fluid (vector/axial) regimes.

**Key observables.** Hydrodynamic action ($	ext{Seff}$), transgression forms, conserved currents ($J, 	ilde{J}$), and the resulting equations of motion derived from restricted variations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Zero temperature limit, residual irrelevant current-current interactions, and coupling constants related to anomaly coefficients ($\alpha$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation assumes an infrared fluid phase where a semiclassical low-energy effective action can be obtained, requiring the use of restricted variations (gauge/diffeomorphism) to define local 4D equations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from analyzing the fermionic path integral and obtaining the initial effective action via anomaly inflow. It then details the derivation of the single-fluid and two-fluid actions, culminating in applying restricted variational principles to derive the final local 4D hydrodynamic equations of motion.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The path integral of the Dirac fermion with vector and axial gauge backgrounds is analyzed near the infrared limit in the presence of residual irrelevant current-current interaction. After integrating out fermions, a semiclassical low-energy effective action is obtained, written in terms of currents. Its expression is found to correspond to the hydrodynamic action previously proposed for perfect barotropic fluids with anomalies at zero temperature. This approach also leads to two further hydrodynamic actions to be associated, respectively, with the Weyl fermion, and the Dirac fermion having independent vector and axial currents. These actions feature four- and five-dimensional bulk-boundary terms, owing to anomaly inflow, which are identified as being the so-called transgression forms. These are generalizations of Chern--Simons forms that involve two gauge fields: the dynamical field and the background field. The path-integral argument provides a ``microscopic'' explanation for several ingredients of the action formulation of hydrodynamics that are necessary to incorporate anomalies. It also clarifies the infrared reduction required to pass from the effective field theory to a local hydrodynamic description. This reduction is implemented by considering restricted variations of the action, familiar from hydrodynamics, which at the same time lead to four-dimensional equations of motion from the five-dimensional transgression terms.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a comprehensive model showing that complex quantum phenomena—from interference patterns to classical motion—are unified as coarse-grained manifestations of stochastic unitary diffusion in projective Hilbert space. By using random matrices, it numerically confirms how measurement and environmental monitoring effectively 'lose' information about the full quantum history, leading to apparent irreversibility.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, unifying mathematical framework connecting fundamental quantum concepts like measurement collapse, decoherence, and classical emergence through the lens of random matrix theory dynamics. It offers concrete numerical tests for abstract theoretical conjectures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research investigates how various quantum phenomena, such as Born rule frequencies, double-slit interference, and classical motion, emerge from the stochastic unitary evolution governed by random matrices in projective Hilbert space.

**Main result.** It demonstrates that these diverse physical manifestations are all consistent coarse-grained consequences of a single underlying stochastic unitary mechanism driven by Gaussian Unitary Ensemble Hamiltonians.

**Method.** The work relies heavily on numerical simulations to test theoretical predictions, analyzing projected increments, path reversibility, and the behavior of state vectors under measurement constraints.

**Model / system.** The core model is projective quantum mechanics ($\mathbb{CP}^{N-1}$), where state evolution is driven by random Hamiltonians drawn from GUE. The analysis extends to multi-particle systems and incorporates detector-defined equivalence classes.

**Key observables.** Isotropy/Homogeneity of diffusion, Gaussianity of projected increments, Born frequencies matching hitting probabilities, and the stability of recorded measurement records (Zeno effect analogue).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality $N$, the ratio of device localization scale to detector resolution ($\delta D/R_D$), and the number of environmental monitoring steps ($n$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include approximating continuous dynamics via discrete random matrix steps, assuming Gaussian state representations on localized manifolds, and defining effective irreversibility through coarse-graining by measurement.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the failure of GOE vs. GUE isotropy (Fig 1), Brownian scaling on classical submanifolds (Fig 2/3), matching Born frequencies to random walk hitting probabilities (Fig 8), and showing path loss leading to apparent irreversibility (Figs 41, 43).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds logically by first establishing the basic stochastic diffusion properties in projective space (GUE vs GOE), then restricting this motion to classical submanifolds ($M_\sigma$), analyzing boundary effects and measurement outcomes (Born rule/double-slit), extending to multi-particle systems, and finally synthesizing these findings into a unified picture of effective irreversibility.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present numerical simulations supporting the random-matrix state-reduction framework developed in the companion theoretical paper. The simulations test the main derived features of the model: isotropic diffusion generated by Gaussian Unitary Ensemble Hamiltonians in projective state space, the restriction of this diffusion to Brownian motion on the classical submanifold, Born-rule frequencies for detector-defined outcome classes, and stroboscopic Newtonian motion for macroscopic systems under repeated environmental monitoring. We also compare GUE and GOE random Hamiltonians and show that GOE fails to produce the required isotropic complex projective diffusion. Further simulations examine finite-resolution detector records in the double-slit experiment, Zeno stability of recorded equivalence classes, effective irreversibility from high-dimensional state-space dynamics and loss of path information, and tensor-product particle-device dynamics in the device limit. The results show that microscopic state reduction, stable measurement records, effective irreversibility, and macroscopic classicality can be described as different coarse-grained manifestations of the same stochastic unitary mechanism.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces and analyzes the Topological Spectral Form Factor (TopSFF) as a probe for global quantum properties in chaotic many-body systems. By mapping this observable to an effective non-Hermitian single-particle model, the authors precisely locate and characterize a $\mathcal{PT}$ symmetry breaking transition at a finite interaction strength. This methodology offers a universal signature of underlying spectral phase transitions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, universal diagnostic tool ($	ext{TopSFF}$) to detect fundamental symmetries like $\mathcal{PT}$ symmetry breaking in complex quantum chaotic systems without needing explicit knowledge of the full many-body Hamiltonian dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how topological defects inserted into quantum partition functions can probe global properties, specifically mapping the Topological Spectral Form Factor (TopSFF) to an emergent non-Hermitian single-particle problem.

**Main result.** The TopSFF reveals a clear signature of $\mathcal{PT}$ symmetry breaking in the effective dynamics: monotonic exponential scaling below a critical interaction strength ($\epsilon_{	ext{EP}}$), and oscillations above it, with linear enhancement at $\epsilon_{	ext{EP}}$.

**Method.** The authors use diagrammatic techniques, mapping the TopSFF to an emergent $(3+1)$D temporal domain wall (tDW) problem whose dynamics are analyzed via a transfer matrix approach.

**Model / system.** The analysis is applied to generic 1D many-body chaotic systems modeled by random quantum circuits (e.g., Random Phase Model). The key diagnostic tool is the TopSFF, which probes mismatched spacetime world-sheet topologies.

**Key observables.** Topological Spectral Form Factor ($	ext{TopSFF}$), effective discriminant ($b_\Delta$), and scaling functions for topological free energy differences ($\Delta F_{	ext{top}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strength ($\epsilon$) relative to the critical point ($\epsilon_{	ext{EP}}$), system size ($L$), and defect type (global swap vs. time translation).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping the complex many-body problem onto an effective non-Hermitian single-particle dynamics, particularly in the large-$q$ limit.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the three distinct regimes of $	ext{TopSFF}$ vs. interaction strength ($\epsilon$), showing monotonic growth, oscillations, and linear enhancement at the exceptional point. Other figures confirm universal scaling collapses for both SFF and TopSFF in different Thouless limits.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops the concept by defining the $	ext{TopSFF}$ using topological defects, deriving its mapping to a tDW problem, analyzing the resulting non-Hermitian Hamiltonian's spectral properties (eigenvalues/discriminant), and finally confirming these findings through multiple scaling limits and finite-$q$ numerical checks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In equilibrium physics, topological defect insertions in quantum and classical partition functions provide non-perturbative probes of phase transitions beyond local observables. In non-equilibrium physics, the spectral form factor provides a minimal probe of universal quantum dynamics, and admits a representation as a product of two partition functions at imaginary inverse temperature. We define the topological spectral form factor (TopSFF) by inserting topological defects acting non-trivially on the doubled partition functions, producing mismatched spacetime world-sheet topologies. For the minimal $\mathbb{Z}_2$ spatially extended defect, implemented by the global swap operator, we derive an exact mapping of the TopSFF of a generic 1D many-body chaotic system to an emergent $(3+1)$D non-Hermitian single-particle problem describing a temporal domain wall (tDW). We show analytically that the effective tDW dynamics undergoes a $\mathcal{PT}$ symmetry breaking transition at a finite interaction strength $ε_{\mathrm{EP}}$: below $ε_{\mathrm{EP}}$, the leading modes are polarized into Gaussian or non-Gaussian tDW sectors and the TopSFF varies monotonically and exponentially with system size; above $ε_{\mathrm{EP}}$, the tDW sectors hybridize and the TopSFF oscillates with system size; at the exceptional point $ε_{\mathrm{EP}}$, Jordan non-diagonality produces a linear-in-system-size enhancement. For temporally extended topological defects, we derive exact universal scaling forms for the TopSFF free energy in systems with time reversal or time translation symmetry, and verify them numerically in independent models.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores how isolated quantum systems can spontaneously exhibit classical behavior through intrinsic equilibration processes. It establishes that operational classicality arises when coherence becomes inaccessible to measurements, rather than requiring its physical disappearance. This provides a direct theoretical connection between thermalization in closed systems and the origin of classicality.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a fundamental theoretical link between two major open problems—thermalization and quantum-to-classical transition—within the context of closed quantum systems, challenging the necessity of external environments for classical behavior.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates whether the intrinsic equilibration mechanism observed in fully isolated quantum systems can also account for the emergence of classical behavior (the quantum-to-classical transition).

**Main result.** Operational classicality can emerge even when substantial coherence remains in the equilibrium state, provided that this coherence becomes operationally inaccessible to measurements.

**Method.** The authors derive sufficient conditions for operational classicality by analyzing time-averaged expectation values and bounding fluctuations using concepts derived from equilibration theory.

**Model / system.** The study considers isolated quantum systems evolving unitarily under a Hamiltonian H, focusing on the transition from pure state evolution to behavior indistinguishable from a classical mixture.

**Key observables.** Expectation values of observables $\langle \hat{O} angle(t)$, operational distinguishability between states ($ho$ vs. $ho_B$), and coherence components ($\|ho_{	ext{coh}}^{H,B}\|$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The time-average limit ($T 	o \infty$) and the effective dimension ($d_{	ext{eff}}$) governing fluctuation bounds.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is operational, focusing on indistinguishability with respect to a restricted set of coarse-grained observables; it assumes specific mathematical relationships between state evolution and equilibrium states.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the established understanding of thermalization in isolated systems to derive rigorous bounds connecting this equilibration process directly to the conditions required for operational classicality, showing two complementary routes for this emergence.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The emergence of classical behavior and the origin of thermal equilibrium are two central problems in the foundations of physics. In the standard accounts, both phenomena are typically explained through interactions with an external environment: decoherence suppresses quantum interference, while coupling to a thermal bath drives relaxation toward equilibrium. Over the last decades, however, it has become clear that equilibration and thermalization can arise even in fully isolated quantum systems, in the operational sense that the expectation values of relevant observables remain close to equilibrium values for most of the time. Here, we ask whether the same intrinsic equilibration mechanism can also account for the emergence of classical behavior. Using rigorous bounds on equilibration in closed systems, we derive sufficient conditions under which a time-evolved pure state becomes, for most times, operationally indistinguishable from a classical mixture associated with a chosen physical property. We identify two complementary routes to such operational classicality: either the chosen property almost commutes with the system Hamiltonian or the observables used to probe the system lose access to the remaining coherence after equilibration. Our results show that classical behavior need not be confined to the energy basis and may emerge even when substantial coherence remains present in the equilibrium state. This establishes a direct connection between two foundational questions: the origin of thermalization in isolated quantum systems and the quantum-to-classical transition

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a hydrodynamic model for exciton transport coupled to an electron-hole plasma in 2D semiconductors. It shows that while simple collision models predict positive diffusion, including the inertia of the plasma leads to hybridization with acoustic plasmon modes. This coupling generates a dynamical instability, manifesting as a theoretically predicted negative effective exciton diffusivity.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter transport, the mechanism—where collective plasma modes drive a non-dissipative instability that modifies particle transport coefficients—is analogous to instabilities studied in open quantum systems or driven many-body dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates exciton transport dynamics, specifically focusing on the theoretical conditions under which an effective negative diffusion coefficient can arise in two-dimensional semiconductors.

**Main result.** Negative exciton diffusivity is predicted to occur when the system's collective plasma oscillations (plasmons) couple strongly with the slow excitonic motion, leading to a dynamical instability rather than simple nonlinear effects.

**Method.** The authors developed and utilized a minimal hydrodynamic framework by treating excitons, electrons, and holes as coupled fluids, analyzing the resulting dispersion relations in Fourier space.

**Model / system.** The physical system is an exciton-electron-hole plasma within 2D semiconductors (like TMDs). The model treats the three species using coupled fluid dynamics equations, moving from simple collision models to full self-consistent analysis incorporating plasmons.

**Key observables.** Effective exciton diffusivity ($D_{eff}^X$), dispersion relation ($\omega$), and the sign of the effective diffusion coefficient (positive for damping, negative for instability).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Plasma inertia, collective charge oscillation frequency ($\omega_p$), collision rates ($\gamma_X, \gamma_p$), and the degree of coupling between excitons and plasma modes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Initial analyses assume quasi-neutrality or symmetric effective masses; the key result requires incorporating plasmons beyond simple diffusive plasma dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 illustrates normal, dissipative diffusion (positive $D_{eff}^X$), while Figure 3 demonstrates the negative diffusion regime characterized by localized density enhancement and growing amplitude due to hybridization with the acoustic plasma mode.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from analyzing simple collisional regimes where diffusivity remains positive, through incorporating full self-consistent dynamics involving plasmons, culminating in the derivation of the instability condition leading to negative effective diffusivity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a minimal hydrodynamic framework to describe exciton transport in the presence of an electron hole plasma in 2D semiconductors. Treating excitons, electrons, and holes as coupled fluids, we show that exciton diffusion is strongly renormalized by momentum exchange with the plasma. In the collisional regime, mutual diffusion leads to a nontrivial redistribution of transport coefficients but preserves the positivity of the exciton diffusivity. In contrast, when plasma inertia and collective charge oscillations are accounted for, the exciton diffusive mode hybridizes with acoustic plasma modes, giving rise to a dynamical instability manifested as an effective negative diffusion coefficient. We demonstrate that this instability originates from the nonequilibrium coupling between slow excitons and fast plasma degrees of freedom, rather than from nonlinear diffusion or thermodynamic effects. Our results provide a unified physical mechanism for negative exciton diffusivity reported in recent experiments and establish collective plasma dynamics as a key control parameter of exciton transport in 2D materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Blind Symmetry Matching, an advanced technique to estimate unknown statistical properties in quantum computations by first discovering the underlying symmetry group from measurement data. By selecting and exploiting this discovered symmetry, the method significantly reduces the required number of measurements (shots) compared to structure-blind estimation techniques.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful, general-purpose primitive for quantum algorithm design that moves beyond assuming prior knowledge of system symmetries, which is crucial for applying quantum computation to complex physical systems where symmetries are emergent or unknown.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a method, called Blind Symmetry Matching, that reduces the required number of measurement shots for estimating unknown statistical quantities in quantum computations when the underlying symmetry is not known beforehand.

**Main result.** The proposed pipeline successfully discovers an unknown symmetry group and exploits it to achieve significant shot count reductions, with the strong confinement mode showing superior scaling ($\propto L^2$) compared to structure-blind methods.

**Method.** The method involves a three-step process: (1) Discovery of candidate symmetries via specialized tests (weak/strong twirls), (2) Selection of the largest passing group ($G^\star$), and (3) Exploitation by measuring the target observable in the symmetry-adapted basis.

**Model / system.** The framework is general over finite groups, demonstrated using quantum computations on systems like an L-site ring or two interacting systems. The target quantity to be estimated is a statistical expectation value of an operator $R$.

**Key observables.** Matching Score ($s_T(R)$), Shot Count Reduction Factor, Weak/Strong Twirl measurements.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size ($L$), group order ($|G|$), and the ratio between discovery cost and exploitation savings.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The target quantity must be statistical (an expectation value or distribution) rather than a single deterministic outcome. The discovery overhead must be less than the shot savings achieved by exploiting the symmetry.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the overall blind-matching pipeline (Discovery $	o$ Selection $	o$ Exploitation), detailing circuits for weak vs. strong twirls, and comparing scaling costs ($L^4$ vs $L^3$ vs $L^2$) across different modes.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of unknown symmetry estimation, details the mathematical framework involving weak (commutation) and strong (confinement) symmetry tests, proves the selection rule is unbiased, and concludes with seeded demonstrations showing exponential resource savings.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Measuring a quantum computation in a basis adapted to a symmetry it carries reduces the repeated measurements, commonly referred to as ``shots'', needed to read a statistical answer. Detecting the symmetry a quantum state carries has many uses: certifying a claimed symmetry, identifying a conserved-charge sector, flagging symmetry-breaking as an error signature, and selecting a compression or readout basis; shot-count reduction is developed here as one exemplary case. Existing methods assume the symmetry is known in advance; we remove that assumption. When it is unknown, the carried symmetry is discovered from the data by a symmetry test that scores candidate groups, and the largest passing group is exploited as the measurement basis. We state the pipeline precisely, prove the selection rule is unbiased, and charge discovery in full. Two conditions are treated, both detected by the same score with a different projection: a weak condition, commutation with the representation, and a strong condition, confinement to a single charge sector, the distinction drawn in the quantum-reference-frame literature. A single circuit, a controlled twirl followed by a SWAP test, discovers both: discarding the group register tests the weak condition, post-selecting it the strong one. The framework is general over finite groups, with cyclic (Fourier), dihedral, and symmetric-group (Schur-Weyl) examples; strong confinement to the symmetric, or Dicke, subspace is an exponential reduction. Seeded demonstrations show the loop wins net of discovery: weak matching on momentum readout reduces shots by a factor widening from ten to several thousand, and strong matching on a two-system target by a further factor of the subsystem size. Blind symmetry matching is a practical primitive for the common case where the matched basis cannot be written down in advance.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors developed and tested a miniaturized diamond magnetometer capable of measuring magnetic fields non-contactly in confined spaces. By coupling excitation and collection through separate fiber cores, they overcame previous performance bottlenecks. This allowed them to successfully image the internal current flow within a lithium-ion battery, opening new avenues for portable energy diagnostics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work represents a significant engineering achievement in applied quantum sensing, demonstrating how fundamental quantum phenomena ($	ext{NV}^-$ magnetometry) can be packaged into robust, deployable tools for industrial diagnostics (battery testing).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is developing a quantum magnetometer sensitive enough for real-world, non-contact measurements within confined spaces, such as imaging the internal current flow of packaged batteries.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated an endoscopic diamond magnetometer achieving $91 	ext{ pT}/\sqrt{	ext{Hz}}$ sensitivity while mapping depth-integrated current density maps inside a lithium-ion pouch cell during charge and discharge cycles.

**Method.** Magnetic field measurements are performed using Optically Detected Magnetic Resonance (ODMR) on Nitrogen-Vacancy centers in diamond, coupled with advanced signal processing like closed-loop frequency tracking and real-space regularization for inverse problem solving.

**Model / system.** The system uses a miniaturized, multi-core fiber-coupled diamond sensor head. The physics relies on the spin triplet ground state of $	ext{NV}^-$ centers, whose resonance frequencies shift linearly with the local magnetic field ($\delta
u \propto B$).

**Key observables.** Magnetic-field sensitivity ($91 	ext{ pT}/\sqrt{	ext{Hz}}$), depth-integrated sheet current density maps ($\mathbf{K}(x, y)$ in $	ext{A/m}$), and the ODMR resonance frequency shift.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Sensor diameter ($\le 6 	ext{ mm}$), measurement bandwidth ($2 	ext{ kHz}$), stand-off distance ($\approx 2 	ext{ mm}$), and regularization parameters ($\lambda$, $\mu_{	ext{div}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The reconstructed current density is depth-integrated, meaning the exact spatial distribution of current within the battery thickness cannot be resolved. The measurement assumes a linear relationship between field shift and magnetic field.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the endoscopic sensor architecture (multi-core fiber bundle), the setup for imaging batteries, and maps showing reconstructed current density components ($K_x, K_y$) during charging/discharging cycles.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical limitations of miniaturization to detailing the optical engineering solution (multi-core coupling). It then describes the quantum measurement technique (ODMR with closed-loop tracking) and culminates in applying this system to solve a complex inverse problem: reconstructing internal current sheets from external magnetic field measurements.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Transitioning quantum magnetometry from laboratory environments to real-world applications has been limited by a persistent trade-off between sensor miniaturization and magnetic sensitivity. While bulky systems can achieve high sensitivity, endoscopic probes commonly suffer from inefficient fluorescence collection and reduced performance. Here we resolve this trade-off and present a miniaturized diamond quantum magnetometer with a 6 mm diameter endoscopic sensor head, achieving a magnetic-field sensitivity of 91 pT/sqrt(Hz) with a 2 kHz measurement bandwidth in a magnetically unshielded environment. The fluorescence collection bottleneck is overcome by separating excitation and collection into different cores of a fused multi-core fiber bundle, coupled to the diamond through a custom high-numerical-aperture micro-objective. A compact FPGA-based backend performs microwave control, lock-in detection and real-time resonance tracking, enabling robust operation during magnetic-field imaging. To demonstrate the practical utility of the miniaturized sensor, we image the magnetic field of a commercial lithium-ion pouch cell during charge and discharge and reconstruct depth-integrated current-density maps of the current flow. These results show that endoscopic diamond magnetometers can combine high sensitivity with a probe geometry suitable for confined, unshielded measurements, opening new avenues in battery technology and beyond.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops a novel, fidelity-based measure of quantum coherence applicable within the framework of strictly incoherent operations. It proves this measure's mathematical validity and monotonicity, and critically applies it to multi-path interferometers. The results yield quantitative bounds on wave-particle duality expressed in terms of fidelity, offering deeper insights into fundamental quantum behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous mathematical tool ($C_{	ext{diag}}$) to quantify quantum coherence loss under realistic physical constraints (SIOs), which is crucial for understanding decoherence processes relevant across quantum optics and information theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the fundamental challenge of quantifying quantum coherence, particularly within the mathematical framework defined by strictly incoherent operations (SIOs). It aims to establish a robust measure that characterizes this loss of quantumness.

**Main result.** A fidelity-based coherence measure, $C_F^{	ext{diag}}(ho)$, is proposed and proven to be monotonic under SIOs. Furthermore, the authors derive quantitative wave-particle duality relations for multi-path interferometers using this measure.

**Method.** The methodology involves defining a general coherence measure based on the fidelity between the state $ho$ and its diagonal projection $\Delta(ho)$, leading to $C_F^{	ext{diag}}(ho) = 1 - F(ho, \Delta(ho))$. This is then applied analytically and numerically to interferometric models.

**Model / system.** The primary model involves general quantum states $ho$ in a $d$-dimensional Hilbert space. The application context is the multi-path interferometer, which allows for the characterization of wave-particle duality using coherence measures.

**Key observables.** Coherence measures ($C_{	ext{diag}}$), Wave Measure ($W(ho)$), Particle Measure ($P(ho)$), and Fidelity ($F$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionality $d$ (number of paths) is a key parameter, as are the rank $k$ of the diagonal state $\Delta(ho)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The coherence measure $C_{	ext{diag}}(ho)$ is inherently basis-dependent because it relies on projecting onto a specific reference basis via $\Delta(\cdot)$.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the calculated values of the coherence measure against theoretical upper bounds (e.g., Theorem 4). Figure 3 specifically shows $P-W$ diagrams for sampled pure states in various dimensions ($d=3, 10, 25, 50$), illustrating that these points lie within a boundary defined by the quadric form $W^2 + P^2 = 1 - 1/\sqrt{d}$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the general theory of coherence measures in SIOs, proposing and proving properties for $C_{	ext{diag}}(ho)$. It then rigorously proves its monotonicity. Finally, it applies this framework to derive quantitative bounds on wave-particle duality in multi-path interferometers.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantifying coherence is an essential endeavor in both quantum foundations and quantum technologies. In this paper, we study the coherence measures in terms of the diagonal states in the strictly incoherent operations framework. Specifically, we propose a coherence measure in terms of fidelity and provide its analytical expression. The relations between the proposed coherence measure and some other coherence measures are derived. Furthermore, we prove its monotonicity under incoherent operations. As an application, we explore the role of the proposed coherence measure in characterizing the waveness in the multi-path interferometer. As a result, some wave-particle dualities in terms of fidelity are presented. This work not only deepens the interpretation of the diagonal states on characterizing quantum states, but also promotes the quantitative description of the wave-particle behaviors in the multi-path interferometer.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates how large deviations in order parameters reveal fundamental aspects of criticality that standard averages miss. By studying percolation and Ising models on various lattices, the authors derive universal scaling laws for these tails. The results demonstrate a deep connection between geometric constraints (like those found on complete graphs) and the underlying critical physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical statistical mechanics, the rigorous mathematical techniques for analyzing rare events and universality in critical systems are foundational concepts applicable across many-body physics, including quantum phase transitions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on probing universality beyond standard finite-size scaling by analyzing the large-deviation tails of critical order-parameter distributions.

**Main result.** The analysis establishes universal scaling forms for these tails, revealing that rare configurations in complex systems (like the complete graph FK-Ising model) can be governed by different underlying physics (e.g., percolation vs. Ising).

**Method.** The authors employ large-deviation theory and finite-size scaling arguments to derive and test universal scaling forms for the cumulative distribution functions of rescaled order parameters.

**Model / system.** The work examines critical phenomena in both bond percolation and the Fortuin–Kasteleyn (FK) Ising model across various geometries: 2D lattices, 3D lattices, and complete graphs.

**Key observables.** Rescaled magnetization-like variable ($x_m = |M|/\langle|M|angle$) and the rescaled largest-cluster variable ($x_C = C_1/\langle C_1angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Critical exponents, dimensionality (2D, 3D), and the mean-field limit (complete graph).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the leading exponential cost in the large deviation tail is governed by specific critical exponents derived from scaling theory.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the collapse of scaled probability distributions for both order parameters across different dimensions, highlighting deviations and crossovers (e.g., percolation-like vs. Ising-like scaling on the complete graph).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops theoretical frameworks using finite-size scaling to predict tail behavior, applies these forms to specific models (percolation/Ising) on different lattices, and uses Monte Carlo simulations to test the predicted universal scaling laws.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Large-deviation tails of critical probability distributions provide a sensitive probe of universality beyond standard finite-size scaling. We study these tails for critical percolation and Fortuin--Kasteleyn Ising models on two-dimensional lattices, three-dimensional lattices, and complete graphs. We consider two rescaled order parameters: the magnetization-like variable $x_m=|M|/\langle |M|\rangle$, including a signed cluster-mass analogue for percolation, and the largest-cluster variable $x_C=C_1/\langle C_1\rangle$. For $x_m$, we test the expected stretched-exponential large-deviation tail and show that the same form applies to the percolation analogue. For $x_C$, guided by the exact complete-graph result and scaling arguments, we propose universal scaling forms for both tails of the cumulative distribution and test them by extensive Monte Carlo simulations. In the complete-graph FK-Ising model, the left tail is governed by rare configurations with percolation-like scaling rather than by the typical Ising scaling. Our results show that the tails of order-parameter distributions reveal universal features of critical fluctuations that are not captured by averaged observables alone.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an all-reflective interferometer designed to overcome wavefront distortion issues inherent in free-space quantum communication. By using only mirrors, the device maintains high interference visibility and remains achromatic across broadband light sources. This makes it a robust alternative to traditional interferometers for applications like satellite quantum links.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses hardware limitations in quantum networking by proposing a robust, passive optical element suitable for field-free propagation channels, which is crucial for scaling up quantum information infrastructure.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is developing passive interferometers for free-space quantum communication that can operate reliably over turbulent atmospheric channels without complex adaptive optics.

**Main result.** The proposed all-reflective interferometer achieves high interference visibility (>0.97) for spatially multimode beams and is inherently achromatic across broadband wavelengths, outperforming refractive alternatives.

**Method.** The authors employed numerical optical ray tracing simulations to model the performance of their novel reflective interferometer design (Offner Relay Interferometer).

**Model / system.** The core system is an All Reflective Field-widened Unbalanced Interferometer utilizing only standard spherical mirrors in a cavity configuration. This setup is designed for time-bin encoded qubits used in quantum communication.

**Key observables.** Interference visibility (V), physical footprint, and chromatic dependence of the interference signal across broadband wavelengths (600 nm to 1000 nm).

**Important parameters / regimes.** High visibility (>0.97); achromatic performance; ability to handle spatially multimode beams.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The design is assumed to be limited by beam clipping in the horizontal direction and that refractive interferometers suffer from significant chromatic dispersion.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the ORI schematic, simulated interference maxima/minima showing high visibility even with angular offsets, and comparisons demonstrating the ORI's wavelength independence versus dispersive glass setups. Other figures show imaging capabilities.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the need for turbulence-resistant quantum links, presents the reflective Offner Relay Interferometer (ORI) design, validates its performance via simulations against refractive and standard Michelson designs, and demonstrates its applicability in sensing and communication contexts.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Interference of time-bin encoded signals over free-space optical channels typically requires stringent mode filtering on receivers due to wavefront distortions from atmospheric turbulence, conventionally addressed with adaptive optics. Passive multimode receivers based on field-widened interferometers present a compelling alternative, enabling direct interference without the overhead of wavefront correction. We demonstrate a field-widened interferometer design that is implemented solely with reflective surfaces and achieves a high interference visibility (greater than 0.97) for spatially multimode beams. The interference of the multimode beams is enabled by two imaging systems that consist of a cavity configuration between a spherical concave mirror and a flat mirror. The configuration enables small form-factors, is inherently achromatic, and is based on standard spherical mirrors which reduces the complexity of the system. The interferometer is applicable for spatially multimode and turbulent optical channels, such as satellite communication, and is designed for quantum systems that use time-bin encoded qubits.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports definitive experimental evidence for $d$-wave altermagnetism in the material $	ext{La}_2	ext{O}_3	ext{Mn}_2	ext{Se}_2$ using resonant inelastic X-ray scattering (RIXS). By analyzing circular dichroism, the authors resolve a long-standing debate by proving that this signal is an intrinsic consequence of altermagnetic symmetry rather than an experimental artifact. This establishes polarized RIXS as a powerful diagnostic tool for characterizing exotic magnetic phases.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a highly sensitive spectroscopic tool (polarized RIXS) to probe subtle magnetic symmetries in correlated materials, offering insights into how lattice structure dictates emergent quantum magnetic order beyond simple collinear models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to definitively resolve whether circular dichroism observed in Resonant Inelastic X-ray Scattering (RIXS) is an intrinsic signature of altermagnetism or merely an artifact of experimental geometry.

**Main result.** The authors provide unambiguous evidence for $d$-wave altermagnetism in $	ext{La}_2	ext{O}_3	ext{Mn}_2	ext{Se}_2$, proving that the observed RIXS dichroism is a direct consequence of fundamental altermagnetic symmetry constraints.

**Method.** The research combines advanced experimental measurements using circularly polarized RIXS at the Mn $L_3$ edge with theoretical modeling via Exact Diagonalization (ED) calculations based on lattice symmetries.

**Model / system.** The system is the strongly correlated Lieb-lattice magnet $	ext{La}_2	ext{O}_3	ext{Mn}_2	ext{Se}_2$, which exhibits altermagnetism—a magnetic order with compensated moments lacking relativistic spin-orbit coupling. The theoretical framework models two non-interacting Mn sublattices connected by specific symmetry operations.

**Key observables.** Circular dichroism ($\Delta I = I_{CL} - I_{CR}$) in the single-magnon excitation peak, which exhibits a distinct $d$-wave symmetry and vanishes above the Néel temperature.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The measurements are performed at low temperatures (e.g., 15 K) and involve varying incident photon energy, momentum transfer ($\mathbf{q}$), and azimuthal angle ($\phi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed dichroism is governed by symmetry constraints derived from the altermagnetic order, independent of magnon branch splitting or quasi-elastic susceptibility.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the crystal structure and RIXS geometry; key figures map the momentum dependence and azimuthal ($\phi$) variation of the circular dichroism signal, showing clear $d$-wave anisotropy at low temperatures.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical context of altermagnetism, details the experimental setup using polarized RIXS on $	ext{La}_2	ext{O}_3	ext{Mn}_2	ext{Se}_2$, presents the measurement results showing $d$-wave dichroism, and concludes by theoretically proving that this signal arises directly from symmetry breaking inherent to the altermagnetic state.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Altermagnetism defines a third fundamental class of collinear magnetic order, featuring compensated magnetic moments with antiparallel spin alignment, yet lifted Kramers degeneracy without the need for relativistic spin-orbit coupling. Its ability to host spin-polarized electronic bands and unconventional chiral magnons makes it a promising platform for functional materials. However, experimental verification has proven challenging; while circular dichroism in resonant inelastic X-ray scattering (RIXS) has been suggested as a signature of chiral magnons, it remains controversial whether this effect is an intrinsic property of altermagnetism or an artifact of experimental geometry. In this work, we resolve this debate and provide unambiguous experimental evidence of $d$-wave altermagnetism in the strongly correlated Lieb-lattice magnet La$_2$O$_3$Mn$_2$Se$_2$. The RIXS spectra exhibit a $d$-wave-symmetry circular dichroism in the magnetic excitations that vanishes in the paramagnetic phase. Through RIXS-operator symmetry analysis and exact-diagonalization calculations, we prove that the observed dichroism is a direct consequence of altermagnetic symmetry constraints, independent of magnon branch splitting. Our results provide definitive evidence for the experimental realization of $d$-wave altermagnetism in La$_2$O$_3$Mn$_2$Se$_2$ and establish circularly polarized RIXS as a highly symmetry-sensitive spectroscopic framework for detecting magnetic phases that evade conventional probes.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops an effective field theory framework to analyze the notorious fermion sign problem in BFSS matrix quantum mechanics. By using high-temperature expansions and symmetry arguments, the authors determine that while the sign problem is present, its perturbative onset is delayed, suggesting manageable errors at moderate temperatures. The work establishes scaling laws for the suppression of the phase factor in the large-$N$ limit.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on high-energy physics, the methodology of analyzing complex phases and divergences in path integrals is highly relevant to understanding sign problems in lattice simulations of quantum field theories, which share mathematical structures with many statistical mechanics models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is the fermion sign problem, which prevents direct classical simulation of quantum theories involving fermions using Monte Carlo methods.

**Main result.** The authors show that while the sign problem exists in the continuum limit for BFSS theory, its leading perturbative onset is at a high loop order, and for moderate temperatures, the resulting error in thermodynamic quantities is small.

**Method.** They employ an effective field theory approach combined with the high-temperature expansion to reduce the path integral to a bosonic zero-mode integral. The sign problem is diagnosed by analyzing the expectation value of the Pfaffian phase $\langle e^{i	heta} angle$.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on the D0-brane/BFSS matrix quantum mechanics, which is generalized to higher-dimensional maximally supersymmetric Yang-Mills (SYM) theories at finite temperature.

**Key observables.** The expectation value $\langle \cos 	heta angle$ of the Pfaffian phase operator ($	ext{Pf}(M)$), and thermodynamic quantities like Free Energy $F(T)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** High temperatures ($T$), large-$N$ limit ('t Hooft limit), coupling constant $\lambda$, and inverse temperature $eta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies heavily on the high-temperature expansion, integrating out massive Matsubara modes. The perturbative approach is assumed to be valid up to certain orders.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate planar Wick contractions for correlators contributing to $\langle \cos 	heta angle$ in the replica expansion and show Monte Carlo results benchmarking analytic calculations of $\langle(T_{10})^2angle$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the sign problem using effective field theory, deriving scaling laws in the large-$N$ limit via multiple techniques (replica method, large-D expansion), and calculating subleading temperature corrections through symmetry analysis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The sign problem is a notorious obstacle for classically simulating quantum theories with fermions. We propose an effective field theory method for analyzing the sign problem. At high temperatures, a $d$+1 dimensional field theory reduces to a bosonic $d$-dimensional theory; the phase of the Pfaffian in the higher dimensional theory is encoded in an operator in the lower dimensional theory. We apply this framework to the D0-brane/BFSS matrix quantum mechanics, where the phase becomes an operator in a bosonic multi-matrix integral. Our results show that the continuum theory has a sign problem that persists in the large-$N$ 't Hooft regime. However, detecting the sign problem involves going to 10-loop order in the high-temperature expansion. This delayed onset follows from the fact that the Pfaffian phase transforms as an $O(9)$ pseudoscalar. Furthermore, the relevant diagrams give a numerically small prefactor. Consequently, ignoring the sign problem leads to a relatively small fractional error in thermodynamic quantities for temperatures $T \gtrsim λ^{1/3}$. However, at stronger coupling in the 't Hooft regime, the sign problem may become more severe. Finally, we initiate the application of this framework to higher-dimensional maximally supersymmetric Yang-Mills theories.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously analyzes how entanglement distribution schemes affect the overall 'resourcefulness' of quantum networks. It demonstrates that utilizing multi-path entanglement purification (MPEP) significantly boosts average teleportation fidelity compared to simpler methods. The findings provide critical guidelines for designing robust quantum communication channels across various network topologies.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed resource assessment framework for quantum networks, directly impacting the design principles for long-distance quantum communication infrastructure by quantifying the benefit of advanced protocols like MPEP over simple repeater chains.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is quantifying the 'resourcefulness' of quantum networks by comparing entanglement distribution schemes, specifically single-path swapping versus multi-path entanglement purification.

**Main result.** Multi-path entanglement purification (MPEP) significantly enhances the average teleportation fidelity ($	ext{F}_{	ext{tel}}^{	ext{avg}}$) compared to using only the best available path, and the order of purification matters due to non-associativity.

**Method.** The authors analyze network resourcefulness by calculating $	ext{F}_{	ext{tel}}^{	ext{avg}}$, which is averaged over all source-target pairs. They compare protocols based on entanglement swapping versus sequential entanglement purification using multiple paths.

**Model / system.** The study models quantum networks (QN) with various topologies, including regular lattices (Ring, Square, Triangular) and random graphs (Erdős-Rényi). Communication relies on shared entangled states distributed over physical links.

**Key observables.** Average teleportation fidelity ($	ext{F}_{	ext{tel}}^{	ext{avg}}$), which quantifies the network's overall quantum communication capability.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Visibility parameter ($p$), number of nodes ($N$), edge usage constraints ($k=1$ vs $k=	ext{kmax}$), and the order/strategy of purification (SPF vs SPL).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes homogeneous networks with isotropic edges, although generalizations to irregular graphs are performed. The superiority of the Shortest Path Last (SPL) strategy over Shortest Path First (SPF) is a key theoretical result.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate purification procedures for various lattices (RN, CGN, TLN), showing how $	ext{F}_{	ext{tel}}^{	ext{avg}}$ changes as a function of entanglement visibility ($p$) and the number of paths/purification steps used. Saturation behavior is also shown.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes network resourcefulness by first defining the metric ($	ext{F}_{	ext{tel}}^{	ext{avg}}$), then comparing single-path vs. multi-path strategies, deriving theoretical results for optimal purification ordering (SPL > SPF), and finally applying these findings to various lattice topologies under different edge usage constraints.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Complex quantum networks can support a diverse set of long-range entanglement distribution schemes ranging from linear repeater protocols to multipath entanglement purification strategies. As a result, a network's resourcefulness, that is its ability to facilitate quantum communication, depends on the deployed distribution scheme. In this work, we analyse and compare the resourcefulness of quantum networks across a broad range of network topologies, including both regular and random networks, under two distinct entanglement distribution schemes. The first relies on entanglement swapping along a single path connecting a source-target pair, while the second exploits entanglement purification using multiple paths between the same source and target nodes. The resourcefulness of the network is quantified using a recently described metric [1] that averages over the maximum teleportation fidelity between arbitrary source-target pairs in the network. We present algorithms for estimating this metric under constraints of edge-usage and ordering of paths. Our results not only demonstrate the sensitivity of the average maximum teleportation fidelity metric to the choice of entanglement distribution protocol, but also highlight the significant improvements enabled by network purification schemes. In particular, purification-based approaches can substantially enhance average teleportation fidelity, thereby improving the overall teleportation capability of quantum networks.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper demonstrates that an adaptive variational quantum algorithm, TETRIS-ADAPT-VQE, can accurately simulate the ground states of complex random Hamiltonians like the SK and SYK models. While achieving high fidelity, the authors find that preparation efficiency is limited by the inherent volume-law entanglement in these systems, suggesting a fundamental challenge for current quantum simulation techniques.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the computational limits of simulating highly entangled quantum many-body systems using near-term quantum hardware algorithms like VQE variants. The comparison between SK and SYK provides crucial benchmarks for assessing algorithmic scalability in disordered physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is efficiently preparing the ground state of random, all-to-all interacting Hamiltonians, which exhibit volume-law entanglement making them hard for classical tensor network methods.

**Main result.** TETRIS-ADAPT-VQE successfully prepares high-fidelity ground states for both quantum SK and SYK models up to moderate sizes, though the preparation proves inefficient for dense or moderately sparse SYK systems due to inherent entanglement structure.

**Method.** The authors employ an advanced variational quantum algorithm, ADAPT-VQE, which adaptively builds a quantum circuit ansatz by selecting operators based on their local gradient contribution to the Hamiltonian expectation value.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on random Hamiltonians, specifically the Quantum Sherrington-Kirkpatrick (SK) model and the Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev (SYK) model. These models feature all-to-all interactions and are used to probe quantum simulation limits.

**Key observables.** Fidelity ($\ge 99.3\%$ for SYK, $\ge 99.9998\%$ for SK), ground-state energy error ($\delta$), circuit depth/CNOT count, and bipartite von Neumann entanglement entropy (SEE).

**Important parameters / regimes.** System sizes ($L=18$ for SK; $N=20$ for SYK), transverse field strength ($\Gamma$), and Hamiltonian sparsity parameter ($k_s$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the difficulty in simulation scales with entanglement structure (volume-law) rather than just the explicit number of non-zero terms in the Hamiltonian.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic representations of SK and SYK models, scaling plots showing resource requirements vs. system size, and comparisons between ADAPT performance for different disorder realizations.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem (hard ground states), details the ADAPT-VQE methodology, applies it to the SK model (showing efficiency gains) and then compares its performance on dense versus sparse SYK models, concluding that entanglement structure dictates difficulty over Hamiltonian sparsity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The ground state of random Hamiltonians with all-to-all interactions such as the quantum Sherrington-Kirkpatrick (SK) model and the Sachdev-Ye-Kitaev (SYK) model follow volume-law entanglement and are expected to be hard to model using tensor networks. In recent years, some progress has been made to push the limit of classical methods using neural quantum states. However, it remains an open question whether there exist quantum algorithms that could offer a quantum advantage over the state-of-the-art classical methods in simulating random Hamiltonians. In this work, we show that one such algorithm, TETRIS-ADAPT-VQE, can construct accurate ground states for dense and sparse SYK models containing up to $N=20$ Majorana fermions achieving fidelities $\geq 99.3\%$ and for the quantum SK model with up to $L=18$ sites achieving fidelities $\geq 99.9998\%$. We find that while the preparation of ground states is efficient (in terms of operator pool size and circuit depth) for the SK model, it is not efficient for either dense or moderately sparse SYK models.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates whether the statistical complexity of Variational Quantum Circuits (VQCs) dictates their trainability. By analyzing structured circuits on Ising and Toric Code models, the authors show that standard randomness diagnostics can appear at shallow depths without causing barren plateaus. This suggests a fundamental separation between local structural complexity and global optimization landscape properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant because it provides theoretical guidance on designing quantum algorithms, suggesting that simply maximizing circuit expressivity or achieving high 'randomness' in state preparation might not be necessary—or even desirable—for successful variational optimization.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to separate whether statistical signatures of complexity (like randomness) in Variational Quantum Circuits (VQCs) are inherently linked to the practical trainability of these circuits.

**Main result.** Statistical diagnostics indicating random-state behavior can emerge at shallow circuit depths without causing exponential gradient suppression, suggesting a separation between structural complexity and optimization landscape properties.

**Method.** The authors analyze VQCs applied to structured Hamiltonians using multiple diagnostic tools: Porter-Thomas statistics, entanglement spectrum analysis (adjacent-gap ratio), and Inverse Participation Ratio (IPR).

**Model / system.** The study uses the one-dimensional cluster-Ising model and a generalized toric code Hamiltonian on lattice geometries. The circuits are structured using specific local building blocks (Cartan block) within various depth schedules (FDC, FLDC, GLDC).

**Key observables.** Porter-Thomas KS distance, entanglement-spectrum adjacent-gap ratio ($\langle r angle$), Inverse Participation Ratio ($	ext{IPR}$), and gradient variance/norm ($	ext{Var}(\partial E / \partial 	heta)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Circuit depth ($D$), system size ($N$), Hamiltonian parameters ($J, J', h$, etc.), and the comparison between optimized states vs. random-parameter ensembles.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to finite-depth circuits and finite system sizes; the locality argument relies on the underlying Hamiltonian being local.

**Figures summary.** Figures show VQE results (energy/error) for both models across different depths, alongside diagnostic plots tracking PT distance, $\langle r angle$, and $\langle IPR angle$ versus depth, demonstrating convergence toward random benchmarks while optimization remains accurate.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares the evolution of multiple complexity diagnostics ($	ext{PT}$, $\langle r angle$, $	ext{IPR}$) as circuit depth increases for two different physical models. It contrasts these statistical findings with direct measurements of VQE performance (energy and gradient variance) to argue for a decoupling between the measures.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Variational quantum algorithms (VQAs) are among the leading approaches for near-term quantum computing, yet their performance can degrade in barren plateau regimes characterized by vanishing gradients. A widely held intuition is that increasing circuit expressivity, often associated with random-state behavior, leads to a loss of trainability. Existing results show that sufficiently random circuits can lead to barren plateaus. Here we show that standard statistical signatures of randomness can emerge well before this regime, without degrading trainability. We demonstrate this behavior in structured variational circuits applied to the one-dimensional cluster-Ising model and a generalized toric code Hamiltonian. To characterize state complexity, we analyze Porter-Thomas statistics, entanglement-spectrum level statistics, and inverse participation ratios. Across both models, increasing circuit depth drives these diagnostics toward random-state-like or random-matrix-like behavior, while variational optimization remains effective, with no evidence of exponential gradient suppression in the regime studied. We interpret this behavior in terms of locality. Spectral correlations develop at relatively shallow depth through locally generated structure, while global state randomization and the associated concentration-of-measure effects are not yet realized. These results show that commonly used statistical diagnostics of complexity do not by themselves determine trainability. Instead, they point to a separation between different aspects of complexity in finite-depth variational circuits.

</details>
