**Summary.** The paper introduces enhanced phase sensing in three-level Ramsey interferometers by utilizing a noncyclic geometric phase response. By analyzing the interference between two internal paths, researchers derive a mechanism that sharply amplifies the readout phase near specific operating points. This allows for a projected improvement in measurement stability compared to standard methods, provided external technical noise is present.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, theoretically derived method for engineering quantum sensors by manipulating geometric phase accumulation, offering a pathway to surpass standard metrological limits in atomic systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to enhance phase sensing sensitivity beyond standard linear accumulation by exploiting a noncyclic geometric phase response in Ramsey interferometry.

**Main result.** A net Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR) gain can be achieved only within a finite operating window where the enhanced local phase slope compensates for visibility reduction and technical noise. This allows for a 'geometric shortcut' to improved projected stability via faster sampling cycles.

**Method.** The analysis models the three-level system interference, deriving effective fringe visibility ($V_s$) and readout phase ($\Phi$). Sensitivity is quantified by analyzing the local phase-response gain versus noise penalties.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a three-level Ramsey interferometer. The mechanism relies on the interference of amplitudes from two signal-collecting internal paths onto a shared readout channel, generating a noncyclic geometric phase jump near a geodesic-closure transition.

**Key observables.** Readout phase $\Phi(\phi)$, projected visibility $V_s(\phi)$, inferred signal-phase uncertainty ($\Delta\phi$), and the net SNR enhancement factor ($E$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pathway imbalance ($\epsilon = A_1 - A_2$), technical noise level ($\xi_{CLA}$), and the initial Ramsey phase offset ($\phi_{	ext{off}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The technique's utility is shown to be most pronounced when additive classical phase noise ($\xi_{CLA}$) is present, as this external noise source can be leveraged by the geometric enhancement.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the gain–visibility tradeoff (showing $G_{max}^N$ vs. $V_{min}$), map the finite SNR enhancement window, and compare projected fractional instability for standard versus shortcut protocols.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from deriving the effective fringe structure ($P_s(t)$) to quantifying the local phase-response gain ($G_N$). It then analyzes the trade-off between this gain and visibility loss. Finally, it applies these findings to predict a measurable improvement in clock stability using an offset-controlled 'shortcut protocol'.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In a standard two-level Ramsey interferometer, the measured phase accumulates linearly during the interrogation time. Here, we introduce three-level Ramsey interferometry that employs a noncyclic geometric phase response to enhance phase sensing, with projected internal-path interference reshaping the mapping from accumulated signal phase to readout phase. Near a geodesic-closure transition, a small accumulated signal phase produces a sharply amplified readout-phase shift. We quantify the accompanying gain--visibility tradeoff and identify a finite operating window in which the amplified response yields a net signal-to-noise-ratio gain under technical-noise-limited conditions. By tuning an initial Ramsey phase offset, this high-slope window can be positioned at a desired operating point and sampled repeatedly with shorter cycles, providing a geometric shortcut to improved projected stability. More broadly, these results establish a multilevel Ramsey route to enhanced phase sensitivity in quantum platforms, where two signal-collecting internal paths interfere to produce a noncyclic geometric response.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously characterizes three-qubit controlled unitary gates based on their operator Schmidt rank. It establishes precise mathematical conditions determining when these gates have $	ext{sr}=3$, linking this structural property to the resulting state's entanglement structure and minimal circuit implementation using CNOT gates.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed characterization of gate complexity via Schmidt rank provides fundamental theoretical bounds on quantum circuit design, which is crucial for understanding resource efficiency in quantum computation protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is characterizing three-qubit controlled unitary gates by establishing necessary and sufficient conditions for them to possess a Schmidt rank of exactly three.

**Main result.** The authors derive explicit mathematical conditions for $	ext{sr}(U)=3$ for various gate forms ($U_1, U_2$), showing that this structural property relates to the linear independence of component operators.

**Method.** The work employs advanced linear algebra techniques, analyzing the span and linear dependence of operator components (like Pauli matrices) derived from the unitary structure. It also analyzes state-level entanglement using measures like the tripartite tangle.

**Model / system.** The system under study is a three-qubit quantum processor implementing controlled unitary gates ($U$). The analysis focuses on the mathematical properties of these unitaries, rather than simulating physical hardware.

**Key observables.** Schmidt rank ($	ext{sr}(U)$), bipartite and tripartite entanglement measures (tangles $	au$), and circuit complexity (minimum CNOT count).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Phase parameters ($\gamma, \delta$) governing the gate structure, and structural coefficients ($t_0, t_1, t_2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on simplifying assumptions regarding local unitary invariance to reduce general forms into manageable structures ($U_1, U_2$), and assumes specific linear independence conditions for certain operator sets.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate quantum circuits implementing specific gates ($U_3, U_8, U_{11}, U_5$) and demonstrate the relationship between gate structure and entanglement properties.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first establishing general structural forms for controlled unitaries. It then analyzes these forms case-by-case using linear algebra to derive necessary and sufficient conditions for $	ext{sr}=3$. Finally, it connects this operator-level classification to state-level entanglement capabilities.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We characterize three-qubit controlled unitary gates of Schmidt rank three, establishing necessary and sufficient conditions for such gates to have Schmidt rank three. We construct explicit examples and analyze their entanglement capabilities, showing that gates with Schmidt rank three can generate output states ranging from fully separable to maximal GHZ-class states. Within this classification, we present a parameterized gate family that produces an output W-class state whose bipartite tangles for subsystems AB and AC are modulated as simple trigonometric functions of a single phase parameter. The examples also include gates implementable with only three CNOT gates, showing that this lower bound is achievable. Decompositions that achieve the minimum possible CNOT count for several other cases are provided as well. Our results bridge Schmidt rank classification, entanglement structure, and resource-efficient circuit synthesis.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes the Frustrated Synchronization Network (FSN), an attention mechanism that reinterprets context processing as frustrated synchronization among coupled oscillators. By replacing standard consensus mechanisms with dynamics incorporating data-driven frustration and delays, the FSN achieves state-of-the-art performance on character-level text and code benchmarks compared to transformers.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit mapping of complex neural network dynamics onto established concepts from coupled oscillator theory (Kuramoto models) provides a novel physical interpretation for sequence modeling failures, particularly concerning long-range dependencies.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses how sequence prediction can be improved by modifying attention mechanisms from consensus-based retrieval to a process modeled by frustrated synchronization.

**Main result.** The proposed Frustrated Synchronization Network (FSN) consistently outperforms tuned transformer baselines on character-level text and code corpora, especially when analyzing performance on long-range copy events.

**Method.** It introduces the FSN, which models token state updates using a complex coupling kernel derived from coupled oscillators on a torus, incorporating frustration angles and data-dependent delays.

**Model / system.** The core model is the Frustrated Synchronization Network (FSN), an oscillator-based attention layer replacing standard self-attention. It uses phases on a torus ($\mathbb{T}^k$) and models coupling via Kuramoto–Sakaguchi-like dynamics, where prediction is framed as synchronization frustrated by the data's transition.

**Key observables.** Validation loss (bits per character) on enwik8 and code corpora; performance margins analyzed across 'copy depth' bins ($\ell$); convergence rates over epochs.

**Important parameters / regimes.** One-million to eight-million parameters; copy depth $\ell$; frustration angles derived from data transitions ($\delta_u$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the failure of standard attention on long repetitions is due to its inability to capture non-periodic, accumulated 'winding information' present in the raw phase state.

**Figures summary.** Figures show per-token cross-entropy margins vs. copy depth bins, demonstrating the FSN's advantage where base layers fail (deep bins $\ge 4$). Other figures track loss vs. training time across parameter scales.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first defining the physical analogy of attention as synchronization, then introducing the FSN kernel structure (including harmonics and delay terms), presenting quantitative results showing superior performance over transformers on convergence and deep-copy tasks, and finally analyzing efficiency scaling with model size.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A network of oscillators that synchronizes perfectly computes nothing further, so an attention architecture built from synchronization must locate its computation in structured departures from agreement. We introduce the Frustrated Synchronization Network (FSN), whose token states are phases on a torus and whose entire value pathway is one learned complex coupling kernel over harmonics and a one-step delay. Each component of the kernel is a frustration in the sense of the synchronization literature. The complex phases are static Kuramoto-Sakaguchi frustration angles, the signed harmonics are repulsive Daido components, and the delay term, which couples each token to the successors of the tokens it attends to, is algebraically identical to Kuramoto-Sakaguchi coupling whose frustration angle is the data's own transition, so next-token prediction is implemented as synchronization frustrated by the data. At matched one-million-parameter and training budgets on character-level text and code, the FSN's validation loss is below a tuned RoPE-SwiGLU transformer's at every epoch measured, and the comparison survives training the baseline to convergence: every thirty-epoch enwik8 seed finishes below the transformer's converged fifty-epoch loss of 1.611, and the FSN's completed fifty-epoch runs converge to 1.5953 +/- 0.0014. A variant with every feed-forward block replaced by mean-field coupling to learned collective modes, leaving no multilayer perceptron in the stack, tracks the transformer. On natural text the unfrustrated base layer falls behind the converged transformer at every copy depth, worst on long-range copy events; the kernel reverses the deficit at every depth of four and beyond. Headline comparisons are at the one-million-parameter scale; a scale ladder is complete through four million parameters with the advantage persisting, and remaining arms are marked as in progress.

</details>

**Summary.** This work explores how hard sphere packings change when confined in slightly deformed elliptic cylinders compared to perfect circles. By combining theory, simulation, and experiment, the authors show that even minor anisotropy can induce entirely new, complex helical phases. This provides crucial insights for designing materials and understanding biological structures like those found in zebrafish.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical packing, the study of phase transitions driven by subtle geometric changes (anisotropy) and the emergence of complex, ordered states from simple constraints is highly relevant to understanding order-disorder transitions in soft matter systems studied in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how chiral packing structures of hard spheres change when confined within non-circular (elliptic) cylinders compared to ideal circular ones.

**Main result.** Weak cross-sectional deformation triggers entirely new phases, including double oscillated-chain phases, revealing the significant role of cylindrical anisotropy in determining dense packings.

**Method.** The research combines theoretical development for helical phases with extensive simulations (Monte Carlo) and macroscopic experiments using table-tennis balls.

**Model / system.** The system models hard spheres packed inside elliptic cylinders defined by a minor axis $D_b$ and an aspect ratio $D_a/D_b$. The analysis focuses on finding the densest possible packing structure under these anisotropic boundary conditions.

**Key observables.** Packing fraction ($\phi$), global chirality, and identification of specific helical phases (e.g., Zigzag, Helical Phase I/II, Double Oscillated-chain).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cylinder anisotropy parameters ($D_b \in [1.60, 2.00]$, $D_a/D_b \in [1.00, 1.10]$) and the number of spheres ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theoretical methods rely on constructing structures using specific geometric constraints (e.g., triplet-sphere packing block method), which are approximations compared to infinite systems.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams showing eight distinct regions of densest packing structures across varying anisotropy parameters, alongside experimental snapshots confirming these predicted phases.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the baseline in circular cylinders, then systematically introducing elliptic deformation. It develops a new theory for helical phases and validates this theory through simulations (MC) and finally confirms the predicted structures using physical experiments.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Sphere packings in circular cylinders have attracted substantial research interest, among which the discovery of chiral helical structures is the most iconic. However, recent experimental results on zebrafish do not match the known packing structures in circular cylinders. To account for the inherent imperfections of biological tubes, we take elliptic cylinders as the canonical deformation of circular cylinders and investigate the densest packings of hard spheres in them using simulation, theory, and experiments. Starting from the chiral structures in circular cylinders, we demonstrate that even a weak cross-sectional deformation can trigger entirely new phases, including ones that either eliminate global chirality or significantly complicate the chiral structures. This reveals the significant effect of cylindrical anisotropy. The new helical phases under anisotropic confinement remain chiral and develop hierarchical periodic structures, which are difficult to obtain by simulations but are predicted by our newly developed theory for helical phases in elliptic cylinders. The theory also predicts double oscillated-chain phases without chirality, which perfectly match the simulations. Our work offers fresh insights into understanding packings in anisotropic cylinders, which will help researchers to design new materials and to understand many living systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the challenge of simulating entanglement generation in noisy quantum networks using the BK protocol. The authors derive analytical formulas to calculate success probability and fidelity under various noise sources, integrating these into a significantly faster simulator. This advancement enhances the field's ability to design robust, large-scale quantum internet infrastructure.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the engineering challenges of scaling quantum communication networks by providing validated simulation tools that account for realistic physical imperfections beyond simple loss models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing efficient and realistic simulation tools for entanglement distribution in quantum networks, which is crucial for realizing the quantum internet.

**Main result.** The authors developed an upgraded simulator that is significantly faster (over 60% reduction in computation time) while accurately modeling multiple noise sources like imperfect mode matching, dark counts, and channel loss. The simulation successfully benchmarks against real experimental data.

**Method.** They derived generalized analytical expressions for key metrics—success probability, output state fidelity, and entanglement generation time—for the Barrett-Kok (BK) protocol under various noise models. These analytics were then integrated into an upgraded quantum network simulator (SeQUeNCe).

**Model / system.** The work focuses on simulating the probabilistic Bell state generation using the BK protocol between two spatially separated quantum memories, incorporating realistic imperfections such as detector efficiency ($\eta_d$), channel loss ($\eta$), and initial state deviations.

**Key observables.** Success probability, output state fidelity ($F$), average entanglement generation time, and final state fidelity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise parameters include memory/detector efficiencies ($\eta_m, \eta_d$), channel transmittance ($\eta$), polarization mismatch angle ($	heta$), dark count probability ($p_d$), and initial state preparation errors ($\alpha, eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often simplifies by assuming certain error sources are negligible or zeroed out (e.g., $	heta = \phi = \alpha = eta = p_d = 0$) to derive core analytical results, though the final simulator handles multiple sources.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare average entanglement time vs. loss and computation time scaling; other figures illustrate how fidelity and attempts change as a function of indistinguishability ($I$) or dark count probability ($p_d$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from deriving analytical results for the BK protocol under various noise sources, upgrading an existing simulator (SeQUeNCe) to incorporate these analytics for speed, and finally benchmarking this improved tool against real experimental data.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

End-to-end entanglement distribution is a key capability of upcoming quantum networks, enabling applications like distributed quantum computing, quantum sensor networks, and secure communications. Hence, its realistic and efficient simulation is crucial for quantum network design and for assessing the ability of a network to run certain applications. This work provides tools to scale-up and improve the realism of entanglement generation simulations in quantum networks. This is achieved by deriving analytical results that directly return the success probability, the output state and corresponding fidelity of a selected entanglement generation protocol, while accounting for a variety of noise sources affecting the protocol. These results are then integrated and streamlined in an upgraded version of SeQUeNCe, one of the most popular quantum network simulators. The resulting simulator features increased scalability by reducing computation time by more than 60%, while allowing for a variety of realistic noise sources, including imperfect mode matching, dark counts, and imperfect memory initialization. The simulator is also benchmarked with real experimental data and is capable of replicating the average entanglement generation time and the final state fidelity of a selected experiment. Altogether, the results can enhance current quantum network simulation capabilities towards large-scale networks, paving the way for the future quantum internet.

</details>

Away from 𝜆= 0, the error is reduced for larger 𝑛. Notice that the real error is much less than the general error bound, Eq. (18), with 𝛾= (𝑁𝑥𝜆)1/3. As mentioned above, the specifics of the circuit can reduce the error. In this case, there are two major factors that reduce the error. Firstly, applying a single flip cannot result in the final measurement 𝐹= 00000, and therefore there is nearly zero first order error. Secondly, as there...</figcaption>

**Summary.** This work introduces a scalable technique called 'quantum circuit knitting' to perform distributed quantum computations. By assuming weak coupling across circuit cuts, the classical resources required scale polynomially rather than exponentially. This allows for simulating large, complex quantum circuits by decomposing them into manageable, weakly interacting sub-problems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on computation, the rigorous mathematical framework developed for bounding error propagation ($\epsilon_{	ext{total}}$) through weak coupling connections is highly relevant to open quantum systems where system-environment interactions are modeled perturbatively.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of performing Distributed Quantum Computing (DQC) by reducing the classical information cost associated with circuit knitting.

**Main result.** A method is presented that reduces the classical resource requirement from exponential to polynomial scaling by exploiting weak coupling approximations across circuit cuts.

**Method.** It uses a weak-coupling approximation, modeling the quantum process as a partition between two sub-circuits connected by a single weakly coupled qubit. This allows for the derivation of transformation matrices ($\Gamma$) relating measurement probabilities.

**Model / system.** The model involves general quantum circuits (e.g., those used in QAOA) that are conceptually cut along a central, weakly coupled qubit line. The analysis tracks probability evolution using symbolic string representations ($\alpha$-type and $\sigma$-type strings).

**Key observables.** Approximate full-circuit probability ($P_n^F$), total error ($\epsilon_{	ext{total}}$), and the condition number of the reconstruction matrix ($\kappa_\infty(\Gamma_n)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling strength ($\lambda$) and approximation parameter ($\gamma$); the approximation level $n$ dictates the polynomial scaling of resources.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that the coupling between the two main parts of the process via the cut qubit is weak, allowing for Taylor-like expansions in $\lambda$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate quantum circuits cut along a single qubit line showing input/output operations; another figure depicts a simulated 5-qubit register circuit with layers of gates.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops from the weak-coupling expansion leading to commutator relations, details the positional-symbolic correspondence (PSC) for maintaining consistency between string types, and culminates in error bounding formulas that analyze the total error as a combination of approximation, hardware noise, and statistical errors.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a method for performing distributed quantum computing with controlled approximations. Exact distributed quantum computing requires exponential classical information to reconstruct the quantum process. However, we show how the classical cost is reduced to polynomial if the quantum procedure can be partitioned between a qubit that is weakly coupled the other qubits. We demonstrate our method for a layered circuit based on the circuits used for the quantum approximate optimization algorithm.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces the first receiver-centric framework for covert communication over noisy bosonic channels. It exploits the fundamental difference in how an eavesdropper (Willie) and a legitimate receiver (Bob) detect signals—quadratic versus linear distinguishability—to derive optimal signaling strategies. The result provides explicit design laws linking channel parameters, security budgets, and required detection segment lengths for quantum optical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete bridge between abstract information-theoretic security guarantees (covertness) and practical quantum optical engineering design rules (receiver optimization, signal processing).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a receiver-centric framework for covert quantum communication over thermal-loss bosonic channels, aiming to maximize detection probability while satisfying an adversary's covertness constraint.

**Main result.** It derives an explicit minimum detection-segment length law that balances the linear growth of Bob's detectability against Willie's quadratic detectability using a CUSUM detector.

**Method.** The authors use small-signal approximations to analyze distinguishability measures (KL divergence) and optimize energy allocation subject to covertness constraints, leading to concrete design rules for blockwise coding.

**Model / system.** The system is quantum communication over thermal-loss bosonic channels. Detection relies on a physically realizable Gaussian receiver followed by a single-pass Cumulative Sum (CUSUM) detector.

**Key observables.** Bob's post-change information growth (linear in signal energy, $D_B \sim E$), Willie's detectability via Quantum Relative Entropy (quadratic in signal energy, $D_W \sim E^2$), and the CUSUM crossing probability.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Thermal loss ($\eta$), background brightness ($ar{n}_B$), per-block covert budget ($\delta_{	ext{block}}$), and detection segment length ($L_{	ext{signal}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies heavily on small-signal regime approximations for the distinguishability measures, and assumes a Gaussian receiver readout.

**Figures summary.** Figures validate the small-signal asymmetry (Willie quadratic vs. Bob linear) and confirm that the empirical same-block crossing probability scales correctly with a predicted characteristic length scale derived from system parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first establishing the physical model, deriving the asymptotic distinguishability laws for both parties, optimizing energy allocation under the covertness constraint (uniform allocation), and finally deriving the explicit minimum detection segment length required for reliable same-block detection using CUSUM theory.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop, to our knowledge, the first receiver-centric blockwise sequential-detection framework for covert communication over thermal-loss bosonic channels. In this architecture, each block serves as a binary super-symbol, and the key design problem is to determine the minimum detection-segment length that enables Bob to detect an active block before the block ends while remaining covert to Willie. For any fixed physically realizable general-dyne receiver, Bob's post-change information growth is linear in the small-signal regime, whereas Willie's detectability obeys a quadratic quantum relative entropy law. Exploiting this asymmetry, we show that under a per-block covertness budget the asymptotically optimal signaling strategy is uniform across the detection segment, and we derive an explicit minimum-length condition under which a single-pass cumulative sum (CUSUM) detector crosses threshold within the same block with exponentially high probability. The resulting design law yields a covert blockwise binary codebook over a finite transmission horizon and establishes a concrete link between bosonic covert communication, sequential detection, and blockwise signaling design. More broadly, these results provide design guidance for covert quantum communication systems with physically realizable receivers, and help bridge information-theoretic covertness guarantees with implementable receiver-aware optical communication design.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates how structural randomness in silicon oxide interfaces broadens X-ray photoelectron spectra. By combining MD simulations with DFT calculations, the authors show that spectral broadening results not from multiple distinct chemical states, but from a continuous statistical distribution of binding energies dictated by local atomic arrangements.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state spectroscopy, the methodology of linking macroscopic spectroscopic features to underlying statistical distributions arising from quenched disorder is highly relevant to understanding complex many-body systems where local environments dictate global properties.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study challenges conventional chemical state assignments in oxide X-ray photoelectron spectra by demonstrating that core-level binding energies arise from a continuous distribution governed by local structural variation.

**Main result.** Statistical simulations successfully reproduce the observed spectral broadening, showing that intermediate stoichiometry regions exhibit wide energy spreads rather than distinct, discrete spectral fingerprints.

**Method.** The research combines Density Functional Theory (DFT) calculations with Molecular Dynamics (MD) simulations to generate ensemble averages of core-level binding energies from amorphous silicon oxide structures.

**Model / system.** The system under investigation is the $	ext{SiOx}$ interface, modeled using supercells derived from MD quench simulations spanning varying oxygen concentrations ($x$). The analysis focuses on how local structural disorder influences electronic properties.

**Key observables.** Core-level binding energies ($	ext{O } 1s$ and $	ext{Si } 2s$), spectral broadening (up to $5 	ext{ eV}$), depth profiles reconstructed from sputtering, and structural descriptors like the void distribution curve.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis is sensitive to local structure variations over a radius of approximately $5 	ext{ Å}$, and simulations cover compositions from pure Si to $	ext{SiO}_2$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include treating the observed spectrum as an integral over underlying layers (sputtering model) and assuming constant ionization cross-sections for DFT calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate conceptual XPS build-up, layer-resolved spectra comparisons with simulations, correlation plots between BE shifts and structural descriptors ($D(R)$), and void size distributions for $	ext{SiO}_2$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the experimental challenge (XPS interpretation in disordered oxides), developing simulation tools (DFT/MD), quantifying the structural disorder using descriptors, and finally demonstrating that this continuous variation explains the observed spectral broadening.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that the broad X-ray photoelectron lines of silicon oxide on silicon arise from a continuous statistical distribution of core-level binding energies. Statistical simulations spanning compositions from Si to SiO$_2$ reproduce the full extent of this broadening, reaching 5 eV for SiO$_{1.0}$ , in quantitative agreement with 0.23 nm layer-resolved spectra reconstructed from Ar$^+$ sputtering data. This continuous distribution blurs distinct spectral fingerprints of local structural motifs, thereby challenging conventional chemical state assignment in oxide X-ray photoelectron spectra.

</details>

**Summary.** The research uses advanced Quantum Monte Carlo methods to study how tensile strain affects electron correlations in graphene. By comparing standard and Resonating Valence Bond (RVB) wave functions, the authors find that stretching the lattice enhances correlation effects up to a critical strain ($\delta_{cr}$). This demonstrates a tunable pathway to stabilize highly correlated electronic states in Dirac materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides direct, quantitative evidence that external mechanical tuning (strain) can be used as a simple knob to tune fundamental electronic interactions in Dirac materials, potentially stabilizing exotic correlated phases like those described by RVB theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine how tensile strain modifies electronic correlations in graphene, potentially driving a transition into a regime dominated by Resonating Valence Bond (RVB) physics.

**Main result.** Tensile expansion enhances RVB-like singlet correlations up to a critical strain ($\delta_{cr} \approx 15\% - 20\%$), where the energy gain of the RVB ansatz peaks, indicating a nonmonotonic evolution of correlation strength.

**Method.** Variational and Diffusion Quantum Monte Carlo (VMC/DMC) simulations are used to calculate ground state energies by comparing two wave function ansätze: a standard Jastrow-Slater determinant and an RVB-based Geminal Product ansatz.

**Model / system.** The system is graphene, modeled on its honeycomb lattice. The study investigates the effect of biaxial tensile strain ($\delta$) on the electronic correlation energy, treating electron interactions beyond simple mean-field approximations.

**Key observables.** Energy gain ($\Delta E = E_{	ext{RVB}} - E_{	ext{JSD}}$) as a function of strain $\delta$; band structure renormalization (pDOS); total ground state energy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Tensile strain ($\delta$); Critical Strain ($\delta_{cr}$) in the $15\% < \delta_{cr} < 20\%$ range; System sizes ($N=16, 36, 64$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The use of fixed-node approximation in DMC; The necessity of using an RVB ansatz (JAGP) to capture static correlation effects neglected by single-determinant forms.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the energy gain ($\Delta E$) vs. strain, showing nonmonotonic behavior with a peak/crossover point at $\delta_{cr}$. Tables present calculated total energies using both JSD and JAGP wave functions across various strains.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically calculates ground state properties (energy) for graphene under increasing biaxial strain ($\delta$) by comparing the results from two increasingly sophisticated quantum many-body wave function ansätze (JSD vs. RVB/AGP), revealing a critical transition point.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Electronic correlations in graphene are generally considered weak due to the large bandwidth of its $π$ electrons. Here we show that tensile expansion of the honeycomb lattice provides a direct route to enhancing correlation effects. Using variational and diffusion quantum Monte Carlo, we compare a conventional Jastrow-Slater determinant wave function with a resonating-valence-bond (RVB) Jastrow-antisymmetrized geminal product ansatz for a series of stretched graphene lattices. We find that the energy gain of the RVB state relative to the single-determinant description increases with bond expansion up to a critical strain $δ_{\mathrm{cr}}$, and decreases beyond it, revealing a nonmonotonic evolution of electronic correlations. The crossover is found to occur in the range $15\% < δ_{\mathrm{cr}} < 20\%$, in agreement with mechanical stability limits. This behavior indicates a transition from a weakly correlated Dirac semimetal to a regime with enhanced non-dynamic correlation and short-range singlet pairing. Our results provide direct many-body evidence that lattice expansion drives graphene into a regime where RVB-like correlations become energetically favorable, offering a simple route to tuning correlation effects in Dirac materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously benchmarks Pauli Correlation Encoding (PCE) as an efficient method to map large combinatorial optimization problems onto small qubit systems. By testing on multiple NP-hard instances and analyzing noise effects, it demonstrates that PCE offers a scalable quantum approach with performance competitive to classical solvers. The work provides critical guidance on parameter tuning required for near-term quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a detailed roadmap for applying quantum computation principles—specifically encoding techniques like PCE—to classically hard optimization problems, offering insights into the necessary parameter regimes for near-term devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work evaluates a quantum-classical optimization framework using Pauli Correlation Encoding (PCE) to solve complex combinatorial optimization problems like Max Cut, Bin Packing, and TSP.

**Main result.** The PCE-based framework achieves competitive performance on benchmark problems, demonstrating that higher compression orders ($k$) improve solution quality by enhancing expressive capacity, even when considering hardware noise effects.

**Method.** Optimization is performed by minimizing a cost function over parameters ($\vec{\Theta}$) applied to a Parametrised Quantum Circuit (PQC), often using gradient-free optimizers like Differential Evolution (DE).

**Model / system.** The model uses Pauli strings ($\Pi$) to encode $m$ binary variables onto a polynomial number ($n$) of qubits, forming the basis for variational quantum circuits with hardware-efficient ansatze.

**Key observables.** Solution ratio (comparing achieved cost to best known/max), feasibility rate of solutions, and quantitative metrics like mean/min row max and gap derived from relaxed variables.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Compression order ($k$), relaxation hyperparameters ($\alpha$, $eta$), penalty weights ($\lambda_i$), number of shots, and noise levels (ideal vs. noisy backend).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The performance heavily relies on careful calibration of numerous hyperparameters; the analysis often assumes a specific ansatz structure and uses post-processing heuristics to refine solutions.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare solution ratios across different compression orders ($k$) for MCP, show scaling of qubits vs. variables for BPP/TSP, and use heatmaps to visualize how penalty parameters affect variable separation metrics (e.g., meangap).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically benchmarks PCE on multiple problems (MCP, BPP, TSP), analyzing the impact of compression order ($k$), noise models, post-processing steps (like 2-opt for TSP), and hyperparameter tuning ($\alpha, eta$) to establish performance boundaries.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The continuous progress of quantum technologies has spurred the exploration of their potential applications across diverse fields, particularly in combinatorial optimisation. In this work, we study a quantum-classical optimisation framework based on Pauli Correlation Encoding, an encoding scheme that can represent m binary variables using a polynomial number of qubits. To evaluate the performance of the method, we use four classical optimisation problems against the instances of the QOPTLib benchmark. The study includes an analysis of the impact of the compression order of the encoding scheme, the problem structure, and hyperparameter selection on solution quality, as well as the role of post-processing in improving performance. Additionally, we study the effect of shot-based execution and hardware noise, showing how these factors influence both the accuracy of expected value estimation and the overall dynamics of the optimisation process. The results indicate that the proposed PCE-based framework achieves competitive performance against the benchmark and, in several cases, obtains equivalent or even superior solutions, highlighting its potential as an efficient encoding strategy for quantum optimisation in the NISQ and near fault-tolerant era.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously proves that the replica overlap in the random field Edwards-Anderson spin glass model exhibits self-averaging in the infinite volume limit. This is achieved by relating order parameters to derivatives of the free energy density and employing sophisticated mathematical inequalities. The result confirms a specific behavior for finite-dimensional disordered systems, distinguishing them from mean-field models.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical spin glasses, the mathematical techniques used for analyzing quenched disorder and replica symmetry breaking are foundational tools in modern statistical physics that can inform theories of quantum disordered systems or complex energy landscapes relevant to AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is proving the self-averaging of the replica overlap in the Edwards-Anderson (EA) spin glass model when subjected to a random external field.

**Main result.** The variance of the replica overlap is proven to vanish in the infinite-volume limit for almost all coupling constants, establishing self-averaging properties for this disordered system.

**Method.** The proof utilizes advanced techniques involving derivatives of the free energy density with respect to the random field strength and relies on correlation inequalities (like Tasaki's) to bound the variance terms.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the Edwards-Anderson (EA) spin glass model, defined by a Hamiltonian incorporating quenched random couplings ($J_{x,y}$) and an external random magnetic field ($h_g$). The analysis is performed in the thermodynamic limit (infinite volume).

**Key observables.** Replica overlap ($R_{\alpha,eta}^L$), Edwards-Anderson (EA) order parameter ($q_{EA}$), and the free energy density ($\varphi_L$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($eta$), random field strength ($h_g$), system dimension ($d$), and the infinite volume limit ($L 	o \infty$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proof requires that the free energy density be independent of boundary conditions for the random field EA model, and it is restricted to short-range interactions.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates a lattice structure ($\Lambda_7$) and its boundary ($\partial\Lambda_7$) in two dimensions ($d=2$).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by relating order parameters to derivatives of the free energy density, proving self-averaging theorems using correlation inequalities, and contrasting these findings with mean-field models like SK.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The self-averaging of the replica overlap is proven in the Edwards-Anderson (EA) model under random field almost everywhere in the coupling constant space in any dimension. The EA order parameter is represented in terms of the derivative of the free energy density with respect to the random field strength, regardless of boundary conditions. Tasaki's correlation inequality for finite-dimensional spin glass models shows that the expectation of the squared replica overlap is bounded by the squared EA order parameter. These simple evaluations enable us to prove that the variance of the replica overlap vanishes in the infinite-volume limit. The self-averaging of the replica bond overlap is proven also in the EA model with Gaussian exchange interaction without random field. Short-range spin glass models have been shown to behave differently from mean-field spin glass models with RSB phase.

</details>

**Summary.** The study reports the observation of a measurable Hall effect for trions—three-particle bound states—in MoSe2/WSe2 heterobilayers. By measuring transport properties under magnetic fields, the authors confirm that these composite particles carry unique charge responses, including an electron-Hall effect in a hole layer. This opens avenues for studying quantum phenomena associated with multi-body correlated phases.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides experimental realization of correlated electron physics beyond simple excitons, exploring three-body bound states (trions) and their unique magneto-transport signatures, which is highly relevant to strongly correlated materials research.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to observe and characterize the Hall effect for trions (three-particle bound states of electrons and holes) in electron-hole double layers.

**Main result.** A distinct Trion Hall effect is observed, including an electron-Hall effect measured within a hole-doped layer due to negative trion drags. This confirms the existence and transport properties of these composite particles.

**Method.** The research combines electrical transport measurements (standard Hall bar and Coulomb drag) with optical spectroscopy (reflection contrast) under perpendicular magnetic fields.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a Coulomb-coupled electron-hole double layer realized in 2D semiconductor heterostructures, specifically MoSe2/WSe2 heterobilayers. The physics involves the formation and transport of excitons and trions within this coupled quantum well structure.

**Key observables.** Hall resistance ($ho_{xy}$), Coulomb drag resistance ($ho_{	ext{drag}}$), longitudinal resistance ($R_{xx}$), and optical resonance shifts (RC spectra).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T \approx 1.5	ext{K}$ to $10	ext{K}$), Magnetic Field ($B$ up to $12	ext{T}$), Gate Voltages ($V_g$, $V_+$), and carrier densities.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed transport phenomena are dominated by the contributions of free carriers, excitons, and trions, which can be modeled using conductivity tensors.

**Figures summary.** Figures detail phase diagrams mapping out regions hosting different charge states (exciton insulator vs. trion fluid) as a function of magnetic field and gate voltage, alongside measurements showing the disappearance of the effect at high T or B.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically presents evidence by first establishing the platform, then measuring transport coefficients ($R_{xx}, R_{xy}$, drag), mapping out phase boundaries in $B$-field/gate-voltage space, and finally confirming the physical mechanism through temperature and density dependence studies.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The realization of Coulomb coupled electron-hole double layers in 2D semiconductor heterostructures has enabled the thermodynamic and transport studies of equilibrium exciton fluids without a magnetic field. By doping the exciton fluid with additional electrons/holes, an equilibrium fluid of trions - three particle bound states of electrons and holes - further emerge, providing the platform to explore new transport phenomena associated with such composite particles. Here we report the observation of a Hall effect for trions in MoSe2/WSe2 heterobilayers, which support Coulomb-coupled electron and hole fluids with tunable densities. The Hall effect arises from a Lorentz force on trions under a perpendicular magnetic field. It is manifested in both Hall drag measurements and standard Hall effect measurements on just one of the semiconductor layers. For negatively charged trions, an electron Hall effect is observed even in a hole doped WSe2 monolayer due to the presence of trion drags. The trion Hall effect also disappears when the trions are ionized at elevated temperatures and/or high trion densities. Our work opens the door for realizing quantum oscillations and the quantum Hall effect for trions.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces two-photon two-electron coincidence ARPES ($2\gamma 	o 2e$ $2e$ARPES) as a superior technique for studying electronic pairing in correlated materials. Theoretically, it shows that this method yields an unambiguous energy separation between photoemission signals originating from electrons within the same pair versus those from different pairs. This allows for model-independent identification of superconducting signatures directly from the coincidence spectrum.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful spectroscopic diagnostic tool applicable to strongly correlated materials, offering a way to probe pairing mechanisms directly without relying on complex material-specific assumptions or complementary measurements.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a method that provides a direct and unambiguous probe of electronic pairing in strongly correlated quantum materials, overcoming limitations of standard ARPES.

**Main result.** Two-photon two-electron coincidence ARPES ($2\gamma 	o 2e$ $2e$ARPES) offers a model-independent separation in the photoemission energy plane between signals from electrons originating from the same pair and those from different pairs, allowing direct measurement of pairing signatures.

**Method.** Theoretical derivation of coincident photoelectron intensity formulas and comparison of spectral weight regions in the $(\omega_1, \omega_2)$ energy plane for paired versus unpaired ground states.

**Model / system.** The study applies general theoretical grounds to strongly correlated electron systems, modeled using frameworks like the Hubbard model or Hubbard-Holstein model. The analysis focuses on distinguishing between paired and unpaired electronic ground states.

**Key observables.** Coincidence photoelectron intensity $D(\omega_1, \omega_2)$ in the $(\omega_1, \omega_2)$ energy plane; specific energy bounds related to pair binding energy ($\Delta$) and boson energy ($\Omega$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Pairing gap ($\Delta$), pair binding energy, chemical potential ($\mu$), and the photon/boson energies ($\omega_{ph}, \Omega$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the sudden approximation when photoelectrons depart; results are derived at zero temperature but expected to apply to preformed pair liquids.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the spectral weight regions in the $(\omega_1, \omega_2)$ plane for both quasiparticle liquid and paired states, showing distinct boundaries separating type-(i) (different pairs) and type-(ii) (same pair) emission signals.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes general theoretical grounds by deriving intensity formulas, analyzes the spectral separation in the $(\omega_1, \omega_2)$ plane for both paired and unpaired systems using numerical simulations of correlated models, and contrasts this method with one-photon setups.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding strongly correlated quantum materials remains a central challenge in condensed matter physics and materials science. While angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (ARPES) has become an indispensable probe of single-quasiparticle excitations, it accesses electronic correlations only indirectly. Here we show that unlike one-photon in, two-electrons out coincidence ARPES ($γ\!\rightarrow\!2e$ 2eARPES), the two-photon in, two-electron out $2γ\!\rightarrow\!2e$ 2eARPES provides a direct and unambiguous probe of electronic pairing. We establish this on general theoretical grounds and substantiate it through large-scale numerical simulations of strongly correlated models with both paired and unpaired ground states. The key result is a model-independent separation in the $(ω_1,ω_2)$ plane of the two photoelectrons' energies, between signal from electrons emitted from the \emph{same} pair and signal from electrons emitted from \emph{different} pairs; this follows from energy conservation alone and is independent of any material-specific assumptions. Our findings demonstrate that $2γ\!\rightarrow\!2e$ 2eARPES can identify pairing and extract the pair binding energy as well as the energy of the 'glue' boson without any sophisticated data analysis or complementary measurements.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents the construction of exact, propagating wave-packet solutions for the Dirac equation in an attractive Coulomb-like potential. The key findings include demonstrating that the probability density decouples from spin and that the wave packet's time evolution freezes at a critical coupling strength. This provides significant analytical insight into relativistic quantum dynamics in structured potentials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides exact analytical solutions for wave propagation in a confining potential within a relativistic framework, which is highly relevant to understanding particle confinement in structured media or quantum waveguides beyond simple approximations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is constructing exact, positive-energy, normalizable wave-packet solutions for the Dirac equation subjected to an attractive Coulomb-like potential ($V = -v_0/ho$).

**Main result.** The authors constructed novel families of exact solutions exhibiting two key features: spin-density decoupling and temporal freezing at a critical coupling. These solutions correctly reduce to known nonrelativistic forms like the Hermite–Gauss wave packet.

**Method.** Methods involve solving coupled partial differential equations derived from the Dirac Hamiltonian, utilizing superposition techniques, and mapping solutions from the 2D Helmholtz equation (H->D map).

**Model / system.** The model is the relativistic Dirac equation solved in cylindrical coordinates with a central potential $V = -v_0/ho$. The analysis focuses on propagating wave packets characterized by their longitudinal marginal density.

**Key observables.** Longitudinal marginal density $P(z, t)$, spin-density decoupling ($\Psi^\dagger\Psi$ independent of spin), and the group velocity's slowing down as confinement increases.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling constant $v_0$, which has a critical value leading to temporal freezing; the nonrelativistic limit ($c 	o \infty$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis initially sets $\hbar=m=c=1$ and focuses on positive-energy eigenstates. The derivation relies on symmetry considerations for axisymmetric Hamiltonians.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the time evolution of the marginal density showing dispersive spreading that slows down as confinement increases, leading to temporal freezing. Figure 2 shows this effect for varying coupling parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically derives solutions by first establishing the governing PDEs, providing explicit examples using elementary functions, and later developing a general mapping scheme (H->D) from known solvable equations like the Helmholtz equation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct exact, positive-energy, normalizable wave-packet solutions of the Dirac equation in the axisymmetric potential $V=-\,v_0/ρ$ -- to our knowledge, the first such solutions in any external potential. Remarkably, one family comprises only elementary functions whose longitudinal profiles reproduce the free-Schrödinger Hermite--Gauss wave packets in the nonrelativistic limit. All packets share two striking features: (i) a probability density that is pointwise decoupled from spin orientation -- despite the inherent spin-orbit coupling of the Dirac equation -- and (ii) a complete freezing of their time evolution at the critical coupling $v_0\to\hbar c/2$. We also present a simple scheme that maps solutions of the 2D Helmholtz equation to further exact Dirac wave packets.

</details>

### [Quantum algorithm for Valiant-Vazirani reduction](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.18428v1)

**Authors:** Patrick Kelly, Victoria S. Ordonez, Michael R. Geller  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.18428v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5**

Points marked black (chosen randomly) indicate s satisfying assignments with f(x) = 1. In this example, n = 10 and s = 9. (b) Random partition into 2k subsets for different values of k. For each k, the individual sectors can be indexed by a number a ∈{0, 1}k (not shown in the illustration). When s ≈2k there is, on average, 1 satisfying assignment per circular sector. In the example shown, this occurs when k = 3.</figcaption>

<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 2.</b> Figure 3: Circuits to implement the 8 clause signatures. Here a, b, c ∈{0, 1} and X is a σx gate. The 3AND gate is defined in Figure 4.</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2606.18428_figures/2606.18428_fig3.jpg" alt="Figure 3">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 3.</b> Figure 5: Circuit to implement M-bit conjunction using CCNOTs. Here xi ∈{0, 1}. This requires O(M) CCNOT gates and O(M) additional ancilla initialized to |0⟩. The case M = 5 is shown.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** The paper develops a quantum algorithm implementing the Valiant-Vazirani reduction by transforming SAT into UNIQUE SAT. This is achieved by constructing a complex oracle circuit based on random hashing constraints and leveraging 'torsion nonlinearity' found in systems like Bose-Einstein condensates. The result suggests that fault-tolerant implementation could solve NP problems, though this speedup requires nonlinear quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges abstract complexity theory (NP-completeness) with concrete proposals for nonlinear quantum computation, offering a pathway to solving hard problems using physical models like BECs that exhibit such nonlinearities.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To construct a quantum algorithm for the Valiant-Vazirani reduction, which reduces the NP-complete SAT problem to UNIQUE SAT.

**Main result.** The authors provide a randomized polynomial-time reduction from SAT to UNIQUE SAT using a filtered oracle. This implies that fault-tolerant implementation could solve NP problems in polynomial time via nonlinear quantum coprocessors.

**Method.** The core method involves constructing a filtered Boolean function $\phi(x)$ by combining the original 3SAT formula with indicator functions derived from random hash constraints, which are then implemented using reversible oracle circuits.

**Model / system.** The theoretical framework relies on extending gate-based quantum computation to include auxiliary degrees of freedom governed by a nonlinear Schrödinger equation. The specific nonlinearity utilized is 'torsion nonlinearity,' arising in models like two-component Bose-Einstein condensates or spin systems with all-to-all Ising interaction.

**Key observables.** The probability $p = 	ext{Pr}(X=1)$ of finding the unique satisfying assignment, and the expected number of solutions $\lambda$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis requires choosing an optimal hash dimension $k$ such that $1/16 < \lambda \le 1/8$, and the speedup is contingent on operating in the noise-free limit.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumptions are the existence of a fault-tolerant nonlinear quantum coprocessor simulating torsion, and the analysis holds strictly in the idealized noise-free limit.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Valiant-Vazirani filter concept using random partitioning, circuits for encoding hash constraints (8 clause signatures), and general circuit structures for implementing 3AND gates and conjunctions using CCNOTs.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical reduction from SAT to UNIQUE SAT. It then details the construction of the filtered oracle by rewriting the indicator function into a system of linear equations over $\mathbb{F}_2$, which is finally implemented via complex quantum circuits involving ancilla qubits and CCNOT gates.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

There is growing interest in extensions of the standard model of gate-based quantum computation to include auxiliary degrees of freedom evolving according to a nonlinear Schrödinger equation. By reducing the Boolean satisfiability problem SAT to quantum state discrimination, Abrams and Lloyd argued that the right type of nonlinearity can be used to solve {\sf NP} and \#{\sf P} problems in polynomial time, at least in an idealized noise-free limit. For practical implementation, however, we are restricted to simulated and emergent nonlinearities, such as that appearing in mean field models for ultracold atoms and similar ensembles. A prominent example is the torsion model, which arises in two-component Bose-Einstein condensates and spin models with all-to-all Ising interaction. But torsion-based state discrimination appears to fall short of solving SAT. Here we close this gap by constructing the filtered oracle of the Valiant-Vazirani theorem, providing a randomized polynomial-time reduction from SAT to UNIQUE SAT, a promise problem where there is at most 1 satisfying assignment. In the noise free limit, the UNIQUE SAT problem can be olved in polynomial time using torsion nonlinearity. Quantum Valiant-Vazirani reduction is no faster than the efficient classical version, but a fault-tolerant implementation coupled to a nonlinear quantum coprocessor simulating torsion would enable polynomial time solution to {\sf NP} (but not \#{\sf P}) problems.

</details>

### [Quantum circuit decomposition of the tangent-fermion Dirac operator](http://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19020v1)

**Authors:** C. W. J. Beenakker  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.19020v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** The paper tackles the challenge of implementing lattice Dirac operators on quantum computers, where standard methods suffer from exponentially growing circuit complexity. By reformulating the problem using the tangent-fermion discretization within a generalized eigenvalue framework, the authors achieve an encoding whose resource requirements scale favorably (polylogarithmically or $O(1)$) with system size.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the resource scaling of simulating fundamental condensed matter Hamiltonians (like Dirac fermions) on near-term quantum hardware by providing a method to encode them efficiently in terms of unitaries.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing an efficient quantum circuit representation for the Dirac operator on a lattice that avoids fermion doubling while maintaining polynomial scaling with system size.

**Main result.** The tangent-fermion discretization, when formulated as a generalized eigenvalue problem $(	ext{H} - E	ext{P})\Psi = 0$, yields operators whose quantum circuits have term counts and subnormalization factors independent of lattice size, matching the efficiency of elliptic operators.

**Method.** Analyzing the Linear Combination of Unitaries (LCU) decomposition for discretized Dirac operators and reformulating the problem into a local generalized eigenvalue structure to achieve efficient encoding.

**Model / system.** The 1D/higher-dimensional Dirac equation discretized on a lattice, comparing standard discretizations ($\partial_{\sin}$) against the tangent-fermion scheme. The core mathematical tool is casting the problem as $(	ext{H} - E	ext{P})\Psi = 0$.

**Key observables.** Eigenvalues near $E=0$, smallest singular value $\sigma_{\min}(E)$, and the efficiency metrics ($L$ and $\lambda$) of the quantum circuit encoding.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Lattice size ($N$), energy ($E$), and the structure of the operators $	ext{H}$ and $	ext{P}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on recasting the problem into a generalized eigenvalue form $(	ext{H} - E	ext{P})\Psi = 0$ to bypass the efficiency bottlenecks found in direct LCU decompositions of non-local operators.

**Figures summary.** Table I compares the figures of merit ($L$, $\lambda$) for different discretizations, showing that only the pencil representation for $\partial_{	ext{tangent}}$ achieves $O(1)$ scaling for both metrics.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the efficiency limitations of standard Dirac operator discretizations in quantum circuits. It then introduces and analyzes the tangent-fermion discretization by recasting it into a local generalized eigenvalue problem $(	ext{H} - E	ext{P})\Psi = 0$. Finally, it discusses methods for solving this system, such as quantum landscape scanning.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Dirac operator on a lattice cannot be both local and free of fermion doubling, at least not without breaking fundamental symmetries. Non-local, symmetry-preserving discretizations that avoid doubling have a quantum circuit representation as a linear-combination-of-unitaries (LCU) in which both the number of terms and their norm (the subnormalization factor) grow with the lattice size, compromising the efficiency of a quantum algorithm. We show that the tangent-fermion discretization escapes this obstruction when the Dirac equation is written as a generalized eigenvalue problem with a local operator pencil: Each member of the pencil has an exact LCU, with term count that is independent of lattice size and with subnormalization factor of order unity, on a par with elliptic operators. This provides an efficient block-encoding primitive for Dirac spectra and Green functions without fermion doubling.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces significant algorithmic enhancements to Quantum-Classical Auxiliary-Field Quantum Monte Carlo (QC-AFQMC) methods. By improving how singular Pfaffians are calculated and utilizing advanced differentiation techniques, the method's scaling is dramatically improved from $\mathcal{O}(N^{5.5})$ to $\mathcal{O}(N^{4.5})$. This advancement brings QC-AFQMC closer to treating chemically relevant systems by demonstrating feasibility for larger molecular models.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the computational bottleneck in simulating strongly correlated electronic structure problems, providing a concrete pathway toward achieving quantum advantage in chemistry by optimizing classical post-processing steps.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is improving the computational scaling of Quantum-Classical Auxiliary-Field Quantum Monte Carlo (QC-AFQMC) for calculating ground state energies in fermionic systems.

**Main result.** The authors achieved a significant algorithmic improvement, reducing the dominant per-step classical scaling from $	ilde{\mathcal{O}}(N^{5.5})$ to $	ilde{\mathcal{O}}(N^{4.5})$, leading to substantial estimated runtime reductions for chemically relevant systems.

**Method.** The core method is QC-AFQMC, which combines quantum state preparation (using matchgate shadows) with classical Monte Carlo sampling. Key algorithmic improvements include applying Aitken's block transformation to handle singular Pfaffians and using algorithmic differentiation for the force bias calculation.

**Model / system.** The work applies to calculating ground-state energies of molecular systems, demonstrated on $	ext{H}_8$ and hydrogen chains up to $	ext{H}_{12}$, as well as the $	ext{Li}_2	ext{O}_4$ lithium superoxide dimer rearrangement pathway. The underlying physics is described by a second-quantized Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Ground-state energy; computational runtimes (classical and quantum); scaling exponents ($N$, number of molecular spin-orbitals).

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size (up to 100 orbitals demonstrated in estimates); error mitigation threshold ($\xi$ or $\eta$); required qubit counts for fault tolerance.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on algorithmic improvements like Aitken's transformation and the use of matchgate shadow tomography, which are approximations necessary due to current quantum hardware limitations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate resource requirements vs. system size, reaction pathway diagrams for $	ext{Li}_2	ext{O}_4$, and energy evolution curves comparing QC-AFQMC against other methods like ph-AFQMC.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the theoretical framework of QC-AFQMC, presents algorithmic improvements (Aitken's transformation, differentiation), validates these improvements through scaling analysis, and applies the method to benchmark molecular systems ($	ext{H}_8$, $	ext{Li}_2	ext{O}_4$).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce algorithmic improvements to quantum-classical auxiliary-field quantum Monte Carlo (QC-AFQMC) that reduce the dominant per-step classical scaling from $\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(N^{5.5})$ to $\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(N^{4.5})$ as a function of the number of molecular spin-orbitals $N$. Central to this improvement is the application of Aitken's block transformation to handle singular Pfaffians arising in the estimation of overlaps between a quantum trial state and classical Slater-determinant walkers. Together with the use of algorithmic differentiation for the computation of the force bias, this yields a $248\times$ estimated runtime improvement for a system of 100 molecular orbitals. Using our workflow, we demonstrate a ground-state energy calculation for $H_8$ from quantum data collected on IQM Emerald and post-processed with a tensor-network-based error-mitigation technique. We further validate the method's scalability through noiseless simulation of hydrogen chains up to $H_{12}$, and on the lithium-air battery related rearrangement pathway of the $Li_2O_4$ lithium superoxide dimer in a (26e, 20o) active space. We estimate both quantum and classical runtimes for a potential fault-tolerant implementation of QC-AFQMC, showing that the method holds promise for the early fault-tolerant era. These results move QC-AFQMC a step closer to treating chemically relevant systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the critical problem of distributing the QFT circuit across multiple QPUs by minimizing entanglement overhead using a technique called gate packing. The authors prove that their specialized, optimal partitioning scheme significantly reduces the required e-bit count compared to general methods. This work provides both theoretical guarantees and empirical validation on quantum hardware for building scalable distributed quantum processors.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, resource-aware compilation strategy for one of the most fundamental subroutines in quantum algorithms (QFT), directly addressing the engineering challenge of scaling quantum computation across multiple physical devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop an optimal method for partitioning the Quantum Fourier Transform (QFT) circuit so that it can run efficiently on a distributed quantum system while minimizing required entanglement resources.

**Main result.** The authors propose and prove an optimal gate-packing-based partitioning scheme for the QFT, demonstrating resource savings compared to general algorithms. This method achieves analytically proven lower bounds on the required e-bit count in bipartite cases.

**Method.** The work employs circuit rewriting using quantum teleportation protocols (Teledata/Telegate) and introduces 'gate packing' to efficiently group multiple two-qubit gates into single entanglement operations. Theoretical proofs establish optimal qubit assignment strategies based on minimizing the total required e-bits.

**Model / system.** The model is a distributed quantum computing architecture utilizing multiple Quantum Processing Units (QPUs) connected by entanglement channels, with the QFT circuit being the specific unitary operation analyzed. The analysis compares theoretical bounds derived from communication complexity ($Q_0$) against practical implementations on simulated and physical hardware.

**Key observables.** e-bit count (the number of maximally entangled qubit pairs required), Average Fidelity ($F$), Circuit Depth, and Time taken for execution.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of QPUs ($m$), total qubits ($n$), QPU capacities ($n_1, \dots, n_m$), and the constant ancilla limit (e.g., 1 or 2).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes access to necessary entanglement resources; comparisons are sometimes made between exact circuits and approximate versions generated by standard compilers like Pytket.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate protocols like Teledata/Telegate, show comparisons of e-bit requirements (packed vs. non-packed), and present fidelity decay curves comparing naive partitioning against the proposed gate-packed scheme on simulated and physical hardware.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the problem (QFT distribution) to introducing advanced techniques like gate packing and detached gates, establishing theoretical lower bounds via communication complexity analysis, deriving optimal assignment theorems, and finally validating these results through both simulation and actual quantum hardware measurements.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A promising avenue for scaling quantum computing is to connect quantum processing units (QPUs) by generating entanglement between them. This requires circuit partitioning: partially rewriting quantum circuits to run on a distributed quantum system using quantum teleportation protocols, while preserving the unitary operation implemented by the circuit. The key metric to minimize when partitioning is the e-bit count, defined as the number of maximally entangled qubit pairs that must be generated between QPUs. We focus on partitioning the quantum Fourier transform (QFT) circuit, which is widely used as a subroutine in quantum algorithms such as quantum phase estimation and arithmetic circuits. Specifically, we present a partitioning scheme based on optimal gate-packing, compare it against prior analytical partitioning schemes for the QFT, and evaluate it against partitions produced by general-purpose circuit partitioning algorithms. We further validate our approach by implementing the partitioned circuit on quantum hardware.

</details>

**Authors:** Yizhou Ma, Gen Yue, Tian Lan  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.19137v1>
