**Summary.** This theoretical work establishes a comprehensive bulk-boundary correspondence for one-dimensional gapped quantum phases with generalized symmetries. By employing operator algebras and advanced category theory, the authors rigorously prove that the bulk topological order can be mathematically derived from the structure of the boundary physics via the enriched center construction. This provides a powerful classification tool for understanding how edge states are dictated by the bulk material properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical machinery developed—connecting algebraic structures like centers of enriched categories to physical observables—provides a powerful, general tool for classifying topological phases beyond simple symmetry groups, which is highly relevant for understanding correlated electron systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a rigorous operator-algebraic framework to establish the bulk-boundary correspondence for $(1+1)$D gapped quantum phases possessing categorical symmetry.

**Main result.** It proves that the enriched monoidal category describing the bulk topological structure is equivalent to the enriched center of the category describing the boundary physics. This provides a complete mathematical classification linking bulk and edge properties.

**Method.** The methodology relies on working in the thermodynamic limit using $C^*$-algebras, constructing Hamiltonians via commuting-projector interactions, and employing advanced tools from enriched category theory (DHR bimodules, centers of categories).

**Model / system.** The focus is on one-dimensional gapped quantum spin chains exhibiting categorical symmetry. The analysis uses abstract mathematical structures like unitary fusion categories ($\mathcal{C}$) and module categories ($\mathcal{M}$), rather than a single concrete Hamiltonian.

**Key observables.** Ground states of constructed Hamiltonians, the boundary DHR category, and the bulk/boundary topological field theories (SymTFTs).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system is analyzed in the thermodynamic limit; symmetry structure is governed by the fusion category $\mathcal{C}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes the existence of a unitary fusion category and an indecomposable semisimple right module category, allowing for the construction of quasi-local algebras.

**Figures summary.** Figure 6 summarizes the complete description of 1D symmetric gapped phases, relating four components: bulk SymTFT, boundary SymTFT, the category of boundary conditions ($	ext{BCond}$), and the underlying algebraic structures.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from general concepts in infinite lattice algebras (AF algebras) to specific constructions for fusion spin chains. It then systematically establishes equivalences between operator-algebraic descriptions of boundary physics (via DHR categories) and abstract categorical definitions, culminating in the central theorem relating bulk structure to the center of the boundary structure.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop an operator-algebraic framework for boundary conditions and bulk-boundary correspondence in one-dimensional gapped phases with categorical symmetry. Working directly in the thermodynamic limit, we construct half-infinite fusion spin chains and commuting-projector boundary Hamiltonians from a unitary fusion category $\mathcal{C}$, an indecomposable semisimple right $\mathcal{C}$-module category $\mathcal{M}$, a Q-system $Q\in\mathcal{C}$ specifying the bulk phase, and a right $Q$-module $K\in\mathcal{M}_{Q}$, regarded as an object of $\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{\mathrm{op}}$, specifying the boundary. We prove that these Hamiltonians have unique ground states and that the resulting realization functor $\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{BCond}$ is an equivalence, so simple boundary conditions are classified by simple objects of $\mathcal{M}_{Q}$ and general boundary conditions by their finite direct sums. We also give a microscopic formulation of the boundary symmetry topological field theory using DHR bimodules of the boundary quasi-local algebra. For a half-infinite fusion spin chain, the boundary DHR category is monoidally equivalent to $(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\vee})^{\mathrm{rev}}$, and the canonical action of the bulk DHR category on it agrees with the categorical action of $Z_1(\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{rev}})$. Finally, we identify the action of the boundary DHR category on boundary conditions with the categorical action of $(\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{M}}^{\vee})^{\mathrm{rev}}$ on $\mathcal{M}_{Q}^{\mathrm{op}}$. This yields a one-dimensional bulk-boundary correspondence: the enriched monoidal category describing the bulk is the enriched center of the enriched category describing the boundary.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates superconductivity in 2H-NbSeS, a material engineered to suppress CDW order. By analyzing critical fields, specific heat, and magnetic penetration depth across multiple probes, the authors conclusively demonstrate that the superconducting state requires a two-band model. This provides strong evidence for an intrinsic multiband pairing mechanism, distinguishing it from competing electronic instabilities.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous differentiation between intrinsic pairing mechanisms (multiband) and competing orders (CDW) using quantitative measurements is highly relevant for understanding complex many-body ground states in condensed matter physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is distinguishing intrinsic multiband superconductivity from spurious effects caused by competing electronic orders, such as Charge Density Wave (CDW) formation, in layered superconductors.

**Main result.** The superconducting properties of 2H-NbSeS are robustly described by a two-band model, indicating an intrinsic, strongly asymmetric multiband pairing mechanism rather than CDW-related artifacts.

**Method.** A combination of advanced experimental measurements (critical fields, specific heat, penetration depth) and theoretical modeling (single-gap vs. two-band fitting) was employed to analyze the superconducting state.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on single-crystalline 2H-NbSeS, a mixed-chalcogen analogue of 2H-NbSe2. The system is characterized by intrinsic substitutional disorder (Se/S), which suppresses long-range CDW order while preserving the layered crystal structure.

**Key observables.** Upper and lower critical magnetic fields ($H_{c2}, H_{c1}$), superfluid density ($ho_s(T)$), electronic specific heat ($C_e(T)$), and superconducting gap values ($\Delta_1, \Delta_2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The in-plane upper critical field exceeds the weak-coupling Pauli limit; the analysis requires a dirty-limit two-band model with a large diffusivity ratio.

**Assumptions / limitations.** It is assumed that random substitutional disorder effectively suppresses long-range CDW order, thereby isolating the intrinsic superconducting pairing mechanism for investigation.

**Figures summary.** Figures show XRD patterns confirming crystal structure and stoichiometry; subsequent figures analyze $H_{c2}(T)$ curvature requiring a two-band fit, and $\Delta\lambda(T)$ and $C_e(T)$ data are used to confirm the necessity of a two-gap model.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the clean structural platform (disorder suppressing CDW), then using transport measurements ($H_{c2}$) to necessitate a two-band description, and finally confirming this multiband nature through multiple thermodynamic probes like specific heat and penetration depth ($\Delta\lambda$).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The nature of superconductivity in 2H-NbSe2 has generated sustained debate in the recent past. While angle resolved photoemission spectroscopy data have been interpreted as evidence for multiband superconductivity, the data from scanning tunneling microscope experiments relate to strongly anisotropic single-band superconductivity. In the later case, the charge density wave (CDW) order mimics the multigap character. Because the CDW reconstructs the Fermi surface and modifies the superconducting gap distribution, disentangling intrinsic multiband pairing from CDW-related effects is challenging. To address this issue, we investigate single-crystalline 2H-NbSeS, a mixed-chalcogen analogue of 2H-NbSe2 in which random Se/S substitution suppresses long-range CDW order while preserving the layered crystal structure P63/mmc. The material becomes superconducting below 6.0 K with moderate magnetic anisotropy. The upper critical field exhibits a pronounced upward curvature that cannot be described within a single-band framework but is well captured by a dirty-limit two-band model with a large diffusivity ratio. This indicates strong band-dependent scattering. The in-plane upper critical field exceeds the weak-coupling Pauli limit. Measurements of the lower critical field, superfluid density, and electronic specific heat are consistent with an interpretation of a fully gapped superconducting state with two nodeless gaps of different magnitudes.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes barren plateaus in VQAs by developing a first-moment diagnostic tool. It proves that avoiding these flat regions is non-unique, allowing for exponentially many distinct initialization strategies. The key takeaway is that choosing an initialization strategy becomes a new challenge: selecting the right trainable 'pocket' among many viable options.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a deep theoretical understanding of the geometry of quantum optimization landscapes, suggesting that 'avoiding flatness' is not enough; one must also select the correct basin of attraction among many possibilities.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the barren plateau phenomenon in Variational Quantum Algorithms (VQAs), which causes loss functions to become exponentially flat during training.

**Main result.** It demonstrates that avoiding barren plateaus is highly non-unique, allowing for the construction of exponentially many inequivalent initialization strategies. Furthermore, these different initializations can lead the optimization process toward distinct local minima.

**Method.** The analysis introduces a first-moment framework and uses operator diagnostics (like the expected Heisenberg-evolved operator gap) to characterize when an initialization escapes the fully concentrated barren-plateau fixed point.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on Parameterized Quantum Circuits (PQCs) acting on $n$-qubit Hilbert spaces, where the goal is minimizing a loss function derived from quantum expectation values.

**Key observables.** Expected Heisenberg-evolved operator gap ($\xi_{BP,\gamma}$), first moment coefficients ($a$ and $b$), and the squared derivative of the loss function ($\mathbb{E}_	heta \left[ rac{\partial L(	heta)}{\partial	heta} ight]^2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of qubits ($n$), circuit depth ($L$), initialization width ($\varsigma$), and specific graph structures (e.g., constant-odd-degree graphs).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes the existence of a naïve parameter distribution causing BPs, or relies on simplifying assumptions about graph topologies and gate commutativity for deriving bounds.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic trade-offs between restricting dynamics vs. smart initialization; heatmaps show gradient magnitudes across qubits/layers for different initializations; contour plots visualize local gradient landscapes.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the BP problem to introducing the first-moment diagnostic, proving existence theorems for exponentially many non-concentrating initializations, and finally showing that these distinct initializations lead to measurably different optimization behaviors.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Barren plateaus are stated as an average-case phenomenon: pick an ansatz, initialize it naively, and concentration follows. This has led to the common view that a potential cure for barren plateaus is simply to initialize the parameters more carefully. Here we show that the situation is subtler. We introduce a first-moment framework that gives a simple operator-level diagnostic for when an initialization may escape the fully concentrated barren-plateau fixed point, and for comparing the biases induced by different initialization strategies. Our framework recovers several known initialization schemes such as identity and Gaussian initialization, but also shows that barren-plateau avoidance is highly non-unique. Indeed, many shifted, biased, and non-symmetric parameter distributions can avoid concentration, and these choices need not be equivalent. In fact, our results show that one can generate exponentially many families of inequivalent initialization strategies. Then, our numerics indicate that different first-moment-distinct initializations can lead to different attained minima, suggesting that avoiding barren plateaus via smart initializations can trade the exponential concentration problem for the challenge of selecting the right trainable pocket amongst many options.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper addresses the difficulty of measuring scaling exponents ($\alpha$) in finite hyperuniform systems by developing a sophisticated, multi-method protocol. It combines analysis from structure factors, number variance, and dynamic spreadability to yield a robust 'effective' exponent estimate. This methodology is vital for accurately characterizing structural correlations in real or simulated disordered materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides advanced analytical tools for characterizing long-range correlations in disordered materials, which is crucial for understanding the physics of amorphous solids or colloidal suspensions where true asymptotic measurements are impossible.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is accurately estimating the effective scaling exponent ($\alpha$) that characterizes the suppression of long-wavelength density fluctuations in finite-size hyperuniform point configurations.

**Main result.** A robust, method-aware protocol combining structure factor analysis, number variance diagnosis, and spreadability measurements provides a stable estimate for $\alpha$ by assessing internal consistency across different diagnostic tools.

**Method.** The authors developed a joint empirical estimator that synthesizes results from three complementary methods: regularized low-$k$ window screening on $S(k)$, Class-like diagnosis from number variance, and plateau-window analysis of the spreadability excess $E(	au)$.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on theoretical models of hyperuniform point configurations in 2D, often generated by constraining the structure factor $S(k)$ to follow a prescribed power law, allowing for systematic testing across various target exponents $\alpha$.

**Key observables.** Effective scaling exponent ($\alpha$), Structure Factor ($S(k)$), Number Variance ($\sigma^2(R)$), and Spreadability Excess ($E(	au) = S(\infty) - S(t)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis is highly dependent on the accessible length scales (low-$k$ cutoff, window size $R$, time $	au$) due to finite-size effects.

**Assumptions / limitations.** It assumes that by combining multiple diagnostic methods and implementing rigorous window selection protocols, one can extract a stable *effective* exponent even when true thermodynamic limits are inaccessible.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the sensitivity of extracted $\alpha$ to fixed fitting windows (Table II) and demonstrate how different physical observables ($S(k)$, $E(	au)$) require specific intermediate-range analysis windows to yield consistent results.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces the limitations of individual methods, proposes a unified protocol by combining three distinct analyses ($S(k)$, $\sigma^2(R)$, $E(	au)$), and validates this combined approach using benchmark systems and independently generated physical models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hyperuniform systems strongly suppress long-wavelength density fluctuations, which is quantitatively characterized by the small-wavenumber scaling. In finite samples, however, accurately estimating the hyperuniformity exponent α can be challenging. The inferred value depends strongly on the range of length scales accessible in the measurement, finite-size effects, and the specific characterization method employed, whether based on Fourier-space structure factors, real-space density fluctuations, or dynamical probes such as diffusion spreadability. In particular, the structure-factor method provides the most direct estimate of α, but is sensitive to empirical low-k fitting cutoffs. The number-variance method offers a real-space Class-like diagnosis, but contributes a numerical exponent only when the finite-size data retain Class III-like scaling information. The spreadability method provides a smoother dynamic estimate and reduces configuration-level fluctuations, but requires a physically admissible long-time fitting window. Here, we develop a practical method-aware protocol for robust estimation of the effective scaling exponent α in finite-size hyperuniform point configurations, combining three complementary methods with distinct roles. Our protocol summarizes the method-specific estimates through a joint empirical estimator and reports the internal dispersion among the participating methods to determine the optimal estimate.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a comprehensive, multi-layered evaluation framework designed to rigorously test LLMs generating quantum circuits for chemistry simulations. It moves beyond checking if code runs by assessing physical validity, structural consistency (design entropy), and adherence to known chemical principles. The findings emphasize that robust validation infrastructure is as critical as the models themselves for reliable deployment.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the reliability bottleneck for integrating advanced AI tools into computational physics pipelines, providing necessary validation metrics beyond simple functional testing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is developing robust evaluation methods for Large Language Models (LLMs) when they are tasked with generating complex, executable quantum circuits for scientific simulations.

**Main result.** A layered evaluation framework incorporating gatekeeper rubrics, circuit fidelity checks against reference values, and design entropy proves necessary to reliably deploy LLM-assisted quantum circuit generation in practice.

**Method.** The authors propose a three-part validation system: (1) A rubric screening across seven criteria; (2) Fidelity analysis comparing generated circuits to known analytical/reference implementations; and (3) Design entropy measurement for behavioral consistency.

**Model / system.** The focus is on Variational Quantum Eigensolvers (VQE) applied to molecular systems, specifically using the $	ext{H}_2/	ext{STO-3G}/	ext{Jordan–Wigner}/	ext{UCCSD}$ mapping. The evaluation platform itself is an agentic pipeline integrating LLMs with quantum chemistry workflows.

**Key observables.** Five failure modes (e.g., geometry hallucination, nonexistent API usage), rubric scores (0-4), circuit depth/gate counts, and design entropy values.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The fidelity of the generated ansatz relative to analytical expectations; temperature ($T$) for testing model stability.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes that LLM failures can be categorized structurally rather than being tied to a specific model architecture. A significant limitation noted is the reliance on single-rater scoring for rubrics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the system architecture, VQE circuits, rubric heatmaps showing model performance across criteria, and radar plots/graphs detailing design entropy and temperature stability of generated code.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem space (LLMs in quantum workflows), details the layered evaluation framework components (gatekeeper, fidelity check, entropy), presents empirical results using multiple models on molecular systems, and concludes with a discussion on infrastructure auditing and future improvements.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

As large language models (LLMs) become embedded in quantum simulation workflows (IDE copilots, notebook assistants, agentic pipelines), evaluation must move beyond functional correctness to anticipate and catch structured failures before they propagate through expensive pipelines. We present a layered evaluation framework for materials-informed Variational Quantum Eigensolver (VQE) circuit generation: (i) a gatekeeper screening rubric across seven physical and framework criteria; (ii) a circuit fidelity analysis comparing model outputs against analytical and reference-implementation values for H2/STO-3G/Jordan-Wigner/UCCSD, with ansatz classification and gate-composition breakdown; and (iii) design entropy, a run-to-run behavioral consistency metric. We surface a taxonomy of five distinct LLM failure modes (geometry hallucination, nonexistent API usage, runtime integration failures, constraint violations, and plausible-but-unverifiable output), each with distinct detectability profiles and structural to the task rather than to any one model. A forensic audit of the evaluation platform's own source code further establishes that two apparent model failures originated in the harness through silent fallback-template substitution, demonstrating that evaluation infrastructure belongs inside the same trust boundary as the models it tests. Applied across multiple foundation models on a Materials Project integrated pipeline, the framework shows that gatekeeper-style validation is necessary, not optional, for reliable deployment.

</details>

**Authors:** Hanxiang Zhang, Zexin Feng, I-Te Lu, Zhiwei Li, Songhao Guo, Qiuyu Shang, Dening Luan, Mingcheng Panmai, Kenji Watanabe, Takashi Taniguchi, Angel Rubio, Weibo Gao

**Summary.** The authors experimentally demonstrate that coupling a superconductor ($	ext{NbSe}_2$) to an engineered terahertz cavity (CSRR) can enhance its critical temperature. By tuning the cavity resonance near $2 	ext{ THz}$, they achieved up to a 10% increase in $T_c$, establishing cavity engineering as a powerful tool for tailoring quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges cavity quantum electrodynamics principles into condensed matter physics by using engineered electromagnetic environments to tune fundamental superconducting properties, which is highly relevant for open quantum systems research.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To experimentally demonstrate that vacuum electromagnetic fluctuations, controlled via cavity engineering, can enhance superconductivity in quantum materials.

**Main result.** The superconducting transition temperature ($T_{c,0}$) of few-layer $	ext{NbSe}_2$ increased by approximately 10% when coupled to a terahertz cavity resonant at $2.04 	ext{ THz}$.

**Method.** Transport measurements (R-T curves) were performed on superconducting films integrated with a complementary split-ring resonator (CSRR) structure, combined with field simulations.

**Model / system.** The system involves few-layer niobium diselenide ($	ext{NbSe}_2$), a van der Waals superconductor, coupled to a $	ext{THz}$ cavity formed by a CSRR. The study probes the interaction between superconductivity and local electromagnetic fields.

**Key observables.** Superconducting transition temperature ($T_{c,0}$), lower critical field ($H_{c1}$), and the change in $T_{c,0}$ ($\Delta T_c$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Resonant frequency of the cavity (e.g., $2.04 	ext{ THz}$), local electric field strength ($\sim 0.8 	ext{ V/m}$), and material thickness.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The enhancement is attributed to the resonant electromagnetic environment, after rigorously ruling out contributions from sample inhomogeneity or charge density wave (CDW) effects via control experiments.

**Figures summary.** Figures show $R$-$T$ curves demonstrating enhanced $T_{c,0}$ at specific cavity resonances ($2.04 	ext{ THz}$) compared to baseline measurements, and plots illustrating the spatial dependence of this enhancement across the cavity field profile.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem by noting previous suppression results, then presents the experimental setup using $	ext{NbSe}_2$/CSRR. It systematically measures $T_{c,0}$ as a function of location (spatial mapping) and resonance frequency (spectral mapping), concluding that optimal enhancement occurs near $2 	ext{ THz}$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Vacuum electromagnetic fluctuations have recently emerged as a promising means of controlling collective quantum phases. Although cavity-induced modifications of superconductivity have been widely predicted, experimental studies have so far reported only suppression of superconducting properties. Here, by carefully tuning a terahertz cavity to resonate with key phononic modes in few-layer niobium diselenide (NbSe2), we demonstrate cavity-enhanced superconductivity in few-layer NbSe2 coupled to a complementary split-ring resonator. In trilayer NbSe2, the superconducting transition temperature increases by ~10%, from 3.02 K to 3.41 K, when coupled to a cavity resonant at 2.04 THz. The enhancement exhibits a clear spatial dependence following the cavity field profile and a non-monotonic frequency dependence, with maximal enhancement near 2 THz. These results provide experimental evidence that vacuum electromagnetic fields can enhance superconductivity and establish cavity engineering as a powerful platform for tailoring quantum materials.

</details>

**Authors:** Daniel Boyanovsky

**Summary.** The paper analyzes the time evolution of dynamical axion quasiparticles coupled to a photon bath using open quantum system formalisms. By deriving and comparing results from a Quantum Master Equation (QME) with those from quantum many-body linear response, it reveals non-Markovian transient effects like anti-Zeno dynamics. This work provides a unified theoretical framework for understanding dissipation in topological materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it tackles non-equilibrium dynamics in topological systems by bridging two powerful theoretical tools—open quantum system master equations and many-body linear response theory—to study transient phenomena.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the non-equilibrium dynamics of emergent dynamical axion quasiparticles (DAQ) coupled to a photon bath via a Chern-Simons term, treating it as an open quantum system.

**Main result.** The analysis establishes a direct bridge between the Quantum Master Equation (QME) approach and quantum many-body linear response theory, revealing transient non-Markovian effects like anti-Zeno dynamics.

**Method.** The authors derive and analyze the time evolution using a QME derived with only a partial Markov approximation, comparing it rigorously to results from quantum many-body linear response theory.

**Model / system.** The system involves DAQ quasiparticles coupled to an electromagnetic bath via a Chern-Simons term. The dynamics are modeled by deriving and solving the reduced density matrix evolution equation (QME).

**Key observables.** Coherences, population of the DAQ condensate, topological susceptibility ($\chi_t$), and the expectation value of the induced Chern-Simons density.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Transient time scales are crucial; early time dynamics exhibit quantum anti-Zeno effects, violating Fermi's Golden rule and detailed balance.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary approximations include using a partial Markov approximation in the QME and assuming weak coupling ($g^2$ order) when applying factorization techniques from linear response theory.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution of rate integrals, showing the transition from early-time oscillatory behavior to long-time secular growth consistent with different theoretical limits.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first setting up the open quantum system framework (QME), then deriving equivalent equations using linear response theory, and finally analyzing the resulting time evolution in multiple regimes (transient vs. long-time) to confirm consistency across methods.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the non-equilibrium dynamics of emergent dynamical axion quasiparticles (DAQ) coupled to a photon bath in equilibrium via a Chern-Simons term as a quantum open system. A quantum master equation (QME) is derived up to second order in this coupling implementing only a \emph{partial} Markov approximation, allowing time dependent rates in the Lindblad (QME). These are determined by the equilibrium correlation functions of the Chern-Simons density, and their time dependence allows us to explore transient dynamics in coherences and population: the formation of the quasiparticle on short time scales and its decay, and the build-up of population with an effective time dependent rate. Early time evolution features quantum \emph{anti} Zeno dynamics with enhanced quasiparticle decay and population growth. These phenomena describe transient violations of Fermi's Golden rule and of \emph{detailed balance}, and are distinct \emph{non-Markovian} effects directly related to the spectral density of the Chern-Simons correlators. We obtain the equation of motion of coherent (DAQ) condensates both with the (QME) and with quantum many body linear response establishing a direct bridge between both methods. As a corollary we obtain the expectation value of the Chern-Simons density \emph{induced} by a (DAQ) condensate in linear response, the topological susceptibility is shown to be proportional to the (DAQ) many body self-energy. We provide a Feynman diagram-based interpretation of approximations invoked in the (QME) and corrections from system-bath correlations in higher order.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically models the control of magnetic domain walls in superconductor/ferromagnetic insulator bilayers using supercurrents. It shows that the superconducting state allows for low-power, measurable motion of these walls by generating an equilibrium superfluid momentum. This work is significant for developing energy-efficient memory elements for cryogenic quantum computing applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a detailed theoretical framework connecting superconducting phenomena (supercurrents, superfluid momentum) directly to spintronic memory elements (domain walls), offering insights into energy-efficient quantum/cryogenic device operation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to develop a fast, reliable magnetic memory technology for cryogenic computing by controlling magnetic domain walls using superconducting supercurrents.

**Main result.** Supercurrent-driven motion of domain walls is predicted, generating a measurable voltage and dissipating power orders of magnitude lower than in normal state setups. The mechanism relies on the generation of an equilibrium superfluid momentum ($q_0$).

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced theoretical techniques including quasiclassical Green's functions (Keldysh formalism), coupled action formulations for superconducting and magnetic systems, and solving coupled differential equations for domain wall dynamics.

**Model / system.** The system is a superconductor/ferromagnetic insulator bilayer. The physics involves coupling between supercurrent-induced spin accumulation (via ISGE) and the magnetization dynamics described by modified LLG equations.

**Key observables.** Domain wall velocity ($v_{dw}$), measurable voltage ($V \propto q_0 v_{dw}$), dissipated power ($P$), and temperature increase ($\Delta T$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Supercurrent density ($j$), superconducting gap ($\Delta$), spin-orbit coupling strength, Gilbert damping parameter ($\alpha$), and the equilibrium superfluid momentum ($q_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes an adiabatic limit for motion (low speed) and that the FI is much thinner than the SC to ensure spin-charge coupling dominates over Oersted fields.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the physical setup, domain wall dynamics under varying currents relative to a critical current ($I_W$), snapshots of superfluid momentum during motion, and theoretical derivations relating voltage/power to velocity.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the fundamental coupling mechanisms (ISGE, $q_0$ generation) to deriving the coupled equations of motion for domain wall coordinates. It then analyzes the resulting dynamics, power balance ($P=D$), and finally addresses thermal stability by calculating temperature increases due to dissipation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Establishing a versatile, fast and reliable magnetic memory technology is a giant bottleneck for cryogenic computing since present-day room-temperature solutions either cease to work or consume too much power. The long-term goal of superconducting spintronics has been to overcome this bottleneck by generating magnetic memories with equal-spin triplet supercurrent driven through them to control their magnetization direction. This path has been hampered by the short spin relaxation length and strong anisotropy in ferromagnets. Here we show how the supercurrent driven generation of spin accumulation in a superconductor/magnetic insulator bilayer, together with Gilbert damping of magnetization lead to a motion of magnetic domain walls. This manifests as a local voltage across the wall, which allows its position to be identified. Associated with this voltage and the current, there is Joule power which is dissipated via the Gilbert damping. The power required to maintain domain wall motion is orders of magnitude smaller than in the normal state, where most of the power is wasted in producing the current.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the unique 'intermediate state' found in 3D superconductors under applied electric fields. By combining experiments on Nb films with Ginzburg-Landau theory, the authors characterize a nonclassical regime where superconducting supercurrent coexists with measurable dissipative charge transport. This work provides insight into how quantum fluids behave when driven far from thermodynamic equilibrium.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work explores non-equilibrium dynamics in superconductors, which is a key area bridging condensed matter physics with open quantum systems concepts. The analysis of current decomposition into supercurrent and dissipative components touches upon energy dissipation mechanisms far from equilibrium.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the non-equilibrium electronic state of three-dimensional superconductors when subjected to finite electric fields, focusing on an 'intermediate state' where supercurrent and dissipative charge transport coexist.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate the existence of an Electric-Field-Driven Intermediate State (EFDIS) characterized by linear segments in I-V curves, suggesting a nonclassical regime distinct from simple parallel conduction models.

**Method.** The research combines experimental measurements on superconducting films with theoretical modeling based on Ginzburg-Landau theory to analyze order parameter fluctuations under bias.

**Model / system.** The system is pristine, unpatterned 3D superconducting films (Nb). The analysis uses the Ginzburg-Landau framework, simplifying the problem to a one-dimensional variation of the order parameter $\psi(x)$ along the length $L$.

**Key observables.** Resistance ($R$), effective conductance ($	ext{g}_{	ext{eff}}$), supercurrent intercept ($I_S$), and the relationship between voltage drop ($v$) and the rate of change of the wavevector ($k$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coherence length ($\xi$), applied magnetic field ($H$), temperature ($T$), and source voltage ($U_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes small magnetic fields, neglects the vector potential $\mathbf{A}$ in some derivations, and models the system as having alternating regions of IS and SC.

**Figures summary.** Figures show I-V curves under applied voltage/field, illustrating distinct linear segments corresponding to the EFDIS. Plots also quantify the dependence of $I_S$ and $	ext{R}_{	ext{eff}}$ on bias voltage.

**Paper structure.** The paper moves from experimental observation (measuring $I$-$V$ curves) to theoretical modeling using Ginzburg-Landau theory, deriving relationships between voltage drop and order parameter fluctuations ($v \propto |	ext{d}k/	ext{d}t|$).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The coexistence of superconductivity and finite electric fields may enable access to intriguing forms of electronic states. We demonstrate the emergence of an intermediate state in which electric fields penetrate the system while superconductivity still persists. Our measurements reveal a nonclassical regime characterized by the simultaneous presence of supercurrent and dissipative charge transport. This state, realized in a pristine unpatterned three-dimensional system, arises from electric-field-driven order parameter fluctuations. It provides a platform to explore dissipative states of charged quantum fluids far from equilibrium.

</details>

**Summary.** The study investigates the ultrafast dynamics of Josephson plasma oscillations in cuprate superconductors by analyzing their response to frequency-modulated terahertz radiation. By fitting spectral sidebands with models incorporating a finite synchronization time ($	au_s$), the authors quantify the non-equilibrium plasmon lifetime, providing deep insight into the coupled electrodynamics of these superconducting junctions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges solid-state superconductivity (high-Tc cuprates) with non-equilibrium quantum dynamics, using THz spectroscopy as a powerful probe for collective plasma oscillations and relaxation processes in strongly correlated materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to perform a time-resolved synchronization analysis of stacked intrinsic Josephson junctions (IJJs) in a cuprate superconductor when subjected to frequency-modulated terahertz radiation spectra.

**Main result.** The analysis successfully estimates a synchronized relaxation time ($	au_s \simeq 0.28	ext{ ns}$) by analyzing spectral transformations, revealing sub-nanosecond non-equilibrium dynamics of the coupled Josephson plasma.

**Method.** The researchers used frequency-modulated terahertz spectroscopy on $	ext{Bi}_2	ext{Sr}_2	ext{CaCu}_2	ext{O}_{8+δ}$ IJJs, comparing measured sideband spectra to theoretical models incorporating a finite relaxation time ($	au_s$).

**Model / system.** The physical system is a Josephson Plasma Emitter (JPE) formed by stacked intrinsic Josephson junctions in the cuprate superconductor $	ext{Bi}_2	ext{Sr}_2	ext{CaCu}_2	ext{O}_{8+δ}$, coupled with external patch antennas. The dynamics are governed by nonlinear oscillator behavior exhibiting AC Josephson effect excitation.

**Key observables.** Unmodulated intensity distribution ($I_{\mathrm{UM}}(\omega)$), frequency-modulated spectra ($I_{\mathrm{FM}}(\omega)$), and the spectral intensity ratio $\eta(	ext{fm}, 
u_c)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Synchronization relaxation time ($	au_s$), modulation frequencies ($f_m$ up to $1	ext{ GHz}$), and central resonance frequencies ($
u_c \approx 840-870	ext{ GHz}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the spectral changes observed require introducing a finite relaxation time $	au_s$, which is interpreted as the lifetime of the macroscopic plasmon.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the double Gaussian peak structure in unmodulated spectra, and show how sideband intensities in FM spectra shift and change shape as a function of $f_m$ and assumed $	au_s$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from characterizing the static spectrum ($I_{\mathrm{UM}}(\omega)$) to analyzing the frequency-modulation response. It then introduces theoretical models incorporating finite relaxation times ($	au_s$) to quantitatively reproduce observed spectral transformations across varying $f_m$ regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Terahertz radiation from $\text{Bi}_2\text{Sr}_2\text{CaCu}_2\text{O}_{8+δ}$ intrinsic Josephson junctions (IJJs) provides an ideal platform to study the synchronization of a macroscopic quantum system. Here, we present a spectral analysis of a frequency-modulated Josephson plasma emitter coupled with patch antennas. In the unmodulated intensity distribution as a function of radiation frequency $I_{\mathrm{UM}}(ω)$, we observe a double Gaussian peak structure. Crucially, double-peak spectra obtained at a constant bias voltage imply either a rapid temporal distribution of resonances or their simultaneous excitation, driven by the mutual electromagnetic coupling between the IJJ mesa and the antennas. At low modulation frequencies $f_m$, the spectra are well reproduced by the products of $I_{\mathrm{UM}}(ω)$ and frequency combs, yielding a synchronized relaxation time $τ_s \simeq 0.28\text{ ns}$. Incorporating $τ_s$ quantitatively reproduces a drastic spectral transformation observed around $f_m \sim 1\text{ GHz}$, unveiling the sub-nanosecond non-equilibrium dynamics of coupled Josephson plasma.

</details>

**Authors:** Sampurna Karmakar, Aziz Hasan, Sourin Das  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.19310v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `exotic spin models in light-matter` **3/5** · `spintronics-quantum-optics interface` **3/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **2/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **2/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **2/5** · `Frenkel-Kontorova` **1/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5**

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that the statistical nature of charge carriers fundamentally limits the maximum power achievable by quantum heat engines. By modeling magnon transport and incorporating Haldane's fractional exclusion statistics, the authors show that bosonic behavior significantly enhances this limit over fermionic predictions, establishing statistical exclusion as a tunable thermodynamic resource.

**Why it may be interesting.** It connects fundamental concepts of quantum statistics (bosonic/fermionic nature) directly to macroscopic thermodynamic performance metrics like power output in mesoscopic devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate quantum statistical exclusion as a thermodynamic resource by comparing the maximum power extractable from heat engines operating with different carrier statistics (fermionic vs. bosonic).

**Main result.** Bosonic working media yield strictly enhanced universal maximum power compared to fermionic limits, and Haldane's fractional statistics parameterizes this enhancement continuously.

**Method.** The analysis uses the nonlinear Landauer-Büttiker framework applied to a quantum thermoelectric heat engine model.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using magnon transport through a ferromagnetic spin chain, analyzed via an effective quadratic bosonic tight-binding Hamiltonian in the low-temperature limit.

**Key observables.** Maximum power ($P_{	ext{max}}$), efficiency ($\eta$), and the ratio of maximum power output vs. fermionic bound.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Haldane exclusion statistics parameter ($g$), temperature difference ($T_L - T_R$), and magnetic field strengths defining chemical potentials.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the nonlinear Landauer-Büttiker framework, assuming low temperatures for noninteracting magnon approximation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of the spin chain setup, transmission profiles showing filtering thresholds, and plots comparing power output ratios versus the statistics parameter g.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical bounds using Landauer-Büttiker formalism, compares fermionic vs. bosonic limits, introduces Haldane interpolation, and applies this framework to a magnon transport model to quantify the enhancement.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The maximum power extractable from a quantum thermoelectric heat engine operating with free fermion carriers is bounded by the universal Whitney limit, $P_{\text{fermion}}^{\max} \simeq 0.0321π^2 k_B^2(T_L-T_R)^2/h$. We demonstrate that this bound is not fundamental to quantum heat engines but is instead an artifact of fermionic statistics. Within the nonlinear Landauer-Büttiker framework, a bosonic working medium yields a strictly enhanced universal maximum power, $P_{\text{boson}}^{\max} = (\ln 2)^2\, k_B^2(T_L-T_R)^2/h$, exceeding the fermionic limit by a factor of $(\ln 2)^2/(0.0321π^2) \approx 1.52$. We propose magnon transport through a ferromagnetic spin chain as an experimentally viable bosonic realization. Incorporating Haldane fractional exclusion statistics with parameter $g$ provides a continuous interpolation between the bosonic ($g = 0$) and fermionic ($g = 1$) limits, revealing a monotonic enhancement of maximum power for $g < 1$ at reduced bias cost. These results establish quantum statistical exclusion as a previously unrecognized and independently tunable thermodynamic resource, opening performance regimes inaccessible to conventional carrier-engineering approaches.

</details>

**Summary.** This work explores how antiferromagnetic skyrmions exhibit bidirectional motion when subjected to competing spin torques (STT and SOT). By analyzing the force balance governing this motion, the authors demonstrate that a critical current threshold causes the skyrmion direction to reverse. This controllable mechanism is then leveraged to propose and validate programmable logic gates based on magnetic racetrack devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on spintronics rather than fundamental quantum information, the study deals with non-equilibrium dynamics of topological textures driven by external currents, which shares conceptual links with open quantum systems modeling where dissipation and driving fields are crucial.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the previously unexplored bidirectional dynamics of antiferromagnetic skyrmions when driven by competing spin-transfer torque (STT) and spin-orbit torque (SOT). The goal is to utilize this mechanism for developing programmable logic devices.

**Main result.** It was computationally demonstrated that AFM skyrmion motion can reverse direction above a critical current threshold. This bidirectional motion, tunable by an external magnetic field, enables the design of functional logic gates.

**Method.** The dynamics are analyzed using micromagnetic simulations solving the LLG equation and supplemented by the Thiele approach for analytical understanding of the force balance.

**Model / system.** The system models antiferromagnetic skyrmions in a structure coupled with a heavy metal layer to induce interfacial Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction. The dynamics are governed by current-induced STT and SOT forces acting on the topological spin texture.

**Key observables.** Skyrmion velocity ($v_x$), force components ($F_{STT}, F_{SOT}$), skyrmion radius ($R_s$), and logic gate output states (e.g., AND, OR, XOR).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Driving current density ($j$), out-of-plane magnetic field ($B_z$), and the relative strengths of STT vs. SOT torques.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the Thiele equation approximation for describing the collective dynamics, and it assumes that logic operation limits can be controlled by local current modulation factors.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate velocity reversal ($v_x$ vs $j$) showing positive to negative changes; force balance plots show sign reversals in net force; and schematics demonstrate AND/OR/XOR gate functionality based on current ranges.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical model and uses simulations to observe bidirectional motion. It then analyzes the underlying physics using the Thiele equation, showing that the reversal stems from competing torques affecting skyrmion size. Finally, it applies this understanding to design and demonstrate programmable logic gates in a racetrack geometry.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Antiferromagnetic skyrmions are swirling topological spin textures with rich dynamics and intriguing transport properties, yet their bidirectional dynamics remain largely unexplored. Here, we investigate the dynamics of antiferromagnetic skyrmions driven by current-induced spin-transfer and spin-orbit torques. We computationally demonstrate that antiferromagnetic skyrmions moving in one direction at low current densities can reverse their motion direction when the driving current is above a threshold. Based on the Thiele approach analysis, we show that this bidirectional motion originates from a change in the relative strengths of two effective forces arising from spin-transfer and spin-orbit torques. Furthermore, exploiting this bidirectional motion on a single racetrack, we design programmable logic gates. Our results not only uncover a hidden mechanism for bidirectional skyrmion motion but also facilitate the development of antiferromagnet-based logic devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes how a spatially varying interlayer bias creates localized 'kink states' in bilayer graphene under a magnetic field. These states host persistent currents composed of drift and cyclotron components, which combine to form a controllable orbital magnetization channel. This mechanism suggests a pathway for realizing valley-based electronic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter physics, the concept of controlling localized topological edge/junction modes via external fields and biases mirrors principles used in designing quantum interconnects or realizing artificial gauge fields relevant to AMO systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the observable electric and magnetic properties of localized electron states, known as 'kink states,' that form along a junction where the interlayer bias in bilayer graphene changes sign.

**Main result.** These kink states support persistent currents composed of both drift and circulating components, which together create a controllable, quasi-one-dimensional channel of orbital magnetization useful for valley electronics.

**Method.** The analysis involves diagonalizing the effective Hamiltonian derived from the low-energy physics of pseudo-zero modes in momentum space, using techniques like oscillator basis expansions and unitary transformations.

**Model / system.** The system is bilayer graphene subjected to a perpendicular magnetic field ($B_z$) and an interlayer bias ($\mu(y)$) that varies spatially across a junction. The low-energy physics is captured by analyzing the pseudo-zero mode (PZM) levels.

**Key observables.** Persistent current (total, drift $j^{(d)}$, circulating $j^{(c)}$), localized orbital magnetization $\langle M_z angle$, and energy spectra ($\hat{e}_n(y_0)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interlayer bias magnitude ($\mu$), magnetic field ($B$), junction width ($w_{jc}$), and Fermi level position ($\epsilon_F$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the system is in the quantum Hall regime, and approximations are made regarding the relative strength of different coupling terms (e.g., $|e_0| = \mu \ll e_2$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the sample geometry with the bias profile $\mu(y)$, show the evolution of PZM spectra across the junction, and depict the resulting trapped active electron modes.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically derives the effective Hamiltonian for the kink states, analyzes the energy spectrum's gap opening/avoidance, and then calculates the associated current components (drift vs. circulating) to demonstrate the net persistent magnetization channel.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In a magnetic field bilayer graphene hosts an octet of pseudo-zero-mode electron levels nearly degenerate in orbitals $n=(0,1)$, valleys and spins. They split in valleys by electrostatic gating. In gated bilayer graphene, in which the interlayer bias is set up to flip sign across a line, one has a line junction that traps a portion of pseudo-zero-mode electrons inside the insulating bulk band gap, giving rise to electron states localized along the junction, known as kink states. A close look is made into the spectra and electromagnetic response of such kink states. There are two species of valley current associated with them, a drift current driven by the bias gradient and a circulating current coming from cyclotron motion. It turns out that they both flow in essentially the same direction, with the circulating current exhibiting a magnetic character distinct from those of other higher levels. In equilibrium they spatially circulate within the kink states, creating a quasi-one-dimensional channel of orbital magnetization. The electric control of the orbital magnetization and valley currents via a network of gated junctions will find useful applications in valley electronics.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper develops an effective field theory for the interaction between magnons and quantized lattice dislocations in magnetic insulators. By analyzing the coupling strength based on dislocation topology (screw vs. edge), the authors predict distinct, observable signatures like helicity-selective modes or finite spin ellipticity. This highlights topological defects as controllable sources of novel quantum dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete example of how topological defects can introduce novel, geometrically constrained coupling channels into collective excitations (magnons), offering new avenues to control spin dynamics beyond simple bulk interactions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates spin dynamics in ordered magnetic insulators when coupled to topological lattice defects, specifically dislocations (dislons). The goal is to develop a unified theoretical framework describing this magnon-dislon hybridization.

**Main result.** The coupling mechanism is highly dependent on the dislocation type: screw dislocations exhibit helicity-selective coupling and dark modes, while edge dislocations generate finite spin-precession ellipticity due to the glide constraint. This establishes defects as sources of observable polarization fingerprints in magnon spectra.

**Method.** The authors develop an effective field theory using fracton–elasticity duality, recasting the dynamics into a gauge field framework. They calculate the coupled excitations by integrating out the elastic gauge field to derive a mixed magnon-dislon vertex.

**Model / system.** The system is a ferromagnetic crystal containing embedded dislocations. The model uses an effective action $S_{	ext{eff}}$ describing the coupling between spin fluctuations (magnons) and dislocation vibrations (dislons), treated as coupled bosonic excitations.

**Key observables.** Magnon–dislon hybridization gap ($\Delta h$), polarization fingerprints, and finite spin-precession ellipticity ($E_n$) for edge dislocations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling strength $g$, the momentum transfer $\kappa$, and the distinction between screw vs. edge dislocation geometries are crucial parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Approximations include linear order in small transverse dislon fields, assuming a strict quasi-one-dimensional limit for calculations, and using weak-coupling expansions near resonance.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the spectral weights showing avoided crossings for screw dislocations and demonstrate the anisotropic coupling leading to elliptical polarization for edge dislocations.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from deriving the full magnetoelastic action via gauge fields, calculating the propagator components, deriving the mixed vertex by integrating out the elastic field, and finally analyzing the resulting coupled quadratic Hamiltonian for both screw and edge geometries.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spin dynamics in ordered magnets with topological lattice defects is investigated. Using fracton--elasticity duality, we develop an effective field theory of magnons coupled to quantized lattice dislocations (dislons) in magnetic insulators. Within this framework, an elastic gauge field mediates a nonlocal interaction between dislocations and magnetization gradients. The resulting magnetoelastic coupling gives rise to coherent magnon-dislon hybridization whose properties are dictated by dislocation topology. Screw dislocations exhibit helicity-selective hybridization and symmetry-protected dark dislon sectors, while edge dislocations generate anisotropic hybrid excitations with finite spin-precession ellipticity through the glide constraint. Our results establish dislocations as dynamical topological defects with directly observable polarization fingerprints in magnon spectra, and reveal magnon-dislon hybridization as a new route to control spin dynamics.

</details>

(b) The chiral system develops an orbital magnetization M (orange arrow) due to current flow—i.e. this is the OEE. **Summary.** This paper develops a non-equilibrium theory to explain the Chirality-Induced Spin Selectivity (CISS) effect by connecting it mathematically to the Orbital Edelstein Effect (OEE). The authors show that current flow induces an orbital magnetization ($\mathbf{M}$) in chiral materials, and this $\mathbf{M}$ then generates a measurable spin polarization ($P_{\parallel}$). This establishes CISS as a direct consequence of the material's inherent chiro-optical response.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it connects fundamental concepts from spintronics (CISS) with linear response phenomena (OEE), providing a theoretical mechanism for generating spin polarization without relying on traditional magnetic materials, which has implications for quantum transport modeling.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to provide a microscopic theoretical explanation for the Chirality-Induced Spin Selectivity (CISS) effect by establishing a link between CISS and the Orbital Edelstein Effect (OEE).

**Main result.** The theory successfully demonstrates that the current-induced orbital magnetization ($\mathbf{M}$) generates an effective Zeeman splitting, which in turn causes a finite spin polarization ($P_{\parallel}$), thereby linking CISS to intrinsic chiro-optical properties like natural optical activity.

**Method.** The authors employ a non-equilibrium transport theory framework, utilizing a quasi-equilibrium assumption and analyzing the system's response via small-drift expansions of the spin-resolved distribution function.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on chiral electronic systems (conductors) subjected to an external electric field ($\mathbf{E}$), modeling the transport using concepts derived from the Orbital Edelstein Effect and incorporating chirality parameters ($\zeta$).

**Key observables.** Finite spin polarization ($P_{\parallel}$), orbital magnetization ($\mathbf{M}$), and the resulting non-linear, spin-polarized current components.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The theory is sensitive to the ratio of scattering times ($	au_{ee} \ll 	au_{mr}$) and depends on chirality ($\zeta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include reaching a quasi-equilibrium state under a finite drift current density, using the local/static limit for OEE calculations, and employing small-drift expansions.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the three sequential physical steps: (a) generation of the drift current ($\mathbf{j}_d$); (b) induction of orbital magnetization ($\mathbf{M}$) via OEE; and (c) coupling between $\mathbf{M}$ and spin leading to $P_{\parallel}$.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds logically by first introducing the CISS effect, then proposing the theoretical bridge through the OEE, deriving the necessary non-equilibrium transport equations, and finally calculating the resulting spin polarization in terms of system chirality.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The chirality-induced spin selectivity (CISS) effect, discovered by Naaman and collaborators in 1999, describes the emergence of a finite spin polarization in response to current flow through a chiral electronic system. While extensive experimental studies have verified the presence of CISS in molecular systems and, more recently, in chiral materials, a complete microscopic understanding of this effect remains elusive. In this work, we propose a theoretical framework linking the CISS effect to the orbital Edelstein effect. In the latter, a drive current induces a finite orbital magnetization, even in the absence of spin-orbit coupling. Our non-equilibrium theory naturally explains key features of the CISS effect: its persistence in systems with weak or vanishingly small spin-orbit coupling and its connection to natural optical activity, a distinctive signature of chiral systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes supratransmission in lattices where coupling is purely nonlinear, meaning no standard linear pass band exists. Using asymptotic methods, the authors derive a discrete p-Schrödinger equation to model energy transport. They find that while weak coupling suggests thresholding via bifurcations, strong coupling indicates the onset of transmission is governed by spectral instability rather than simple amplitude thresholds.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a detailed theoretical framework for energy transport in nonlinear wave systems lacking linear band structures. The distinction between fold-induced vs. instability-induced thresholds is highly relevant to understanding non-integrable, strongly coupled physical media.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates supratransmission—energy transport outside the linear pass band—in nonlinear lattices where coupling is purely nonlinear, meaning no underlying linear spectrum exists.

**Main result.** The authors establish that supratransmission occurs in these fully nonlinear systems, identifying that the mechanism shifts between fold bifurcations (weak coupling) and instability of the lower stationary branch (strong coupling).

**Method.** The analysis employs asymptotic reduction to derive a discrete p-Schrödinger (DpS) equation, supplemented by perturbation theory for analytical approximations and numerical continuation/stability analysis.

**Model / system.** The model is based on nonlinear lattices governed by power-law interaction potentials, leading to equations like the discrete sine-Gordon model. The coupling is purely nonlinear ($\varphi(x)$), contrasting with systems possessing linear spectral components.

**Key observables.** Supratransmission (energy propagation at frequencies outside the linear pass band), critical driving amplitude ($\mathcal{F}_{crit}$ or $	ilde{a}_{cr}$), and stability eigenvalues ($\lambda$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Nonlinearity exponent ($p$ or $ho$), coupling strength ($C$ or $C_b$), driving frequency ($\omega$), and the approximation regime (weak vs. strong coupling).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary approximations involve deriving the DpS equation for weak/intermediate coupling, which is noted to fail in the strong-coupling limit where instability mechanisms dominate.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate spatiotemporal evolution comparing linear ($ho=1$) and nonlinear ($ho=3$) couplings below and above threshold. Other figures compare numerically computed critical amplitudes against asymptotic predictions based on different coupling strengths.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the model (nonlinear lattices), deriving approximations like the DpS equation, analyzing supratransmission thresholds using bifurcation theory (weak coupling), and finally contrasting these results with numerical findings in the strong-coupling regime where instability is suggested as the true mechanism.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Supratransmission is examined in nonlinear lattices with purely nonlinear coupling, extending the phenomenon to systems that lack a linear pass band. In contrast to standard lattices with mixed linear-nonlinear interactions, the present model has no linear spectrum, so energy propagation arises entirely from nonlinear effects. Asymptotic analysis yields a discrete $p$-Schrödinger (DpS) equation that {provides an accurate description in the weak- and intermediate-coupling regimes and offers qualitative insight in the strong-coupling regime}. Perturbation provides analytical approximations for the critical driving amplitude, explicitly showing its dependence on the driving frequency, coupling strength, and the nonlinearity exponent $p$. The analysis identifies a non-trivial dependence of the critical amplitude on $p$, with distinct trends in different coupling regimes. Numerical continuation and direct simulations {validate the theory in regimes where the asymptotic reduction is applicable and show good agreement across a wide range of parameters}. The results establish supratransmission in fully nonlinear lattices and clarify the associated energy-transport mechanisms, with relevance to mechanical lattices, tunable metamaterials, and nonlinear optical arrays.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a comprehensive theory for calculating the in-plane orbital magnetization and susceptibility in layered quantum materials. It addresses the unique 'transdimensional' regime where interlayer coupling is crucial, leading to an orbital response absent in simpler 2D or bulk models. The key prediction is the realization of a gate-tunable in-plane magnetoelectric effect.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it extends fundamental concepts of orbital magnetism into complex low-dimensional heterostructures, providing theoretical tools for designing novel 'orbitronic' devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a theory for calculating the in-plane orbital magnetization ($\mathbf{M}_{\parallel}^{	ext{orb}}$) and susceptibility ($\chi^{ab}_{	ext{orb}}$) in multilayer quantum systems subjected to an in-plane magnetic field.

**Main result.** A systematic framework is established that predicts a gate-tunable in-plane orbital magnetoelectric effect in layered materials, which is distinct from conventional 3D or ideal 2D responses.

**Method.** The theory uses the current-loop picture to construct the in-plane orbital angular momentum operator and derives expressions for magnetization and susceptibility using modern theory concepts like Berry curvature contributions.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on layered materials, including few-layer graphene and moiré heterostructures, modeled by $N$-layer square-lattice Hamiltonians with interlayer hopping terms. The analysis covers the 'transdimensional' regime where layer thickness is comparable to the vertical mean free path.

**Key observables.** In-plane orbital magnetic moment ($\hat{\mathbf{m}}_{\parallel}$), in-plane orbital magnetization ($\mathbf{M}_{	ext{orb}}^{\parallel}$), and in-plane orbital magnetic susceptibility ($\chi_{ab}^{	ext{orb}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Layer thickness ($d$), interlayer hopping parameters ($\gamma, g_1, g_2$), external gate electric field ($U_d$), and the ratio of layer thickness to mean free path ($d/l_z$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory is most applicable in the transdimensional regime where coherent interlayer tunneling survives; some calculations use continuum limit approximations for specific systems like TBG.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dimensional crossover of orbital moments across 2D, 3D, and transdimensional regimes. Other figures show model validation of $\chi^{xx}_{	ext{orb}}$ vs $E_F$ and demonstrate gate control over the in-plane magnetoelectric response ($K_{xy}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first constructing the in-plane orbital magnetic moment operator from a current-loop picture. It then derives exact expressions for magnetization and susceptibility, applying these to predict phenomena like the magnetoelectric effect and magnetoresistance in specific layered models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The modern theory of orbital magnetization successfully describes the response of Bloch electrons to magnetic fields in fully periodic crystals, but it does not directly address the distinct regime of an in-plane field in multilayer systems with layer hybridization. Coherent interlayer tunneling allows electrons to form circulating current loops, producing an in-plane orbital response that is absent in a strictly two-dimensional limit and qualitatively different from the conventional three-dimensional one. Here we develop a theory of in-plane orbital magnetization for this {\it transdimensional} regime, where the layer thickness is comparable to the vertical mean free path. Starting from the current-loop picture, we construct the in-plane orbital angular momentum operator and derive exact expressions for the orbital magnetic moment and the in-plane orbital magnetic susceptibility. As an application, we predict a gate-tunable in-plane orbital magnetoelectric effect in layered materials. Our framework establishes a general foundation for in-plane orbital responses and suggests new opportunities for orbitronics in layer-hybridized quantum materials.

</details>

**Summary.** The researchers investigated Coulomb charging effects in a quantum Hall cavity using QPCs. They demonstrated experimentally that the charging energy is not static but varies significantly with the magnetic field when the constrictions are strongly coupled. This modulation proves that the QPC barriers act as dynamic electrostatic elements, providing crucial corrections for interpreting advanced measurements like those seeking evidence of anyons.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly challenges simplified electrostatic models in mesoscopic quantum systems, showing that interaction effects (via FQH states) dynamically modify fundamental parameters like charging energy, which has implications for interpreting anyonic statistics measurements.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Interpreting interference signals in quantum Hall interferometers is complicated by Coulomb charging effects, which are often incorrectly treated as static properties.

**Main result.** The charging energy in the cavity is strongly and non-monotonically modulated by the magnetic field, varying up to 60% over a small field range.

**Method.** Differential conductance measurements were used on gate-defined quantum Hall cavities tuned to the Coulomb-dominated regime. The charging energy was extracted from the voltage bias at the apex of measured Coulomb diamonds.

**Model / system.** The system is a quantum Hall cavity fabricated on a GaAs/AlGaAs heterostructure, operating in the fractional quantum Hall regime. Transport is analyzed by tuning Quantum Point Contacts (QPCs) that act as active electrostatic elements controlling coupling to leads.

**Key observables.** Charging energy ($E_C$), differential conductance ($	ext{d}I/	ext{d}V_s$), and Coulomb blockade resonances.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic field ($B$) modulation (up to 60% variation over $100 	ext{ mT}$), strong cavity-to-lead coupling regime, and the formation of incompressible fractional quantum Hall states ($
u=2/3$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that QPC constrictions are active electrostatic elements whose local compressibility dictates the effective capacitance between the cavity and leads.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate device setups, comparisons between weak and strong coupling regimes showing Coulomb diamonds, and plots detailing $E_C$ vs. $B$, correlating modulation with FQH state formation in QPCs.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the problem of static charging energy assumptions, presents measurements demonstrating non-monotonic $E_C(B)$ dependence only when coupling is strong, and attributes this to dynamic screening mechanisms governed by local compressibility changes within the constrictions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Electronic Fabry-Pérot interferometers (FPIs) operating in the fractional quantum Hall regime are a key platform for probing anyonic braiding statistics, yet interpreting their interference signals is complicated by Coulomb charging effects, which are commonly treated as parasitic, static properties governed by the cavity's geometry and electrostatics. Here, using a gate-defined quantum Hall cavity tuned to the Coulomb-dominated regime, we demonstrate that the charging energy is in fact strongly and non-monotonically modulated by the magnetic field, varying by up to 60% over a range of only 100 mT. The effect appears exclusively when the quantum point contacts (QPCs) forming the cavity are weakly pinched off, i.e., in the strong cavity-to-lead coupling regime. By correlating the charging energy modulation with the QPC magneto-conductance, we attribute this behavior to field-dependent changes in local compressibility and electrostatic screening between the cavity and the leads, driven by the formation of incompressible fractional quantum Hall states within the constrictions. This result establishes QPC constrictions of quantum Hall cavities as active electrostatic elements rather than passive boundaries, revealing a dynamic screening mechanism, with direct consequences for the interpretation of interference measurements and the extraction of anyonic statistics.

</details>
