**Summary.** This paper investigates localized wave phenomena on the FPUT lattice by employing a quasi-continuum approximation derived from the modified KdV equation. By using exact solutions of the mKdV equation to initialize the discrete lattice, the authors demonstrate that this reduction accurately models various structures like solitons and rogue waves across different background states. This methodology provides a powerful tool for studying wave dynamics in nonlinear lattices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges the gap between continuum integrable models (like mKdV) and discrete physical systems (FPUT lattice), a common theme in nonlinear physics relevant to understanding wave propagation in structured materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work analyzes localized wave patterns, such as rogue waves, solitons, and breathers, that can exist within the dynamics of the FPUT lattice.

**Main result.** The study successfully demonstrates that exact solutions derived from the modified KdV equation can be used to construct initial conditions for the FPUT lattice, showing good quantitative agreement between numerical simulations and analytical predictions.

**Method.** A quasi-continuum approximation is employed by deriving a reduced integrable equation (mKdV) from the discrete FPUT lattice model. The exact solutions of this continuum equation are then mapped back to initialize and validate time evolution on the discrete lattice.

**Model / system.** The primary system is the Fermi–Pasta–Ulam–Tsingou (FPUT) lattice, governed by a nonlinear chain interaction potential. This discrete model is approximated by the continuous modified Korteweg-de Vries (mKdV) equation under specific assumptions ($K_3=0$).

**Key observables.** Rogue-wave profiles, solitons, breathers, and the relative $L_\infty$ error ($\epsilon(t)$) quantifying the agreement between numerical FPUT dynamics and analytical mKdV solutions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The smallness parameter $\epsilon \ll 1$, which governs the validity of the multi-scale expansion; and elliptic moduli ($k$) used to define various background wave states (snoidal, dnoidal, cnoidal).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes $K_3=0$ ($eta$-FPUT model) for the reduction to mKdV, and relies on formal smallness parameters $\epsilon \ll 1$ for the continuum approximation.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare numerical FPUT dynamics initialized via the method against analytical second-order rational solutions; other figures plot the relative $L_\infty$ error across different solution orders and background types (e.g., dark breathers on snoidal backgrounds).

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by deriving the mKdV reduction from the FPUT lattice, then utilizing exact mKdV solutions to set initial conditions for the discrete system. It systematically tests this approach by examining localized waves—solitons, breathers, and rogue waves—on various background states (homogeneous, snoidal, dnoidal, cnoidal).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In the present work, we present a number of localized wave patterns that are theoretically analyzed and   numerically illustrated to be observable within the   widely applicable paradigm of the FPUT lattice. In particular, we derive a modified KdV equation from the FPUT lattice, which admits a variety of localized waves including these exact rational solutions representing rogue-wave profiles, solitons and breathers on the top of not only homogeneous,   but also periodic elliptic function traveling-wave background. We utilize these exact solutions of the modified KdV reduction to construct consistent initial conditions for the FPUT lattice and perform time stepping of the latter. Relevant comparisons between these numerical solutions of the FPUT lattice and their associated analytical counterparts have been conducted to demonstrate good performance of the derived modified KdV reduction in approximating distinct localized wave structures from the FPUT lattice.   This approach paves the way for importing a number of   quasi-continuum waveforms to the FPUT lattice and the   potential associated physical experiments, including recent   ones in mechanical metamaterials.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work investigates how the topology inherent in Weyl metals dictates the nature of their collective plasmon excitations. It proves that these plasmons carry a topological charge quantified by vorticity ($\zeta$), which manifests as unique optical selection rules, allowing them to interact preferentially with light polarized along specific directions.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between topological invariants (Chern number) and measurable optical properties (polarization selection rules, SHG) is highly relevant for quantum optics and condensed matter theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses the quantum geometric structure and topological properties of plasmons—collective excitations—within Weyl metals.

**Main result.** Weyl metal plasmons are shown to possess a monopole structure and finite vorticity ($\zeta = 2C_W$), leading to unique optical selection rules where they couple selectively to light polarized along the dipole moment $\mathbf{d}$.

**Method.** The analysis employs linear response theory (RPA) applied to the electron-hole pair operators, utilizing concepts from Berry curvature and quantum geometry tensors.

**Model / system.** The system is Weyl metals, modeled by a Hamiltonian describing fermionic spinors. The physics is analyzed through the plasmon polarization bubble $\Pi_R(Q, \omega)$ and its susceptibility tensor $\chi_{ij}(\omega)$.

**Key observables.** Plasmon dispersion ($\omega_Q$), vorticity ($\zeta$), effective dipole moment ($\mathbf{d}$), and optical selection rules for light coupling.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Fermi momentum ($k_F$), plasmon frequency ($\omega_0$), and the topological charge (Chern number $C_W$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the Random Phase Approximation (RPA) level for plasmon calculation, assuming weak light fields and zero temperature in some derivations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the monopole structure of interband components, showing nodal planes ($\zeta=0$) and vortex structures ($\zeta=\pm 2$) related to different plasmon components.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the quantum geometric framework using form factor matrices to calculating the polarization function $\Pi_R$ via integrals. It then analyzes the resulting susceptibility tensor $\chi_{ij}(\omega)$ to derive unique optical selection rules and predict phenomena like Second Harmonic Generation (SHG).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We address the quantum geometric structure of plasmons in Fermi surfaces enclosing a topological charge. We demonstrate that Weyl fermion plasmons have monopole structure, are topological and have a finite vorticity $ζ=2\mathsf{C}_{\text{w}}$, where $\mathsf{C}_{\text{w}}$ is the Chern number of the Fermi surface enclosing the Weyl point. We show that these plasmons selectively couple to light linearly polarized along the plasmon effective dipole moment $\mathbf{d}$, which has quantum geometric origin and points along the direction of the plasmon center of mass momentum $\hat{\mathbf{Q}}$. We suggest that Weyl metal topological plasmons have distinctive optical properties compared to conventional plasmons.

</details>

**Summary.** This work explores how cutting a bulk altermagnetic material into thin films or exposing its surfaces fundamentally changes its magnetic electronic structure. By applying group theory and advanced calculations, the authors show that surface orientation dictates whether the spin splitting is conventional, ferromagnetic-like, or possesses a desirable d-wave symmetry. This demonstrates that surface engineering offers a powerful route to tune quantum magnetic properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism, the concept of controlling electronic properties via boundary conditions (surfaces) by exploiting symmetry breaking is highly relevant to designing quantum materials for spintronics and topological phenomena.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how breaking crystal symmetries at surfaces and in thin films fundamentally alters the electronic structure and spin-splitting patterns of bulk altermagnetic materials.

**Main result.** Symmetry reduction dictates that while some surfaces yield conventional antiferromagnetic behavior, specific orientations (like (210)) can stabilize desirable d-wave spin splitting, suggesting surface engineering is a tunable platform for controlling magnetism.

**Method.** The research combines group-theoretical analysis of symmetry constraints with first-principles electronic structure calculations (DFT/Tight-Binding) to model the projected band structures.

**Model / system.** The system under study is altermagnetic materials, exemplified by CrSb. The theoretical framework analyzes the spin splitting term in the Bloch Hamiltonian based on the material's magnetic octupole moment and the symmetry of the terminating surface plane.

**Key observables.** Spin-split electronic states (momentum-dependent), the symmetry of the spin splitting (e.g., d-wave vs. ferromagnetic-like), and the presence/absence of spin degeneracy in the 2D Brillouin zone.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Surface orientation ((001), (010), (210)), Spin-Orbit Coupling (SOC) strength, and the nature of the altermagnetic order parameter ($\varphi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Initial analyses rely on a bulk-projection picture; some calculations assume fixed atomic positions without structural relaxation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate band structures projected onto surface BZs for different orientations, showing spin degeneracy or specific symmetry splittings (e.g., d-wave splitting confirmed for the (210) plane).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the theoretical dependence on symmetry reduction, then analyzing multiple surfaces ((001), (010), (210)) using group theory and DFT/TB calculations to map out how surface termination dictates the resulting spin texture.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Altermagnets are a newly identified class of magnetic materials that combine vanishing net magnetization within the unit cell with spin-split electronic states. Their theoretical description relies on symmetry properties of the bulk band structure. Surfaces and thin films, however, inherently break these symmetries. Here, we investigate the consequences of such symmetry reduction for the electronic structure of bulk altermagnets near the surface and of thin films. When the surface coincides with a symmetry plane of the bulk altermagnetic order, the resulting two-dimensional Brillouin zone exhibits spin-degenerate bands, corresponding to conventional antiferromagnetic behavior. In all other cases, the symmetry of the altermagnetic order is reduced, leading to modified spin splitting. Remarkably, we discover a thin-film geometry of a $g$-wave altermagnet with a particular surface orientation that enables a $d$-wave spin splitting, which is commonly accompanied by the spin-splitter effect, suggesting the functionalization of non-$d$-wave altermagnets by surfaces. Our findings demonstrate that symmetry breaking at surfaces and in thin films fundamentally reshapes altermagnetic spin textures, providing a tunable platform for controlling spin-dependent electronic phenomena.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel tomography technique to extract detailed electronic properties, such as the density matrix and Quantum Geometric Tensor, from standard STM QPI maps. By exploiting crystal symmetries, the method resolves orbital contributions that are otherwise averaged out by unpolarized tips. This breakthrough establishes impurities as powerful probes for mapping band structures in honeycomb materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state physics, the core concept of reconstructing a full quantum state (density matrix) from limited, symmetry-averaged measurements mirrors challenges in characterizing open quantum systems where only macroscopic observables are accessible.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to reconstruct the density matrix of electron states and the quantum geometric tensor (QGT) from quasiparticle interference (QPI) maps obtained using conventional, unpolarized scanning tunneling microscopy (STM) tips.

**Main result.** The method successfully establishes impurities as tomographic probes for band structures, allowing the reconstruction of the full density matrix and QGT directly from unpolarized LDOS measurements.

**Method.** It utilizes a tomography approach based on exploiting the symmetry properties (point group representations) of scattering states to disentangle orbital contributions within the total measured LDOS modulation.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on two-orbital models defined on a honeycomb lattice, relevant for graphene heterostructures and direct-gap semiconductors. The low-energy physics is described by a $2 	imes 2$ Hamiltonian $\hat{H}(\mathbf{k})$.

**Key observables.** Density matrix ($\hat{\rho}$), orbital populations ($P_\alpha$), quantum coherence ($C_{BA}$), Quantum Metric (Re[QGT]), and Berry Curvature (Im[QGT]).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis is sensitive to the inverse lifetime ($\eta$) which determines the width of Circularly Enriched Crystals (CECs).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that backscattering between time-reversed states maps the density matrix into distinct, symmetry-resolvable orbital contributions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the honeycomb lattice structure, the low-energy spectrum near BZ corners K and K', and show visualizations of the reconstructed QGT components ($\Omega_{xy}$) in various valleys.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem by defining the need for tomography; it details the methodology using symmetry analysis on the $\hat{T}$-matrix approach; it presents numerical results confirming reconstruction accuracy against theoretical models; and concludes by establishing the technique's broad applicability to hexagonal heterostructures.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Characterizing electronic band structures requires precise knowledge of wave functions and their quantum geometry. Here, we introduce a tomography method to reconstruct the density matrix of electron states from quasiparticle interference maps around single impurities. We consider two-orbital models on a honeycomb lattice, relevant to graphene heterostructures and direct-gap semiconductors. For on-site impurities, backscattering between time-reversed states directly maps the density matrix populations and coherences into distinct orbital contributions in the interference map. While local probes usually lack orbital resolution, these orbital contributions transform under distinct symmetry group representations and can thus be disentangled to reveal the density matrix and quantum geometric tensor of the scattering states. This establishes impurities as tomographic probes for band structures in scanning tunneling microscopy using conventional, unpolarized tips.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper develops an exact time-domain formalism for quantum transport in mesoscopic systems by representing fermionic excitations as orthogonal wave packets. This approach rigorously derives Landauer conductance without relying on momentum space, and it extends to analyze complex non-linear devices like RTDs by calculating dynamic admittance from time-dependent packet evolution.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, alternative theoretical lens—the wave packet picture—to understand quantum transport phenomena typically analyzed using steady-state scattering matrices (Landauer/NEGF), offering insights into the underlying time dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish a rigorous, self-contained time-domain construction of Landauer transport that bridges the gap between the continuous energy-domain scattering framework and a discrete, time-resolved picture.

**Main result.** The formalism successfully reproduces the quantized Landauer conductance ($e^2/h$) in the time domain using orthogonal fermionic wave packets, bypassing momentum-space kinematics entirely.

**Method.** It employs second quantization, defining packet creation operators via Fourier transforms and enforcing Pauli exclusion to quantize time ($\Delta t = h/eV$).

**Model / system.** The study models quantum transport in mesoscopic conductors, specifically analyzing structures like the Resonant Tunneling Diode (RTD) using fermionic wave packets (Wannier states).

**Key observables.** Landauer conductance ($G_0$), DC current ($\langle \hat{I}(x, t) angle$), dynamic admittance ($Y(\omega)$), and resonance lifetimes ($	au_i$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T=0$ vs $T>0$), applied bias voltage ($V_{	ext{bias}}$), energy window $[	ext{0}, eV]$, and fundamental time quantization $\Delta t = h/eV$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial derivation assumes the Hamiltonian is elastic, and for finite T, it uses a systematic coarse-graining approximation over disjoint energy windows.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate current convergence from discrete oscillations to the continuous Landauer limit; they also show spatiotemporal evolution of wave packets under parabolic dispersion and the extraction of dynamic admittance components ($G(\omega), B(\omega)$) across resonance features.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the time-domain formalism for $T=0$ transport, then extending it to finite temperature via energy window decomposition, and finally applying this framework to analyze non-linear devices like RTDs, including AC admittance measurements.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum transport in mesoscopic systems is conventionally formulated within the Landauer--Büttiker scattering framework, where steady-state currents emerge from the transmission of plane waves representing propagating carriers. While highly successful, this description obscures the explicit time-domain dynamics of individual fermionic excitations and their role in establishing macroscopic transport. Here, we present an exact and self-contained time-domain construction of Landauer transport based on a discrete basis of orthogonal fermionic wave packets. Starting from a second-quantized formulation, we define packet creation operators via a continuous Fourier transform over a finite transport energy window. By encoding the Pauli exclusion principle, which enforces a fundamental temporal spacing $Δt = h/eV$, the current is reproduced in terms of orthogonal wave packets that are used for the rigorous construction of the many-body fermionic state. In this representation, a noiseless current emerges as a deterministic sequence of charge-carrying events, yielding the Landauer conductance $G_0 = e^2/h$ without invoking momentum-space kinematics. We further demonstrate that this construction remains exact for arbitrary energy dispersion. Additionally, the underlying Fock space decomposition into finite disjoint energy sub-bands renders the numerical approach highly scalable for high performance computing platforms. Our results establish a direct and rigorous bridge between the continuous scattering description of quantum transport and a discrete, time-resolved picture based on fermionic wave packets.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors report discovering an inverse superconducting transition—where superconductivity appears upon heating—in Eu-doped nickelates. They demonstrate this effect persists above the 4He cooling limit using high magnetic fields and current injection. This work suggests that complex magnetic interactions can fundamentally alter the thermal stability of quantum superconducting phases.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work explores non-equilibrium quantum phenomena in strongly correlated materials where thermal fluctuations are not purely decohering but can actively engineer superconducting states, which is highly relevant to understanding exotic pairing mechanisms beyond BCS theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research investigates counterintuitive superconducting transitions, specifically superconductivity induced by an *increase* in temperature (inverse transition), moving beyond previously observed limits.

**Main result.** They report observing field-modulated inverse superconducting transitions above the 4He-cooling limit in Eu-based infinite-layer nickelates, suggesting a mechanism involving temperature-induced magnetic field dominance.

**Method.** The study relies on detailed transport measurements, including resistivity vs. temperature ($ho$-$T$), resistivity vs. magnetic field ($ho$-$H$), and current-voltage ($I$-$V$) characteristics under high fields.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is Eu-doped infinite-layer nickelates (e.g., $	ext{EuxNd}_{1-x}	ext{NiO}_2$). The behavior is analyzed by comparing measured effective magnetic fields to theoretical upper critical fields, deviating from standard Jaccarino-Peter compensation models.

**Key observables.** Zero-resistance state boundaries ($T_{c	ext{-inv}}$ and $T_c$), field dependence ($ho$-$H$ cross-linking), current density triggering of transitions, and reemerged superconductivity at sub-Kelvin temperatures.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic fields up to 35 T; temperature ranges spanning from $\sim 60 	ext{ mK}$ to several Kelvin; doping levels (overdoped/underdoped regimes).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the inverse transition is governed by an alternating dominance of effective magnetic fields related to Eu$^{2+}$ compensations relative to $H_{c2}$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate $ho$-$T$ curves showing the characteristic inverse superconducting dome, high-field phase diagrams mapping the boundaries between normal and superconducting states, and $I$-$V$ characteristics demonstrating current-induced transitions.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically presents evidence for the unusual superconducting phase diagram, first establishing the existence of the inverse transition ($T_{c	ext{-inv}}$), then detailing its field and current dependence, and finally proposing a mechanism based on magnetic field compensation effects.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Thermally driven quantum-orders observed in exceptional instances may redefine the role of thermal-fluctuation from a source of decoherence to a resource for coherent-state engineering. While preliminary signs of counterintuitive temperature-rise-triggered superconductivity manifested in CeCu2Si2, ErRh4B4, Ho1.2Mo6S8 and (La,Ce)Al2, their critical-temperatures (Tc-inv) remain below Kelvin-range, precluding substantial applications. Here, we report field-modulated inverse-superconducting-transitions above 4He-cooling-limit in Eu-based infinite-layer nickelates (EuxNd1-xNiO2 and EuxPr1-xNiO2) grown on a substrate under both overdoped and underdoped regimes. Paradigmatically, superconductivity with zero-resistance is confined between Tc-inv (2.6-5.4 K) and another higher normal-Tc, rising and decreasing with applied magnetic-field, respectively. Starting from the resistive-state below Tc-inv, the inverse-superconducting-transition is driven by not only temperature-rising, but also current-density, while superconductivity further vanishes at higher temperature and current thresholds. The Kelvin-range inverse superconducting transition is plausibly explained by temperature-induced alternating dominance of effective magnetic-fields arising from Eu2+4f7 related compensations relative to the upper-critical-field. Furthermore, an extended-phenomenological-framework is also supported by reemerged superconductivity below 300 mK under magnetic-field, giving rise to an unprecedented temperature-induced reentrant superconductivity. Our findings establish magnetic-interaction-reconfigured high-Tc systems as fertile platforms for exploring quantum phenomena that reverse thermal-decoherence paradigm, also enabling antithetical-designs to unlock untapped application-scenarios for quantum-phase-transition devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that sodium catalysis allows for the room-temperature synthesis of superconducting $	ext{CaC}_6$ by intercalating calcium into graphite. By monitoring magnetic susceptibility and crystal structure over time, the researchers confirmed progressive material formation, suggesting a diffusion-limited mechanism with potential applications in advanced energy storage like Ca-ion batteries.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state chemistry/materials science, the observation of temperature-dependent superconducting transitions ($T_c$ increasing with time) and the underlying mechanism (diffusion control) touches upon non-equilibrium phase transitions relevant to condensed matter physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses the challenge of forming superconducting Calcium Carbide ($	ext{CaC}_6$) via intercalation into graphite at room temperature (RT), a process previously thought to require high temperatures.

**Main result.** Sodium ($	ext{Na}$) catalysis successfully enables the formation of superconducting $	ext{CaC}_6$ at RT, with superconductivity confirmed by diamagnetism and $	ext{XRD}$ peaks that evolve over time.

**Method.** The researchers used a controlled experimental setup involving mixing $	ext{Ca}$, graphite, and $	ext{Na}$ precursors, followed by measurements of magnetic susceptibility (SQUID) and X-ray Diffraction ($	ext{XRD}$) as a function of storage time at RT.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the intercalation reaction: $	ext{Ca} + 	ext{Graphite} (	ext{C}) \xrightarrow{	ext{Na catalyst}} 	ext{CaC}_6$. The process is monitored by tracking the growth of superconducting phases within a solid-state mixture.

**Key observables.** Superconducting diamagnetism (Meissner effect), $	ext{XRD}$ peak intensity for $	ext{CaC}_6$, and the superconducting transition temperature ($T_c$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Storage time ($t$), which showed a square root ($\sqrt{t}$) dependence for superconducting volume fraction indicators.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The process is assumed to be diffusion-controlled, leading to the $\sqrt{t}$ scaling observed in the superconducting signals.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental workflow, temperature dependence of magnetic susceptibility versus storage time, and show how both $	ext{XRD}$ patterns and superconducting indicators scale with $\sqrt{t}$.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a structure detailing sample preparation, performing measurements over varying storage times at RT, analyzing the time evolution of superconductivity via magnetometry and structural changes via XRD.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Calcium (Ca) insertion into graphite (C) has been considered to require elevated temperatures, and its occurrence at room temperature (RT) has been regarded as highly unlikely. Here, we demonstrate that sodium (Na) catalysis enables the formation of superconducting CaC$_6$ even at RT. In mixtures of Ca, Na, and graphite, the gradual development of superconducting diamagnetism and the emergence of X-ray diffraction peaks confirm the formation of CaC$_6$ during storage at RT. The superconducting transition temperature increases with storage time, and the amount of CaC$_6$ scales proportionally to the square root of storage time. These findings provide new insights into the mechanism of superconductivity and Na-catalyzed formation of CaC$_6$, and highlight the potential of this RT intercalation process for practical applications such as electrode materials in Ca-ion batteries.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses polarized small-angle neutron scattering (SANS) to experimentally map the magnetic microstructure of iron oxide nanoflowers. By analyzing the spin-flip scattering intensity at low applied fields, the researchers provide strong evidence for the formation of vortex-type magnetization states within these assemblies. This methodology demonstrates SANS's power in characterizing complex, non-uniform magnetic correlations in densely packed nanomaterials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on magnetism, the use of scattering techniques to probe mesoscopic, non-uniform magnetic order parameters (like vortices) provides an analogy for studying complex, spatially varying order parameters in other condensed matter systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates vortex-type spin correlations within iron oxide multicore assemblies.

**Main result.** Experimental measurements of the spin-flip SANS cross section reveal an isotropic ring structure at low fields, which is quantitatively consistent with theoretical predictions for flux-closure states stabilized by magnetostatic and exchange energies.

**Method.** Polarized small-angle neutron scattering (SANS) was employed to probe the magnetic microstructure of the assemblies.

**Model / system.** The physical system consists of iron oxide nanoflowers, modeled using a linear vortex magnetization Ansatz. The analysis compares measured spin-flip cross sections against theoretical models incorporating particle interactions via structure factors.

**Key observables.** Spin-flip SANS cross section (dSigma_sf/dOmega) and its field evolution at intermediate momentum transfers (q).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low applied magnetic fields are crucial for observing vortex formation; the characteristic isotropic ring feature is key.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses an effective model incorporating the Percus-Yevick structure factor to account for dense packing and size distribution, building upon initial dilute ensemble approximations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows TEM images of nanoflowers and magnetization hysteresis. Figure 2 compares experimental spin-flip scattering at different fields (1.0 T vs 0.01 T) with the linear vortex model prediction.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical system and theoretical framework for vortex modeling to presenting quantitative comparisons between measured SANS data and calculated cross sections, culminating in evidence supporting the vortex state formation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report an experimental investigation of the magnetic microstructure of iron oxide multicore assemblies by means of polarized small-angle neutron scattering (SANS). Guided by a recently developed analytical theory for vortex-state magnetic nanoparticles, we provide a quantitative comparison between the measured and calculated cross sections, revealing signatures that are consistent with vortex-type magnetization configurations at low applied magnetic fields. In particular, the field evolution and the characteristic isotropic ring-type feature of the spin-flip scattering intensity at intermediate momentum transfers are in line with the formation of flux-closure states. The latter are stabilized by the interplay of exchange, Zeeman, and magnetostatic energies. The methodology allows for a statistically significant characterization of vortex states in densely packed nanoparticle systems, thereby complementing surface-sensitive techniques that are commonly limited to the observation of spin structures in individual particles.

</details>

**Authors:** Haoran Chen, Zhipeng Xu, Chunjian Li, Lei Hou, Dechao Yu, Xiaobin Xie, Yue Liu, Bohua Dong, Lixin Cao, Chenghui Xia  
**Type:** experiment · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.18939v1>

**Summary.** This research introduces a novel 'thermodynamic–kinetic decoupling' method to stabilize excitonic emission in defect-prone Cu-based quantum dots. By alloying with Zn and Ga, the researchers suppress native defects thermodynamically while kinetically locking the structure against degradation. This results in high-purity, stable emitters that significantly boost the efficiency of luminescent solar concentrators.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a clear, actionable framework—the 'strain-and-pin' strategy—for controlling intrinsic electronic properties in complex semiconductor materials by manipulating both their equilibrium thermodynamics and kinetic barriers.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is achieving stable, narrow-band excitonic emission in defect-tolerant, heavy-metal-free quantum dots (QDs) that are prone to broad, defect-related photoluminescence.

**Main result.** By implementing a thermodynamic–kinetic decoupling strategy using Zn and Ga alloying, the researchers created highly stable QDs exhibiting near-unity quantum yield and narrow excitonic emission suitable for high-efficiency devices like LSCs.

**Method.** The study combines advanced synthesis techniques (cation exchange, shell growth) with rigorous characterization methods including single-dot spectroscopy, time-resolved PL, and device testing.

**Model / system.** The physical system is colloidal CuInS2-based quantum dots ($	ext{CIZGS/ZnS}$), which are engineered by alloying ($	ext{Zn}^{2+}$) to thermodynamically suppress native defects and subsequently stabilizing the structure using $	ext{Ga}^{3+}$ incorporation to kinetically pin cation migration.

**Key observables.** PL linewidth (narrowing from $>300 	ext{ meV}$ to $\sim 58-120 	ext{ meV}$), Quantum Yield ($\approx 98\%$), Single-photon purity ($g_2(0) = 0.06$), and External Optical Efficiency ($\eta_{opt} = 12.68\%$ in LSCs).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Lattice strain induced by $	ext{Zn}^{2+}$ alloying; the energetic penalty for cation displacement (kinetic barrier); operating temperature ($T=300 	ext{ K}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The observed spectral narrowing and stability are directly attributable to the engineered thermodynamic suppression of defects combined with kinetic stabilization against defect regeneration during processing.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate spectral evolution across different QD compositions ($	ext{CIS} 	o 	ext{CIZS} 	o 	ext{CIZGS/ZnS}$), structural changes via STEM/XRD, and device performance metrics like $J–V$ curves for LSCs.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a logical progression: identifying the defect-limited emission problem $ightarrow$ applying thermodynamic control ($	ext{Zn}^{2+}$ alloying) to narrow the band $ightarrow$ addressing kinetic instability ($	ext{Ga}^{3+}$ incorporation) $ightarrow$ demonstrating final stability and high performance in optoelectronic devices (LSCs).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Colloidal quantum dots that simultaneously offer room-temperature single-photon purity and high photoluminescence quantum yield are sought for quantum optics, but remain elusive in environmentally benign materials. We introduce a thermodynamic-kinetic decoupling strategy that transforms defect-tolerant CuInS2 quantum dots into bright, narrowband, and photostable single-photon emitters. Zn2+ alloying strains the lattice, thermodynamically suppressing native copper vacancies and narrowing the emission from a broad defect band of approximately 300 meV to an excitonic line of approximately 120 meV. Ga3+ incorporation then kinetically pins the cation sublattice against Cu+ migration, preventing defect regeneration during ZnS shell growth. The resulting Cd-free core/shell dots achieve near-unity quantum yield of approximately 98% while retaining narrow excitonic emission. Critically, room-temperature single-dot spectroscopy reveals homogeneous linewidths as low as approximately 58 meV, strongly suppressed blinking, and high-purity single-photon emission with g2(0) = 0.06. This stabilized excitonic emission directly reduces reabsorption losses in luminescent solar concentrators, yielding an external optical efficiency of 12.68%. Our work establishes a generalizable framework to unlock intrinsic excitonic photophysics in ion-mobile, defect-prone semiconductors, opening a viable path toward high-performance heavy-metal-free emitters for quantum light sources.

</details>

**Summary.** This work tackles the computational bottleneck in calculating electronic transport properties by proposing and comparing two efficient alternatives to standard DFT methods: a generalized model approach and a machine learning interpolation technique. By applying these methods to MoS2, the authors demonstrate that ML offers high accuracy while being significantly faster, providing a powerful tool for predicting thermoelectric performance in novel 2D semiconductors.

**Why it may be interesting.** The integration of machine learning directly into solid-state physics calculations for complex many-body interactions like electron-phonon coupling represents a powerful paradigm shift in materials discovery, moving beyond purely deterministic, computationally prohibitive first-principles methods.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Computing electron-phonon coupling and thermoelectric transport properties from first principles is computationally expensive, necessitating efficient predictive methods.

**Main result.** The machine learning approach was shown to be more accurate and straightforward to implement than model approaches for predicting these complex material parameters in 2D semiconductors like MoS2.

**Method.** The study compares three methods: rigorous DFPT+Wannier interpolation, a generalized acoustic deformation potential model, and a novel Machine Learning regression technique (using GPR/PFR) trained on limited first-principles data.

**Model / system.** The focus is on monolayer MoS2, where electronic transport near the K/K' valleys is governed by electron-phonon coupling ($	ext{el-ph}$), which dictates thermoelectric coefficients. The underlying physics involves modeling phonon modes (acoustic and optical) and their interaction with electrons.

**Key observables.** Electrical conductivity ($\sigma$), Seebeck coefficient ($S$), Power Factor ($	ext{PF}$), electron mobility, and $	ext{el-ph}$ matrix elements.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature dependence, carrier concentration regimes (e.g., $10^{12}–10^{13} 	ext{ cm}^{-2}$), and the specific phonon modes ($	ext{LA}, 	ext{TA}, 	ext{LO}_2$, etc.).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The momentum relaxation time approximation is used for transport calculations, and discrepancies with experiment are often attributed to extrinsic disorder effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate energy dispersion, phonon dispersion, comparisons of calculated vs. state-of-the-art $	ext{el-ph}$ matrix elements across different methods (ML vs Model vs DFPT), and temperature/concentration dependence of mobility and thermoelectric properties.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the computational bottleneck, then detailing two alternative methodologies (Model approach and ML interpolation) for calculating coupling parameters, validating these against established benchmarks, and finally applying the best method to predict key transport coefficients ($\sigma$, $S$, $	ext{PF}$) across various doping regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The ability to compute electron-phonon coupling from first principles, using density functional perturbation theory and interpolation techniques, has enabled predictive calculations of electronic transport coefficients in crystalline materials. However, these methods are still computationally expensive. Here we present two inexpensive methods to obtain thermoelectric transport properties of semiconductors using a small number of electron-phonon matrix elements calculated from first principles. The first method combines models for coupling of electrons with different phonon modes whose parameters are obtained from $\sim 10$ matrix elements per electronic band and phonon mode calculated from first principles. Within this method, we formulate the acoustic deformation potential model for arbitrary crystal symmetries and band extrema locations. The second method uses machine learning to interpolate $\sim 100$ electron-phonon matrix elements per electronic band and phonon mode on dense reciprocal space grids in the parts of the Brillouin zone relevant for transport. We apply both methods to two-dimensional MoS$_2$ and show very good agreement with the state-of-the-art method. The calculated thermoelectric properties also agree well with experiments. We find that the machine-learning method is more accurate and straightforward to implement compared to the model approach.

</details>

**Authors:** Didier Felbacq, Emmanuel Rousseau  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.19141v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Frenkel-Kontorova` **1/5**

**Summary.** This work establishes a geometric interpretation for the topological invariant of Bloch bands in inversion-symmetric periodic media. It proves that the physical pole-zero pattern observed at band edges is mathematically equivalent to the first Stiefel–Whitney class of an associated Real line bundle. This unifies several advanced concepts—Berry-Zak phases, local systems, and characteristic classes—into a single coherent framework.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a deep mathematical framework connecting physical band topology (Berry-Zak phase) to fundamental concepts in algebraic topology ($w_1$ class), which is valuable for understanding topological phases in general condensed matter systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To provide a rigorous geometric interpretation of the topological classification of Bloch bands in one-dimensional periodic media possessing inversion symmetry.

**Main result.** The pole-zero pattern invariant ($\eta_{PZ}$) associated with these bands is shown to be geometrically equivalent to the first Stiefel–Whitney class ($w_1$) of an associated Real eigenline bundle.

**Method.** The analysis uses concepts from differential geometry, specifically relating monodromy representations over the Brillouin circle ($S^1$) to characteristic classes via the action of inversion symmetry on Cauchy data in $\mathbb{CP}^1$.

**Model / system.** The study concerns one-dimensional periodic media characterized by an inversion-symmetric potential $V(x)$, analyzed using Bloch wave theory and the associated Helmholtz/Schrödinger equation.

**Key observables.** Pole-zero pattern (ZP vs PZ), monodromy sign ($ho = s_0 s_\pi$), first Stiefel–Whitney class ($w_1 \in H^1(S^1; \mathbb{Z}_2)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inversion eigenvalues at band edges ($s_0, s_\pi \in \{\pm 1\}$), the quasi-momentum $q$ (parameterized by $S^1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The system is assumed to be one-dimensional, periodic, and possess inversion symmetry; the complex Bloch bundle itself is topologically trivial.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the trajectory of Cauchy data on $\mathbb{CP}^1$ showing fixed points corresponding to Dirichlet/Neumann conditions, and plot band structures alongside transmission spectra indicating edge modes.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from characterizing the pole-zero pattern via impedance functions to establishing a geometric link by analyzing how inversion symmetry dictates the monodromy of the associated Real eigenline bundle over $S^1$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In a previous work, the topology of inversion-symmetric one-dimensional periodic media was characterized through the pole-zero pattern of an impedance-like function associated with Bloch waves. This construction reproduces the Berry--Zak invariant and provides a criterion for topological interface states.   In the present work, we give a geometric interpretation of this formalism. We show that poles and zeros arise naturally from the action of inversion symmetry on the projectivized space of Cauchy data. The corresponding Dirichlet and Neumann states are identified with the two fixed points of the induced $\mathbb Z_2$ action on the Riemann sphere.   The key observation is that Bloch eigenvectors are naturally constructed on the universal covering of the Brillouin circle. The topology of the associated Real eigenline bundle is encoded in the action of the deck transformation group on lifted eigenvectors. This action is described by a monodromy sign $ρ\in\{\pm1\}$, determined by the inversion representations carried by the band at the fixed points of the Brillouin zone.   We show that this monodromy defines a natural rank-one local system over the Brillouin circle. The corresponding Real line bundle is classified by its first Stiefel--Whitney class, which coincides with the associated $\mathbb Z_2$ pole-zero invariant. This establishes a geometric connection between the pole-zero formalism, Berry--Zak phases, Real bundles and local coefficient systems.

</details>
